<meta>This page introduces the editorial history and manuscript sources for the first printed edition of the Āryabhaṭīya, a foundational Indian astronomical text.</meta>

# PREFACE.

---

This first edition of the <term>Āryabhaṭīya</term> <note>A seminal 5th-century Sanskrit astronomical treatise by Āryabhaṭa</note> along with the commentary of <term>Paramādīçvara</term> <note>A 15th-century Indian astronomer and commentator</note> is mainly based upon two manuscripts in <term>Malayalim</term> <note>Malayalam, the language and script of Kerala, South India</note> character, one of them belonging to Dr. <term>A. C. Burnell</term>, the other once in possession of <term>Whish</term> and now forming part of the library of the <term>Royal Asiatic Society</term>, London. The latter manuscript dates from A. D. 1820; the former from A. D. 1863, for at the end of it I find the following note in Dr. <term>Burnell</term>’s handwriting: „Copied fr. an <term>Ola</term> <note>A traditional palm-leaf manuscript</note> M.S. in the <term>Chirakkil Râjâ</term> library. — Written by <term>Unni Panikar</term> at <term>Calicut</term> 1863.”

Both manuscripts are derived from one source, either immediately, or remotely, which is clearly proved by the fact that they show the same gaps, indicated as such, after stanza 44 of the 4th Section, and at the end of the comment on stanza 48. Apart from a very few trifling clerical errors both contain exactly the same text.

Besides these manuscripts I have consulted one other containing <term>Āryabhata</term>’s text without commentary, from the library of the <term>Roy. As. Soc.</term> (Nº. 66 of the <term>Whish collection</term>). It proved of little use, except in one important case. It so happens that the gap above mentioned extends over the 45th stanza of the 4th section, and that the reading of this very stanza in another commentary, the <term>Prakâçikâ</term>, is materially different from the reading in the manuscript without commentary. Instead of <note>original: "मध्याङ्गत्वक्रमगु- णितो"</note> "multiplied by the sine of the mean longitude," the <term>Prakâçikâ</term> has <note>original: "मध्याङ्कोत्क्रमगुणितो"</note> "multiplied by the versed sine of the mean longitude." The latter reading involves a gross error, as <term>Bhâskarâcârya</term> <note>A famous 12th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> has pointed out in his <term>Golâdhyâya</term> <note>Chapter on the Celestial Sphere</note>, <term>Grahaṇavâsanâ</term> <note>Explanation of Eclipses</note>, stanza 48—57, cf. <term>Dṛkkarma-vâsanâ</term> <note>Explanation of the visibility correction</note>, stanza 16, and the same author’s <term>Gaṇita</term> <note>Mathematics/Astronomy treatise</note>, <term>Candragrahaṇâdhikâra</term> <note>Chapter on Lunar Eclipses</note>, comment on stanza 20. From the remainder of <term>Paramâdīçvara</term>’s comment

<summary>The editor describes the primary Malayalam manuscripts used for this edition of the Āryabhaṭīya, noting their shared origins and specific textual gaps. He highlights a significant philological correction regarding a mathematical error found in alternative commentaries.</summary>

<keywords>Āryabhaṭīya, Paramādīçvara, manuscripts, Malayalam script, A.C. Burnell, Royal Asiatic Society, Bhâskarâcârya, Indian astronomy, philology</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's preface regarding the textual history and commentaries of the Aryabhatiya, specifically discussing the lineage of astronomical errors and the quality of various manuscript sources.</meta>

# PREFACE.

Regarding the stanza in question, it is indeed clear enough that he <note>The commentator Sûryadeva</note> read *madhyārdharātrakrama°* <note>original: "मध्यार्द्धरात्रक्रम°"; a technical term referring to the "sequence of midnight" in astronomical calculations</note>; but in such a matter it is not wholly superfluous to appeal to the direct evidence of manuscripts.

Though the reading followed by the author of the *Prakāśikā* <note>The "Illumination," a commentary by Sûryadeva</note> is erroneous, both from an astronomical and grammatical point of view, it cannot be denied that the error was one of long standing. For we know by Bhāskara's <note>Bhāskara II, a famous 12th-century Indian mathematician</note> severe criticisms that Lalla, the author of the *Dhīvṛddhida-Tantra* <note>A treatise for "increasing the intellect"</note>, had fallen into the same error. Now Lalla is more than once called a disciple of Āryabhaṭa, and whether this statement be rigidly correct or not, he certainly was a contemporaneous writer, for he uses the same epoch with the latter, as may be seen on page 58 of this edition.

It is by no means strange that Paramādīśvara, who was well read in the works of Bhāskara, disregarded the authority of Lalla and the author of the *Prakāśikā*. It is more strange that a gap occurs in his work exactly at the critical passage. I cannot suppress the suspicion that the defect is not owing to accident, but that it is an intentional erasure by some reader who clung to the opinion of the *Prakāśikā*.

Paramādīśvara's *Bhaṭadīpikā* <note>The "Lamp of Bhaṭa"</note> is, generally speaking, a useful commentary. I for one must confess that but for its aid the meaning of many rules in the text would have remained obscure to me. At the same time it must be granted that some of his explanations, for example at stanza 9 of the 4th section, cannot be admitted to be correct.

I have not been able to find out the time of Paramādīśvara, but this much is certain: that he is posterior to Sûryadeva-Yajvan, the author of the *Bhaṭaprakāśa* or *Prakāśikā*, whom he frequently quotes. The *Prakāśa* is here and there more prolix, but contains after all no more information than the more recent *Dīpikā*. Copies of it are far from scarce, at least in Southern India. One very bad copy makes part of the Mackenzie collection \*) ; an excellent one has been kindly lent to me by Dr. Burnell. Both manuscripts are in the Grantham character <note>A South Indian script used for writing Sanskrit</note>. In the library of Trinity College, Cambridge, there is an abridged copy of it in Nāgarî †) ; see Professor Aufrecht's Catalogue, page 37.

---

\*) In the old catalogue the „*Prakāśa* composed by Sûryadeva” has been transformed into a „Commentary on the *Sûryasiddhānta*;” it has long been lying hidden under this disguise, until Dr. Rost discovered the mistake.

†) Not Devanāgarî, a term unheard of in Hindustân. It is, in fact, no word at all, but a grammatical blunder; see Pāṇini VII, 3, 24 <note>Referring to the ancient Sanskrit grammarian's rules on word formation</note>. By supposing that <term>devanāgarî</term> <note>Literally "Nāgarî of the gods"; the editor argues the prefix "Deva" is a modern or incorrect addition to the name of the script</note> may mean „Nāgarî of the gods” we may save grammar, but at the price of common sense.

<summary>The editor discusses the historical transmission of astronomical errors through various commentators and describes the manuscript sources used for this edition, including works by Lalla and Paramādīśvara.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, Sanskrit manuscripts, Bhāskara, Lalla, Paramādīśvara, Sûryadeva-Yajvan, Nāgarî script, Grantham script, textual criticism</keywords>

<page-num>VII</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

For more than one reason I think it will be worth while here to insert the introduction of the *Prakâçikâ* <note>The "Illuminator," a famous commentary on the astronomical work of Aryabhata</note>, 1º. because it contains some peculiar views and statements not wholly devoid of interest; 2º. to show that the Cambridge manuscript is but a clumsy abridgment of it; and, finally, to give a specimen of the orthography common to most South-indian manuscripts. The introduction then runs thus, according to BURNELL's copy:

->Hari! <note>A name for Vishnu, used here as an auspicious invocation</note> Salutations to the holy Lord of Hosts! <note>original: "Śrīganapataye namaḥ"; Ganesha, the remover of obstacles</note> May there be no obstacles!<-

->I bow to the Supreme Soul, who by nature knows all things,<-
->The first teacher of the sciences, and the efficient cause of the worlds.<-
->Salutations to the abode where all auspicious qualities reside,<-
->To the faultless, the eternal, the great, and the most exalted.<-
->By the sacrificer Suryadeva, who rightly knows the meaning of the three pillars of astronomy,<-
->The meaning of the aphorisms spoken by Aryabhata is here revealed in abridged form.<-

"One's own portion of the Veda should be studied" *) <note>A traditional Vedic injunction</note>. Since the rule for study extends to the understanding of the meaning, and since the study of the auxiliary sciences <term>Vedānga: the six 'limbs' or disciplines necessary to understand the Vedas</term> is ordained as the means to that understanding, it is therefore established that "The Veda with its six auxiliary limbs must be studied by a Brahmin without any selfish motive." Because of this concluding rule, one should also study all the auxiliary limbs in their entirety. Because of the rule regarding the timing of the study of these limbs:

->Prosody is said to be the feet of the Veda, and Ritual is called the hands;<-
->Grammar is declared the mouth, and Astronomy is called the eyes;<-
->Phonetics is the nose of the Veda, and Etymology is called the ears.<-

According to this traditional remembrance of the specific limbs, if one does not study a particular limb, the "Veda-Person" <note>The Veda personified as a human body</note> suffers a deficiency in that limb. Therefore, among the six limbs which must be accepted for study by the three twice-born castes <note>The social classes of priests, warriors, and merchants</note> just like the Veda itself—

---

*) *The Mss. write ddhy, gghy instead of dhy, ghy.* <note>A note on South Indian scribal habits of doubling consonants after a semi-vowel</note>

<summary>This page presents the opening of Suryadeva’s commentary on Aryabhata, justifying the study of astronomy as a vital "limb" of the Vedas. It uses the metaphor of the Veda-Person to show that without astronomy (the eyes), the sacred knowledge is incomplete.</summary>
<keywords>Suryadeva, Prakasika, Aryabhata, Vedanga, Astronomy, Jyotisha, Vedic study, South Indian manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page serves as the opening preface to a treatise on Jyotisha (astronomy/astrology), establishing its divine lineage and its status as the most vital of the Vedic sciences.</meta>

VIII

# PREFACE.

->The face is the most important part of the body, or rather, the face is said to be everything.<-

->Even there, the nose is superior; and even there, the eyes are the most superior.<-

According to this maxim, one must certainly study the primary limb of the "Veda-Purusha" <note>The personification of the Vedas as a human body</note>, which is the eyes <note>original: "Chakshus"; referring to the science of Jyotisha</note>. Bearing this in mind, the Lord Brahma first created the extensive science of Jyotisha <term>Jyotishashastra: the traditional Indian system of astronomy and astrology</term>. Having studied that science from Brahma, the elder Garga <note>Vriddha Garga, a legendary sage</note> summarized it and composed another work <gloss>r. composed</gloss>. From him, sages such as Parashara and others obtained that knowledge and composed other astronomical treatises. Thus, the elder Garga says:

> This eye of the twice-born <term>Dwija: "twice-born," referring to the upper three castes of Vedic society</term> was created by the Self-Existent One <note>Brahma</note> himself. This Jyotisha is a part of the Veda <term>Vedanga: one of the six auxiliary disciplines of Vedic study</term>, equal to the Brahma, and emerged along with the Vedas.
> I obtained from the Self-Existent One this best of the Vedic sciences, which determines the proper time for rituals and brings welfare to the three worlds.
> From me, it was obtained by other sages through an abundant tradition; and by them, through their divine vision, it was again explained in various books.

And so says Garga:

> Let this be heard—it leads to heaven, longevity, dharma, and merit; it brings fame, is rich in knowledge and wisdom, and is the supreme purifier of the twice-born.
> This sacred knowledge of Time is the first and highest science. In ancient times, wishing to create the Vedas, the Self-Existent One created this.
> It is the first limb of the Vedas and the accomplisher of Vedic rituals. Therefore, the wise know that the knowledge of light <note>Jyotisha</note> must be understood by the leaders of the twice-born.
> Through the science of Jyotisha, the auspicious and inauspicious is told for the whole world. He who knows the knowledge of light, knows the supreme limb.

<summary>This preface establishes Jyotisha (astronomy/astrology) as the "eye" of the Vedas, tracing its transmission from the creator Brahma through the sage Garga to other ancient seers. It emphasizes that the science is essential for determining the correct timing of religious rituals and understanding the destiny of the world.</summary>
<keywords>Jyotishashastra, Veda, Brahma, Vriddha Garga, Parashara, Vedanga, Dwija, astronomy, astrology, Vedic ritual, sacred time</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal preface to a Sanskrit astronomical or astrological treatise, establishing the divine and practical necessity of Jyotisha (astral science) within the Vedic tradition.</meta>

<page-num>IX</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

The gods recognize as a true Brahmin one who is united with the essence of that Supreme Being. Therefore, let the best of the twice-born <note>The "twice-born" (dvija) refers to members of the upper three social classes who have undergone spiritual initiation.</note> first study the knowledge of the lights <note>original: "jyotir-jñānam" — the science of stars and planets.</note>. After the scriptures of law <note>original: "dharma-shastra"</note> come the rituals and the methods for performing sacrifices. Therefore, this eternal "Eye of Sacrifice" is equal in merit to the Vedas themselves. It is to be studied by Brahmins of firm vows who seek the imperishable heavenly realm.

And so says the Teacher Lagadha <note>Lagadha is the traditionally cited author of the Vedanga Jyotisha, the earliest known Indian text on astronomy.</note>:

> As the crests of peacocks and the jewels of serpents stay at the very top, so does Astrology/Astronomy <note>original: "Jyotisha"</note> stand at the head of all the sciences belonging to the Vedas.

The Vedas are indeed revealed for the purpose of sacrifice, and sacrifices are ordained according to the sequence of time. Therefore, he who understands this science of time-keeping—this Jyotisha—understands the sacrifice itself.

Thus it is said. And this science consists of three branches <note>original: "skandha"</note> known as Computation, Natal Astrology, and the Branches. As the Elder Garga <note>An ancient sage and authority on astral omens.</note> says:

> That eminent Brahmin who knows Computation, Natal Astrology, and the Branches—he is designated as a "Knower of the Three Branches" and has mastered the entirety of the Compendium <note>original: "Samhita"</note>.

In this regard:
1. The **Branch of Computation** <term>Ganita-skandha: the mathematical part of astronomy</term> is the portion of the astral science that explains the calculation of time, the movement of the planets, the nature of the spheres, the rotation of the celestial circle, and the situation of the earth.
2. The **Branch of Natal Astrology** <term>Jataka-skandha: horoscopic or judicial astrology</term> is the portion known by terms such as "Hour-Science," "Nativity," and "Selection of Auspicious Moments." It explains the good or evil results of births and the performance of rituals based on the rising sign <note>original: "lagna"</note> at the time of birth or the time of action.
3. The **Branch of the Compendium** <term>Shakha-skandha: also called Samhita, dealing with omens and mundane astrology</term> is the portion known by terms such as "The Branch" or "The Collection." It explains the good or evil fortune of the world based on the transit of planets, portents, and natural phenomena.

Thus, it is established that this infinite astral science—scattered in many ways through these three branches and their various internal divisions—is the primary limb of the "Veda-Person" <note>The Vedas are often personified as a human body; Jyotisha is considered the eyes.</note> and must necessarily be studied.

Now, within this "Boar Aeon" <term>Varaha-kalpa: the current cosmic cycle in Hindu cosmology</term>, in the seventh Manvantara <note>A vast period of time ruled by a specific Manu or progenitor.</note>, in the twenty-eighth cycle of four ages, at the beginning of the Kali Age... <note>The text breaks off here as it begins to establish the specific chronological starting point for its astronomical calculations.</note>

<summary>This preface establishes Jyotisha (astronomy/astrology) as the "Eye of the Veda," essential for timing sacrifices and understanding karma. It defines the three-fold division of the science into mathematics, horoscopy, and omens.</summary>
<keywords>Jyotisha, Lagadha, Garga, Ganita, Jataka, Samhita, Vedanga, Vedic sacrifice, Indian astronomy, Three Branches</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page introduces the historical context of the astronomer Aryabhata, contrasting a traditional mythological explanation of his knowledge with a modern critical perspective.</meta>

# PREFACE.

When twenty-three years of the Solar era had passed <note>original: "gamite saurabde gate trayovimshativarshe"</note>, the teacher Aryabhata <note>A famous 5th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> observed that the ancient sciences explaining time-reckoning, the celestial sphere, and worldly mathematics had become somewhat distorted. This was due to the loss of tradition over the long passage of time, resulting in a discrepancy between observed reality and mathematical calculation (1). Desiring to create a system of astronomy that perfectly matched observation, he worshipped the Lord Brahma <note>original: "Svayambhuvam," the self-existent creator</note>—the original source of the zodiac and the planets—with pure penances to obtain the seed of such astronomical knowledge. Then, the pleased Lord instructed him in that supersensible, deeply secret seed of knowledge regarding time and the sphere. Subsequently, Aryabhata revealed this to the world, summarizing the essential seeds taught by the Lord in ten *Giti* stanzas, along with one *Arya* stanza containing the seeds of worldly mathematics derived through his own intellect. Then, in one hundred and eight *Arya* stanzas, he demonstrated the application of the seeds of mathematics, time-reckoning, and the sphere in a general manner. This astronomical science, consisting of two compositions emerging from the lotus-mouth of the teacher Aryabhata, is what we intend to explain.

Suryadeva <note>A later commentator on Aryabhata's work</note> must have lived a considerable time after the great Bhaskara, in an age when the living breath of science had already parted from India. In the foregoing extract it will have been observed that he had not the faintest notion how Aryabhata could have got at his elements of astronomy by mere observation and calculation. The acquir—

---

*) This statement coincides with another found in a stanza from an unknown author:

-> Even though the five *Siddhantas* <note>The five traditional astronomical treatises of India</note> were established, there was a contradiction with observation <-
-> regarding the calculation of the movements of the planets and eclipses. <-
-> In the Kali age, the Sun himself became incarnate in the city of Kusumpura <-
-> as the head of the university named Aryabhata, the knower of the geography of the earth. <-

It is not true that Aryabhata could have corrected the *Romaka-Siddhanta* <note>A treatise influenced by Greek/Roman astronomy</note>, for the epoch of this work is posterior to Aryabhata's (see Dr. Bhau Daji, in Journal of the Royal Asiatic Society of 1865, p. 407). Yet, in a general sense, it may be affirmed that our author has been the reformer of Indian astronomy.

<summary>This page presents a traditional Sanskrit account of Aryabhata receiving astronomical knowledge through divine revelation, contrasted with a 19th-century scholarly critique of his historical role and the decline of Indian science.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhata, Suryadeva, Brahma, Indian astronomy, Siddhanta, Kusumpura, Romaka-Siddhanta, Bhau Daji, Kali Yuga</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the editor's preface regarding the manuscript sources and commentaries of Aryabhata's astronomical work.</meta>

<page-num>XI</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

The origin of those elements is ascribed to supernatural agency, quite in the fashion in which Panini <note>The famous Sanskrit grammarian, c. 4th century BCE.</note> in the story is told to have received the *Shiva-Sutras* <note>original: "Çivasûtrâni"; the foundational sounds of Sanskrit grammar said to be revealed by the god Shiva.</note>.

There is one manuscript of the *Prakasha* <note>The "Light" or "Illumination," referring here to a commentary.</note> belonging to the library of the Royal Asiatic Society, London, which is interspersed with the annotations of a certain Yellayen, or as the name sounds in Sanskritized form: Yallaya, son to Shridharacharya and pupil to Suryadeva. So we find in the second chapter (p. 12 of the MS.) the words: <insert>“Now the explanation of the section on the Reckoning of Time <note>original: "Kâlakriyâpâdasya"</note> made by Suryadeva Yajvan is complete; I, the learned one named Yallaya, son of Shri Baladitya and pupil of Suryacharya, having produced <note>original: "utpâdya"</note> something, have made this explanation.”</insert>

Again at the end of the whole work we read:

> <insert>“Thus the *Aryabhata-Prakasha* composed by Shri Suryadeva Yajvan is finished. Thus the learned one named Yallaya, who obtained the power of speech by the grace of Shri Chandrashekhara <note>A name for the god Shiva.</note>, son of Shri Baladitya and pupil of Suryacharya, has made a brief explanation of the meaning of the section on the Celestial Sphere <note>original: "golapâdasya"</note> proclaimed by Aryabhata.”</insert>

From a casual expression in the same manuscript (p. 48) I gather that once there existed several commentaries <margin>The commentary by Bhutavishnu must have been one of them. See Prof. Whitney, Journal of the American Oriental Society VI. 561.</margin> on the works of Aryabhata. The passage runs thus:

> <insert>“This also has been told by us only as a mere direction; it should be seen at length in the Great Commentaries <note>original: "Bhâshyâdishu"</note> and others.”</insert>

Whether the works alluded to are still extant is a question that waits for an answer from learned Hindus or Europeans having access to unexplored Indian libraries. In the mean while this edition of the *Aryashtashatam* <note>The "Eight Hundred Verses of Aryabhata."</note> will, it is hoped, be conducive to a more accurate knowledge of the ancient astronomer’s system, and stimulate the interest of Indian and European scholars. It will be understood that with the scanty, however valuable, materials at my disposal, I could not attempt to constitute the text such as the author published it. What I could give, is the text Paramadishvara <note>A 15th-century Indian astronomer and commentator.</note> had before him.

In spelling and punctuation <note>original: "interpunction"</note> I have kept close to the manuscripts, with this exception that the doubling of consonants after the *r* has been done away with, and the dental *l* substituted for the lingual one, corresponding with the Nagari <term>ल</term> <note>The standard 'l' sound in the Devanagari script.</note>. Here and there I have added words of my own to fill up ugly gaps, e. g. p. 97, l. 15, sq.

---

<note>original: "utpâdya"</note> The word *produced* is here unintelligible, at least to me. One would expect *expanded* <note>original: "vistîrya"</note> or some synonymous term.

<summary>The editor describes a specific manuscript of the Prakasha containing annotations by the scholar Yallaya and discusses the evidence for lost historical commentaries on Aryabhata's astronomical system.</summary>

<page-num>XII</page-num>

Such passages are marked by ( ). Another form of brackets <note>likely square brackets [ ]</note> indicates that the included words are spurious <note>meaning they are likely later additions not original to the primary text</note>.

<meta>This is the opening page of the commentary on the Aryabhatiya, a foundational Indian astronomical text. The translator establishes the invocation and the author's lineage.</meta>

<page-num>1</page-num>

# May there be no obstacles <note>original: "Avighnamastu" - a traditional invocation to Ganesha or the divine to ensure the successful completion of a task.</note>

---

->We worship that Light which impels the intellect of all, adorned by the moon,<-
->Who holds the deer, the axe, and the gestures of protection and boon-giving, and possesses three eyes.<-
->The *Lilavati*, the works of Bhaskara, the *Laghu* and the other *Manasa*—<-
->All these were explained for the understanding of students, both ancient and modern.<-
->Now, a commentary on this *Aryabhatiya* is being composed by me,<-
->By the one named Parameshvara, titled the *Light on Aryabhata* <note>original: "Bhatadipika"</note>.<-

In this context, the teacher Aryabhata, having performed a salutation to his desired deity for the removal of obstacles, sets forth the subject matter of the work in the form of an *Arya* verse:

> Having bowed to Brahman <note>original: "Kam"</note>, who is one and many, the true deity, the Supreme Spirit,
> Aryabhata speaks of the three: Mathematics, Time-reckoning, and the Celestial Sphere.

Thus: "To Brahman" <note>original: "Kam"</note> means to the creator god. "One" refers to his nature as the single cause; "Many" refers to his manifestation as the manifold effects. "The true deity" implies that God alone is the ultimate reality. The Self-born one <note>original: "Svayambhu"</note> is the only absolute deity; others were created by him and are therefore not absolute—

<vocab>May there be no obstacles, Aryabhata, Light on Aryabhata, Lilavati, Works of Bhaskara, Mathematics, Time-reckoning, Celestial Sphere, Parameshvara</vocab>

<summary>This page serves as the formal opening of Parameshvara’s commentary on the Aryabhatiya, beginning with a prayer to Shiva and Brahman before defining the three core branches of Indian astronomy: math, time, and the spheres.</summary>
<keywords>Parameshvara, Aryabhata, Bhatadipika, Brahman, Indian Astronomy, Mathematics, Time-reckoning, Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory commentary on Aryabhata's work, specifically defining the scope of the three main sections: Mathematics, Time-reckoning, and the Celestial Sphere.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

...meaningful. The Supreme Brahman <note>The ultimate reality or absolute in Hindu philosophy</note> is the root cause of the universe, transcending the three forms <note>The Trimurti: Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva</note>, all-pervading, and is here called the Self-Existent. Aryabhata, having thus prostrated before Brahman, declares these three subjects: **Mathematics**, **The Reckoning of Time**, and **The Celestial Sphere**. Because he refers to himself in the third person, the text says "he declares." 

In this context, **Mathematics** <term>Ganita: includes arithmetic, algebra, and geometry</term> consists of various types such as addition, mixtures, progressions, the ten-fold pulverizer <note>Kuttaka: a method for solving indeterminate equations</note>, shadows, and many others. However, in this work, generally only that much of general mathematics is promised as is necessary to serve as a tool for the Reckoning of Time and the Celestial Sphere, along with a few other topics. 

**The Reckoning of Time** <term>Kalakriya</term> means the movement of time. This refers to the calculation of planetary positions, which is the means of measuring time. 

**The Celestial Sphere** <term>Gola</term> refers to the structure composed of the orbits of the planets and stars situated in the middle of the space within the shell of the universe. It has the solid, circular Earth at its center and possesses all the specific characteristics such as declination. It is impelled by the wind known as *Pravaha* and is expressed by terms such as the "Wheel of Time," the "Wheel of Lights," and the "Cage of Stars." Because it is the basis for imagining various circles like the horizon and the prime vertical, it is specifically a subject of mathematical inquiry.

These three are also of two kinds: that which is known by direct instruction and that which is known through fundamental logic. Among these, the duration of an age <term>Yuga</term>, the apogees, the epicycles, and the declination are known by instruction alone. The movement of a planet on a desired day, the desired declination, the radius of the diurnal circle, the earth-sine, the shadow, and the time in *nadikas* <note>A unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> are known through logic derived from the instructed durations of the ages. Thus, there is a two-fold nature. By offering salutations to the Self-Existent, it is also shown that the *Brahma-Siddhanta* <note>An ancient astronomical tradition</note> is the root source of the system being composed.

Now, intending to briefly show the revolutions in an age and other matters known by instruction through the *Ten Giti Stanzas*, he states the technical terminology useful for that:

-> **The classified letters are placed in the square places; the unclassified letters in the non-square places. 'Ya' equals the sum of 'Ka' and 'Ng'.** <-
-> **The nine vowels are used in the two-times-nine slots of square and non-square places. They may also be used in the final square place.** <-

<summary>This page defines the three core pillars of Aryabhata's astronomical system—Mathematics, Time-reckoning, and the Celestial Sphere—and introduces the alphanumeric notation system used to encode large numbers.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhata, Brahman, Mathematics, Kalakriya, Gola, Brahma-Siddhanta, Alphanumeric notation, Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of Aryabhata's unique alphabetical notation system, following the introductory definitions of the Gitikapada.</meta>

# Section on the Gitika

...thus. **Classified letters** <note>original: "vargākṣarāṇi"; these are the 25 consonants from Ka to Ma arranged in five groups of five</note> are placed in **classified places** <note>odd-numbered decimal places: 1s, 100s, 10,000s, etc.</note>. These classified letters, beginning with *Ka* and ending with *Ma*, are to be established in the classified positions such as the units, hundreds, and ten-thousands places. The numerical value is to be understood in this sequence.

In the **unclassified places** <note>even-numbered decimal places: 10s, 1,000s, 100,000s, etc.</note>, the **unclassified letters** are used. These are the letters starting with *Ya*. These unclassified letters are to be established in the unclassified positions such as the tens, thousands, and lakhs places. 

The numerical values are understood starting from the letter *Ka*. *Ka* has the value of one; *Kha* has the value of two, and so on in order. *Ja* has the value of ten. *Tha* has the value of eleven. *Na* has the value of twenty. *Ma* has the value of twenty-five. Thus, the numerical value is understood according to the traditional order of the alphabet.

**"Ya is equal to Nga and Ma"** <note>original: "ñau yaḥ"; a rule explaining that the semi-vowel Ya (30) equals the sum of the last two nasals (5 + 25)</note>. By joining the values of *Nga* and *Ma*, *Ya* is their equivalent. This means the sum of the value five and the value twenty-five results in the number thirty. Here, thirty is stated by acknowledging the first position <note>the units place</note>, not by acknowledging the second position. Indeed, in the second position <note>the tens place</note>, *Ya* represents the number three. This is what is being expressed. 

The letters starting with *Ra* <note>Ra, La, Va</note> follow in sequence in the second position with values of four and so on. The letter *Ha* in the second position has the value of ten, which means it indicates the number one hundred. Thus, even though the value of *Ha* is prescribed for an unclassified place, it is established in a classified place as a different numerical value. Similarly, the values prescribed for classified places by vowels like *A* are established in unclassified places as different numerical values. This is established by logical reasoning. 

Where it was necessary to say "*Ya* is equal to *Nga*", the fact that it was stated with two letters as "**Nga and Ma are Ya**" demonstrates that numbers will also be expressed by conjunct consonants <note>letters joined together</note>. 

Regarding what should be used for the "zeros" <note>empty places</note> that have no specific assigned numerical value, he says: **"The nine vowels are used in the two-nines of places, classified and unclassified."** In the eighteen places <note>two times nine</note>, the nine vowels are used in sequence: *A, I, U, Ri, Lri, E, Ai, O, Au*. This is what is meant: the vowels attached to the letters starting with *Ka*...

<summary>This page explains Aryabhata's system of phonemic numeration, detailing how consonants represent digits and vowels indicate decimal places (powers of 100). It specifically clarifies the mathematical values of classified (Varga) and unclassified (Avarga) letters.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhata, Sanskrit mathematics, alphabetical notation, Varga letters, Avarga letters, decimal places, Gitikapada, Indian astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of Aryabhata's alphanumeric notation system and introduces the planetary revolution counts for a Great Age (Yuga).</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

The vowels serve as place-markers and do not represent specific numerical values themselves. One might ask: how can only nine vowels be used across eighteen decimal places? To this, the author replies: *In the square and non-square places.* In the nine "square" places <note>the odd-numbered decimal places: 1st, 100th, 10,000th, etc.</note>, the nine vowels starting with 'a' are used in order. Likewise, those same vowels are used in the "non-square" places <note>the even-numbered decimal places: 10th, 1000th, 100,000th, etc.</note>. Others should be understood similarly. Thus, a number formed by consonants like 'va' combined with the first vowel should be placed in the first non-square place. When combined with the second vowel, it is placed in the second non-square place, and so on. In this manner, numbers across eighteen decimal places are known.

If someone intends to express a number even larger than that, how is it done? To this, he says: *Or in the square following the nine.* This means that in the nine square places above the first set, and similarly in the nine non-square places above the first set, these same nine vowels are used again. They should be used in conjunction with some distinguishing mark, such as an <term>Anusvara</term> <note>a nasalization mark in Sanskrit script</note>. However, the technical applications of this science do not exceed eighteen decimal places.

Now, he states the number of <term>Bhaganas</term> <note>complete revolutions in an orbit</note> for the Sun and other planets during a <term>Chaturyuga</term> <note>a Great Age consisting of 4,320,000 years</note>.

<margin>Manuscript Ba, line 61</margin>
The revolutions of the Sun in a Yuga are *khyu-ghṛ* <note>4,320,000</note>; for the Moon, *ca-ya-gi-yi-ṅu-śu-chlṛ* <note>57,753,336</note>; for the Earth <insert>rotation</insert> <gloss>b</gloss>, *ṅi-śi-bu-ṇlṛ-ṣkhṛ* <note>1,582,237,500</note> eastward; for Saturn, *ḍu-ṅghvi*; for Jupiter, *khri-ccyu-bhṛ*; for Mars, *bhad-li-jha-nu-ṣkhṛ*; and for Venus and Mercury, the same as the Sun. || 2 ||

The names for the numbers occupying the eighteen decimal places are:

->Units, tens, hundreds, thousands, ten-thousands, hundred-thousands, millions, and ten-millions in order.<-
->A hundred-million, a billion, ten-billion, hundred-billion, trillion, and ten-trillion.<-
->Then a hundred-trillion, quadrillion, ten-quadrillion, and a hundred-quadrillion; these names each increase by a factor of ten. \*)<-

---

\*) Similarly in the *Lilavati* <note>A famous mathematical treatise by Bhaskara II</note>.

<summary>This page explains Aryabhata's system for representing large numbers using vowels as place-value markers and lists the astronomical revolution counts for the planets in a Great Age.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhatiya, place-value system, planetary revolutions, Yuga, Sanskrit mathematics, Lilavati, decimal places</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Gitikapada, the first section of Aryabhata's astronomical treatise, focusing on the numerical notation system and the revolutions of the planets.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
-># Section on the Song-like Verses<-

<note>The title 'Gitikapada' refers to the first section of the Aryabhatiya, composed in the Arya meter.</note>

This is to be understood by that [method]. **The revolutions of the Sun in a Yuga:** In a Four-Age cycle <term>Chaturyuga; a vast span of 4,320,000 years</term>, the revolutions of the Sun are denoted by the code *khyudhr*. The letter *kh* combined with the vowel *u* signifies twenty thousand <note>original: "ayuta-dvayam"</note>. The letter *dh* combined with the vowel *u* signifies three hundred thousand <note>original: "laksha-trayam"</note>. Thus, everywhere, when two consonants are joined, a single vowel applies to both. The letter *dh* combined with the vowel *r* signifies four million <note>original: "prayuta-chatushkam"</note>. By this logic, the numbers are to be understood everywhere.

**The Moon:** This refers to the Moon's [revolutions]. In the technical rules <term>sutras</term>, usage may occur even without case endings. The revolutions of the Moon in a Yuga are *cayagiyiṅuśuchḷ*. *Ca* is six. *Ya* is thirty. *Śi* is thirty <note>Note: The commentator is breaking down the alphanumeric code used by Aryabhata to store large numbers</note>. *Yi* is three thousand. *Ṅu* is fifty thousand. *Śu* is seven hundred thousand. *Chḷ* is seven million. *Lṛ* is fifty million.

**The Earth:** This refers to the Earth. The revolutions are *ṅiśibuṇḷṣṛ*. These are the revolutions resulting from its eastward motion. *Ṅi* is one hundred and fifty million—meaning five in the ninth place and one in the tenth place. *Vṛ* is two million. *Ṣṛ* is eighty million. Here, the number of rotations the Earth performs eastward in a Four-Age cycle is stated. 

<margin>The Earth is famously known to be stationary; why then is its rotation mentioned here?</margin> 

It is explained: Due to the driving force of the Provector wind <term>Pravaha; the celestial wind believed to move the stars</term>, the star-cluster <term>nakshatra-mandala</term> appears to rotate westward. Due to a false perception <note>optical illusion</note>, the rotation of the Earth is perceived. Accepting that [perspective], the rotation of the Earth is mentioned here. In reality, however, there is no rotation of the Earth. Therefore, it should be understood that the mention of Earth's rotation here is intended to demonstrate the rotation of the star-cluster. As he will say regarding false perception:

> Just as a man in a boat moving forward sees the stationary [objects on the bank] moving backward, so do the stationary stars appear to move straight westward to one at Lanka <note>Lanka refers to the terrestrial equator in Indian astronomy</note>.

In a day and night, the total rotation of the celestial sphere is equal to the daily motion of the Sun...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical exposition of planetary revolutions (yuga-bhaganas) according to the Aryabhatiya, following the calculations for the Sun and Moon.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
# In the Aryabhatiya

The celestial sphere also rotates. Therefore, the measure of the rotation of the star-cluster <note>the sidereal firmament</note> is equal to the number of terrestrial days in a Yuga <note>an immense cosmic cycle of time</note> plus the number of the Sun's revolutions in that same Yuga. This is what is stated here.

**Saturn: *nghu-dhva*** etc. The revolutions of Saturn in a Yuga are: *nghu* is fourteen times ten thousand <note>140,000</note>; *dhva* is five hundred <note>500</note>; *vi* is six thousand <note>6,000</note>; *gha* is four <note>4</note>; *va* is sixty <note>60</note>. <note>Total: 146,564 revolutions</note>.

**Jupiter: *khri-cyu-bha*** etc. The revolutions of Jupiter are: *khi* is two hundred <note>200</note>; *ri* is four thousand <note>4,000</note>; *cyu* is six times ten thousand <note>60,000</note>; *yu* is three hundred thousand <note>300,000</note>; *bha* is twenty-four <note>24</note>. <note>Total: 364,224 revolutions</note>.

**Mars: *bhadli-nkhu-nṛ*** etc. The revolutions of Mars are: *bha* is twenty-four <note>24</note>; *di* is one thousand eight hundred <note>1,800</note>; *li* is five thousand <note>5,000</note>; *nkhu* is nine times ten thousand <note>90,000</note>; *nṛ* is two hundred thousand <note>200,000</note>; *vṛ* is two million <note>2,000,000</note>. By summing these numbers, the total revolutions are established. <note>Total: 2,296,824 revolutions</note>.

**Venus and Mercury are Solar.** The revolutions of Venus and Mercury in a Yuga are the same as the Sun's. The Sun's revolutions are *khyughṛ* <note>4,320,000</note>.

---

Thus, having shown the Yuga revolutions of the Sun and others in the first verse, the author now declares in the second verse the revolutions of the Moon's apogee <term>apogee: the point in an orbit farthest from Earth</term>, the revolutions of the "fast-apogees" <term>shighrocca: the anomaly used to calculate planetary positions, often corresponding to the Earth's own motion</term> of Mercury and Venus, and for the remaining planets—Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—their "fast-apogees," as well as the revolutions of the Moon's nodes <note>the points where the orbit crosses the ecliptic</note> and the starting time of these revolutions.

> The Moon's apogee is *ju-ṣi-kha-dha*; Mercury's [fast-apogee] is *su-gu-śi-thṛ-na*; Venus's is *ja-ṣa-ba-li-khu-hṛ*; the others are like the Sun. <insert>Bal. 61</insert>
> The nodes move in reverse, *bu-phi-na-ca*; [all starting] from the first of the light half of the month of Chaitra, at sunrise in Lanka. <gloss>2</gloss>

The revolutions of the Moon's apogee are *ju-ṣi-kha-dha*. Or, the reading is *rju-ṣi-kha-dha*. *Ju* is eight times ten thousand <note>80,000</note>; *ṣa* is four hundred thousand <note>400,000</note>; *khi* is eight thousand <note>8,000</note>; *vi* is two hundred <note>200</note>; *dha* is nineteen <note>19</note>. <note>Total: 488,219 revolutions</note>.

The revolutions of Mercury's fast-apogee are *su-gu-śi-thṛ-na*. *Su* is nine hundred thousand <note>900,000</note>; *gu* <gloss>m</gloss> is three times ten thousand <note>30,000</note>; *śi* is seven thousand <note>7,000</note>; *thṛ* is one million seven hundred thousand <note>1,700,000</note>; *na* is twenty <note>20</note>. <note>Total: 17,937,020 revolutions</note>.

The revolutions of Venus's fast-apogee are *ja-ṣa-ba-li-khu-hṛ*. *Ja* is...

# Section on the Celestial Song
<page-num>7</page-num>

Eight. *Pa* is eighty. *Bi* is two thousand three hundred. *Khu* is twenty thousand. *Hṛ* is seven hundred thousand. <note>These are coded numerical values representing the revolutions of the planets according to Aryabhata's unique alphanumeric system.</note>

The remaining ones are the same as the Sun's. The revolutions of the <term>apogees</term> <note>the point in an orbit farthest from Earth</note> of the remaining planets—Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—are identical to the Sun's revolutions. Later, he will describe the degrees of the <term>slow apogees</term> <note>Mandocca</note> for these planets. Therefore, it is established that the revolutions of the <term>fast apogees</term> <note>Shighrocca</note> are what are being stated here.

*Bu-phi-na-ca* refers to the revolutions of the <term>Node</term> <note>the point where the moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic</note>, specifically the Moon's Node, which move in a reverse <note>retrograde</note> direction. *Bu* is twenty-three ten-thousands (230,000). *Phi* is two thousand two hundred. *Na* is twenty. *Ca* is six. <note>Totaling 232,226 revolutions</note>. He will later state the revolutions of the nodes for Mars and the other planets. However, no <term>inclination</term> <note>Vikshepa</note> is prescribed for the Sun. Thus, it is proven that these are the revolutions of the Moon's Node. The apogees and nodes are not visible in the sky. As Brahmagupta <note>A famous 7th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> says:

> "The apogees and nodes are conceived merely to explain the motion of the planets."

And from the sunrise at Lanka on Wednesday. This means starting from sunrise at Lanka <note>The theoretical zero-meridian in Indian astronomy</note> on Wednesday at the beginning of the <term>Krita Yuga</term> <note>The first of the four world ages</note>. This refers to the revolutions of the Sun and other planets moving through the zodiac starting from the first point of Aries. "Sunrise" here refers to the mean sunrise; however, the beginning of the <term>Kalpa</term> <note>a vast cosmic eon</note> is the true sunrise. In that context, there is no significant difference between the mean and the true sunrise.

In the third verse, he describes the <term>Manus</term> <note>progenitors of humanity</note> within the duration of a Kalpa and the time that has already elapsed:

->A day of Brahma contains *Dha* (fourteen) Manus.<-
->Each Manu's period contains *Shkha* (seventy-two) Yugas.<-
->Six Manus have passed, and of the current Manu, *Chna* (twenty-seven) Yugas have passed.<-
->Since the beginning of the Kalpa, three-quarters of a Yuga have passed before the Thursday of the Bharata War. // 3 //<- <gloss>B. II. 11 1.9</gloss>

"A day of Brahma contains *Dha*": *Ka* refers to Brahma. In his day (*Ahni*), there are *Dha*, which means fourteen, Manus.
"Each Manu's period contains *Shkha*": In the time of each individual Manu, there are *Shkha* Yugas (sets of four ages). *Sha* is seventy and *Kha* is two. This means seventy-two.
"Those that have passed": Before the current <term>Kali Yuga</term> <note>the present age of darkness</note>, those Manus have passed. They are *Ca*, meaning six.
"And *Chna* of the Manu's Yugas": Of the current seventh Manu, the number of Yugas that have passed is...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and astronomical exposition of the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the dimensions of celestial bodies and the Earth.</meta>

# In the Aryabhatiya

<page-num>10</page-num>

This occurs. In the fifth verse, he demonstrates the measurement of a <term>yojana (a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles)</term> and the dimensions of the Earth and other bodies in yojanas.

<margin>Ba 11. 15</margin>
> **A yojana is eight thousand human lengths. The Earth's diameter is 1,050. The diameters of the Sun and Moon are 4,410 and 315. Meru is one. The diameters of Venus, Jupiter, Mercury, Saturn, and Mars are 1/10th, 1/15th, 1/20th, 1/25th, and 1/30th of the Moon's diameter. Their orbits are equal to the Sun's.**

"A yojana is eight thousand human lengths": Eight thousand times the measure of a man <note>original: "nṛ"</note> is the measure of one yojana. "The Earth's diameter is 1,050": 'Ji' represents one thousand and 'la' represents fifty. These are the yojanas of the Earth's diameter. "The Sun and Moon are 4,410 and 315": The yojanas of the Sun's diameter are 'ghi-ñā'. 'Ghi' is four hundred and 'ñā' is four thousand <insert>plus 'ja' which is ten</insert>. For the Moon, 'gi-ṇa': 'Gi' is three hundred and 'ṇa' is fifteen. "Meru is one": The measurement of Mount Meru's <note>The sacred mountain at the center of the cosmos</note> diameter is 'ka', meaning one. The diameters of the disks of Venus and the others are, in order, 1/10th, 1/15th, 1/20th, 1/25th, and 1/30th of the Moon's diameter. These diameters are calculated based on the Moon's orbit. Therefore, when multiplied by the <term>semi-diameter (radius)</term> and divided by the Moon's distance in yojanas, the result is in minutes of arc. Furthermore, when those are multiplied by the semi-diameter and divided by the average of the <term>slow-motion hypotenuse (mandakarṇa)</term> and <term>fast-motion hypotenuse (śīghrakarṇa)</term>, they become "true" or corrected. This is the instruction. Thus, Maya <note>A legendary astronomer-architect</note> says:

->Divided by the sum of the three and four hypotenuses, doubled, and divided by the radius.<-
<note>original: "Surya Siddhanta, Chapter on Planetary Conjunctions, 14"</note>

Here, the Moon's distance in yojanas refers to the Moon's mean distance. "Equal to the Sun's": This means the number of revolutions in a <term>Yuga (an immense cosmic cycle of time)</term> is equal to the Sun's revolutions. To the north and south of the equator of the planets...

<meta>This page continues the Gitikapada (Section on the Hymn) of Aryabhata's astronomical treatise, specifically focusing on the measurements of planetary inclinations and the standard units of human measurement.</meta>

# Section on the Hymn
<page-num>11</page-num>

In the sixth verse, he states the measure of the maximum declination and the measure of a man.

->The declination of the ecliptic and the degrees of the planets; the moon's deviation is half of nine; <insert>Ball. 15</insert><-
->For Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars, it is two, one, and half of three; for Venus and Mercury, it is two. A man is ninety-six fingers or four cubits. || 6 ||<-

**The declination of the ecliptic and the degrees of the planets:** The declination <note>original: "apakarama"; the angle between the celestial equator and the ecliptic</note> of the planets is twenty-four degrees <note>original: "bha-amsha"; in the Aryabhatiya's alphanumeric system, 'bha' represents 24</note>. This refers to the maximum declination. This means that at a distance of three signs <note>90 degrees</note> from the intersection of the east and west points, the distance between the celestial equator and the ecliptic is equal to twenty-four degrees.

**The maximum deviation of the moon from the ecliptic is half of nine:** that is, four and a half degrees.

**For Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars, it is two, one, and half of three:** The celestial latitude <note>original: "vikshepa"; deviation from the ecliptic</note> of Saturn is two degrees <note>represented by 'kha'</note>. For Jupiter, it is one degree <note>represented by 'ka'</note>. For Mars, it is half of three <note>represented by 'ga-ardham'</note>, which is one and a half degrees.

**For Venus and Mercury, it is two:** The deviation for Venus and Mercury is two degrees.

**A man is ninety-six fingers or four cubits:** A man <note>original: "na"</note> is ninety-six fingers <note>original: "svangula"; 's' = 90, 'v' = 6</note> and four cubits <note>original: "gha-hasta"; 'gha' = 4</note>. This means a man is measured as ninety-six fingers. He is also four cubits tall. By this, it is explained that wherever the word "man" <note>original: "nri"</note> is used—such as in the definition of a *yojana* <note>a unit of distance</note>—it refers to a measure of ninety-six fingers. This is the same as the measure of four cubits. It is also implied that one cubit <term>hasta: a unit of length roughly from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</term> consists of twenty-four fingers. Since the specific size of a "finger" <term>angula: a standard unit of measure based on the width of a finger</term> is not defined here, the common worldly understanding of a finger-breadth is accepted. That measurement is stated in another treatise (the *Lilavati* <note>a famous mathematical text by Bhaskara II</note>):

> Eight barley-corns laid side by side make one finger;
> twenty-four fingers make one cubit.
> Four cubits make one staff;
> and two thousand of those make one *krosha* <note>a unit of distance, roughly 2 miles</note>.

In this discussion of celestial latitude, Saturn and the others were listed separately from Venus and Mercury. By this, it is indicated that there is a difference in the method of calculating the latitude for those two groups. Next, he describes the degrees of the nodes for the five planets starting with Mars...

<summary>This page defines the maximum celestial declination (24°) and the orbital inclinations of the planets, while also establishing standard linear units like the finger and cubit based on human proportions.</summary>
<keywords>declination, celestial latitude, planetary inclination, angula, hasta, units of measurement, Aryabhata, Lilavati</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of planetary parameters from the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the positions of the nodes and apogees for the Sun and the five visible planets.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

With the seventh verse, he <note>the author, Aryabhata</note> states the degrees of the apogees <note><term>Mandocca</term>: the point in a planet's orbit furthest from the Earth</note> for those planets along with the Sun.

> The first nodes of Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn are 20, 60, 40, 80, and 100 degrees.
> For the Sun, it is 78; for these others, the apogees are 210, 90, 118, 180, and 236 degrees. || 7 ||

The degrees of the node for Mercury are *na* <note>original: "na" represents 20 in Aryabhata's alphanumeric system</note> twenty. For Venus, *va* sixty. For Mars, *ra* forty. For Jupiter, *sha* eighty. For Saturn, *hla* one hundred. These are the first nodes in degrees. These stated degrees, starting from the beginning of Aries, are where the first nodes of Mercury and the others are situated. By the word "first," it is indicated that there is also a second node. That second node would be situated at a distance of half a circle <note>180 degrees</note> from the first node. The point of intersection between the ecliptic and the planetary orbit is called the "node" <note><term>Pata</term>: the node where the planet crosses the celestial equator</note>. This occurs at two places. By the expression "moving," the motion of these nodes is intended. And that motion is retrograde <note>moving backward through the zodiac</note>. By the phrase "the nodes are retrograde," the backward motion of the nodes is expressed. This describes the position of the nodes at this specific time.

The apogee of the Sun is likewise *dda*. *Dda* is eighteen; *va* is sixty. This means the Sun's apogee is situated at seventy-eight degrees from the beginning of Aries. For these aforementioned planets (Mercury and the others), the apogees are stated by the syllables *javi* and so on. The apogee of Mercury is *javi*, two hundred and ten degrees. For Venus, *sa*, ninety degrees. For Mars, *hda*. *Hla* is one hundred and *da* is eighteen; thus, one hundred and eighteen degrees. For Jupiter, *hlay*. *Hla* is one hundred, *la* is fifty, and *ya* is thirty; thus, one hundred and eighty degrees. For Saturn, *khicca*. *Khi* is two hundred, *ca* is six, and *ya* is thirty; thus, two hundred and thirty-six degrees. By the expression "moving," the motion of these is also declared. Their motion is direct <note>forward-moving</note>, like the apogee of the Moon. This describes the position of the apogees at this specific time. Of the many nodes and apogees...

<summary>This page details the specific longitudinal positions (in degrees from Aries) for the ascending nodes and apogees of the Sun and the five planets, explaining Aryabhata's unique alphanumeric notation system.</summary>

# Chapter on the Basic Constants

Thinking that even in a short time a specific variation in motion occurs, their motion is not explicitly stated here. However, their revolutions in a <term>Kalpa</term><note>A Kalpa is a vast cosmic period in Hindu cosmology, equivalent to 4.32 billion years.</note> are mentioned in another treatise <note>original: "Suryasiddhante Madhyadhikare 41"</note> (The *Surya Siddhanta*, Chapter on Mean Motion, verse 41):

> The revolutions of the slow motion of the Sun moving eastward in a Kalpa are 387.
> For Mars, they are 204; for Mercury, 388.
> For Jupiter, 900; for Venus, 535.
> For the slow motion of Saturn, 39; and now for the Nodes moving westward:
> For Mars, 214; for Mercury, 488.
> For Jupiter, 174; and similarly for the "Guru" <note>The teacher of the demons, i.e., Venus</note>, 163.
> The revolutions of the Node of Saturn in a Kalpa are 662.

So it is said. "Of the Guru" refers to the Teacher of the Daityas <note>Venus/Shukra</note> <margin>Not so. Since the reading "of Bhrigu" is seen in other manuscripts, it should be inferred that the reading "of the Guru" is an error.</margin>. In this book, the years of the Kalpa that passed before the <term>Kali Yuga</term><note>The current fourth age in the Hindu cycle of time</note> regarding planetary motion are written:

> The years of the Kali era are 1,986,120,000.
> The planets previously mentioned are travelers from the beginning of their movement.

In this book, the <term>Civil Days</term><note>original: "Kudivasa"; solar days measured from sunrise to sunrise</note> are 1,577,917,500. In the *Bhata-prakashika* <note>A famous commentary on Aryabhata's work</note>, a different motion for the apsides and nodes is shown:

> 357,502,248,000.
> This is the Age-cycle of the nodes of Mercury and the others as recited by the learned.

->One, two, three, four, and five revolutions are proclaimed,<-

Through these, the determination of the nodes is achieved using the Rule of Three <note>The Rule of Three (trairashika) is a mathematical method to find a fourth proportional value, similar to cross-multiplication</note> and other methods. Here, the "Yuga" refers to a period of years. For the nodes established by the Sun, the stated values are only in degrees, not a full count of revolutions. These degrees always move in a forward direction <note>original: "kramagata", meaning direct motion</note> and never in reverse <note>original: "vilomaga", meaning retrograde</note>. Similarly, the Yugas and revolutions for the slow-apogees <term>Mandocca: the point in a planet's orbit furthest from the Earth, similar to apogee</term> of the Sun, Mercury, and others are shown:

->For the Sun's apogee, the Yuga is stated as 1,158,791,616 multiplied by a thousand,<-
->and the revolutions are thirteen.<-

->For Mercury's apogee, the Yuga is 2,383,323,827 multiplied by a hundred,<-
->and seven revolutions are remembered therein. <note>*</note>)<-

->For Venus, the Yuga is 7,914,585,440;<-
->for Jupiter, it is half of that, and for both, the revolution is one. <note>†</note>)<-

Thus it is said. Regarding Saturn and Mars, the reading is seen in the text of that specific section. Their reading should be performed as follows:

->Equal to 1,518,511,440,000 is the Yuga for Saturn's apogee;<-
->that of Mars is double that, and the revolutions for both are five. <note>**</note>)<-

In this case as well, only the recited degrees are obtained, not full revolutions. Thus it appears—

<note>**</note>) In the *Prakashika* manuscript, the reading is "equal to 1,785,751,110." Furthermore, "the revolutions for those two are five and nine" is written.

Some intelligent person, having conceptualized this through their own intellect, has written as follows. In this year, the years elapsed before the start of the Kali Age <note>The Kali Yuga, the current fourth age in Hindu cosmology, traditionally calculated to have begun in 3102 BCE</note> are recorded:

->The years before the Kali Age are 1,982,880,000. <gloss>Kali Age</gloss><-

Thus it is said. With the eighth verse, the author describes the *manda-vrtta* <term>manda-vrtta: the slow epicycle used to calculate the equation of center</term> of the Moon and of the Sun, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn mentioned in the previous verse, as well as the *shighra-vrtta* <term>shighra-vrtta: the fast epicycle used to calculate the equation of conjunction, reflecting the Earth's motion</term> of Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, and Mercury.

<margin>Manuscript reference: Ba 11. 18</margin>
->The *manda* epicycle of the Moon is seven half-degrees; for the others, they are 3, 7, 4, 14, 7, and 9 respectively.<-
->For the *shighra* epicycles of Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, and Mercury, they are 9, 16, 53, 59, and 31. || 8 ||<-

"Half of nine" <note>referring to the Sanskrit letter 'ka' which represents 9</note> means half-degrees. The meaning is that the circumferences mentioned here are reduced by four and a half. The *manda* epicycle of the Moon is 7. For the Sun, Mercury, and the others mentioned in the previous verse, the resulting epicycles are 3 and so on. The circumference of the epicycles for the planets is calculated in degrees. Therefore, the epicycles belong to the planets. Among them, the *manda* epicycle of the Sun is 3. Only the *manda* epicycle exists for the Moon and the Sun <note>The Sun and Moon do not have *shighra* (conjunction) epicycles in this system as they do not orbit the Sun in the same manner as the planets</note>. For Mercury, it is 7. For Venus, 4. For Mars, 14. For Jupiter, 7. For Saturn, 9. Regarding the *shighra* high-points of Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, and Mercury: these are the epicycles produced by the power of the fast motion caused by the *shighra* high-point. For Saturn, it is 9. For Jupiter, it is 16 <note>derived from 'ga' (3) and 'u' (13)</note>. For Mars, it is 53 <note>derived from 'ga' (3) and 'la' (50)</note>. For Venus, it is 59 <note>derived from 'ka' (9) and 'la' (50)</note>. For Mercury, it is 31 <note>derived from 'da' (18) and 'u' (13)</note>. Here, there may be a difference in the order of the *manda* and *shighra* epicycles; by this, a difference in the logic of the *manda-sphuta* <term>manda-sphuta: the planet's position corrected for the first inequality</term> and *shighra-sphuta* <term>shighra-sphuta: the final true longitude corrected for the second inequality</term> is indicated. Just as with the result of the fast motion...

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

The necessity of using the hypotenuse to calculate the "slow" correction <note>original: mandaphala; the correction for the planet's elliptical eccentricity</note> and its absence elsewhere. Alternatively, the process of iterative calculation <note>original: avishesha-karana</note> for the "slow" hypotenuse and its components, versus the lack of such a process for the "fast" hypotenuse <note>original: shighra-karna; the correction for the Earth's motion relative to the planet</note>. Having thus demonstrated the epicycles for the odd-numbered quadrants, the author now describes the epicycles for the even-numbered quadrants and the measurement of the Earth's atmosphere in the ninth verse:

->For the retrograde planets, the "slow" epicycles in the second and fourth quadrants are represented by the letters *nga, kha, da, ja, da*.<-
<margin>Aryabhatiya II, 18</margin>
->From the "fast" apex, the epicycles are *nya, na, kla, hrla, kna*; the final orbit of the Earth's wind is *giyingasha*. || 9 ||<-

For the retrograde planets mentioned in the previous verse—Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—the "slow" epicycles <note>epicycles used to account for the planet's own orbital speed</note> produced by their slow motion in the second and fourth quadrants are as follows: For Mercury, *nga* (5). For Venus, *kha* (2). For Mars, *da* (18). For Jupiter, *ja* (8). For Saturn, *da* (13).

Next, for the aforementioned Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, and Mercury, the "fast" epicycles <note>epicycles used to account for the Earth's motion</note> produced by the speed of their "fast" apex <note>original: shighrocca</note> are given for the second and fourth quadrants: For Saturn, *nya* (8). For Jupiter, *na* (15). For Mars, *kla*: *ka* is 1 and *la* is 50, totaling 51. For Venus, *hrla*: *ha* is 7 and *la* is 50, totaling 57. For Mercury, *kna*: *ka* is 9 and *na* is 20, totaling 29. By specifying these for the second and fourth quadrants, it is implied that the previously mentioned values belong to the first and third quadrants.

The "Earth-wind" <note>original: kuvayu</note> refers to the atmosphere surrounding the Earth which has a variable motion; its final orbit or outer limit is *giyingasha*. *Gi* is 300, *yi* is 3,000, *nga* is 5, and *sha* is 70 <note>Totaling 3,375 yojanas</note>. Beyond this is the wind called *Pravaha*, which moves constantly and causes the entire circle of stars to rotate toward the west.

In the tenth verse, the author provides the sine-differences <note>original: jyardha; literally "half-chords"</note> which are useful for the sections on Time-Reckoning and the Celestial Sphere:

->*Makhi, bhakhi, phakhi, dhakhi, nakhi, nyakhi, ngakhi, haska, ski, kishga, shghaki, kidhva...*<- <note>This is the beginning of Aryabhata's famous table of sines, encoded in alphabetical notation.</note>

# Section on the Hymn-like Verses

<gloss>u u ha-ca u u u u u</gloss>
<note>The following string of syllables represents numerical values using Aryabhata's unique alphanumeric system.</note>
**Makhi, Bhakhi, Phakhi, Dhakhi, Nakhi, Ñakhi, Ñaki, Jhaki, Haski, Skaki, Ihakya, Kighva, Ghlaki, Kighra, Hakya, Dhva, Adga, Stga, Sga, Jha, Dva, Lka, Pra, Pha, Ha—these are the sine differences.** || 10 ||

The "sine differences" <term>kallardhajyas; literally 'sine-arcs in minutes'</term> are stated here in terms of minutes of arc. Indeed, the chord <term>jiva; literally 'bowstring'</term> is of two types: the full chord and the half-chord. A line extending from one end of an arc of a circle's circumference to the other end is called a "full chord" <term>samastajya</term>. Half of that is called the "half-chord" <term>ardhajya</term>. In the study of the celestial sphere and the movement of time, the half-chord is almost exclusively used in practice. Therefore, the presentation of half-chords is provided here. Twenty-four sines are recited here. Thus, it is demonstrated that these sines are calculated by dividing a quadrant of a circle <note>90 degrees</note> into twenty-four equal parts <note>each part being 3 degrees and 45 minutes</note>.

The first sine is *Makhi*: 225. *Bhakhi* is 224. The others should be known similarly. The eighth is *Haski*: 199. *Skaki*: 191. *Kighra*: 183. *Jhaki*: 174. *Kidhva*: 164. *Lchaki*: 154. *Kigra*: 143. *Hkya*: 131. *Dhvadga*: 119. *Stga*: 106. *Sga*: 93. *Jha*: 79. *Dva*: 65. *Lka*: 51. *Pra*: 37. *Pha*: 22. *Ha*: 7. 

In this context, the second sine difference is the second sine minus the first sine produced by one arc. The third sine difference is the third sine minus the sine produced by two arcs. The others are to be understood in the same way. Although these half-chords are to be proven through logic, it should be understood that they are taught here because they are used in many calculations. 

He now states the fruit of knowing the *Ten Hymn-like Verses*:

<margin>o/</margin>
-> Knowing this <insert>Ten-Verse Hymn</insert> <gloss>/</gloss> aphorism describing the movements of the Earth and the planets in the cage of the stars, <-
-> one pierces through the orbits of the planets and stars and attains the Supreme Brahman. <- || 11 ||

---

*) In this verse, a break in the meter is observed. Therefore, the reading in the *Prakashika* commentary—*dhaha hachra sga* etc.—is the better reading.

# In the Aryabhatiya

The movement of the Earth and the planets is contained within the *Ten Giti Stanzas* <note>original: "Dashagitikasutra"; the opening section of the Aryabhatiya</note>. Having understood this within the cage of the stars <note>the celestial sphere</note> and within the globe; the Earth stands in the middle of the cage of the stars. The planets, from the Moon to Saturn, moving eastward by their own motion, appear to revolve westward due to the motion of the star-circle. Above them, the circle of the constellations, which lacks its own motion, revolves westward. Having understood all this, a true mathematician who knows the movements of the planets and stars in this way, pierces through their paths and attains the Supreme Brahman <note>the ultimate reality or liberation</note>.

->Thus ends the first chapter, the Section on the Giti Stanzas, in the "Light on Aryabhata" composed by Paramesvara.<-

---

Having thus taught a collection of truths beyond the reach of the senses through the work consisting of the *Ten Giti Stanzas*, the author now intends to demonstrate a collection of truths that can be understood through the logic underlying them. Beginning with a salutation to his chosen deity, he announces the title of this further work:

->Having bowed to Brahma, the Earth, the Moon, Mercury, Venus, the Sun, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, and the clusters of stars,<-
->Aryabhata here sets forth the knowledge honored at Kusumpura. (1)<-

Having bowed to Brahma, the Earth, the planets, and the groups of stars, Aryabhata sets forth the "knowledge honored at Kusumpura"—that is, in this region called Kusumpura <note>modern-day Patna, India</note>. He proclaims the system <term>Tantra: a scientific or systematic treatise</term> which serves as the means for knowing the motions of the planets and was revered by the inhabitants of Kusumpura. <insert>[By the phrase "honored at Kusumpura"]</insert>... Since the sections on "Time-Reckoning" and "The Sphere" are understood through mathematics, he first sets out to explain the Section on Mathematics, beginning with the names and characteristics of the ten decimal places:

->One, ten, hundred, thousand, ten thousand, hundred thousand, million,<-
->ten million, hundred million, and a billion; each place is ten times the preceding. (2) *<-

---

<note>*) In the first half of this verse, a break in the meter is observed. A reading such as "One, ten, ten-thousand, hundred, thousand" would likely be more grammatically correct.</note>

<summary>This page marks the transition from the introductory astronomical parameters to the mathematical foundations of Aryabhata's work, establishing the decimal place-value system and identifying the author's location as Kusumpura.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhata, Kusumpura, Mathematics, Decimal system, Astronomy, Paramesvara, Planetary motion, Brahman</keywords>

# Section on Mathematics
<note>original: "Gaṇitapāda"</note>

Thus, the meaning is clear. The implication is that any numbers not explicitly mentioned should be understood from other scientific treatises. He explains the name and nature of a square and its area in half a verse:

->**A square is an equilateral quadrilateral; its area is the product of two equal quantities.**<-

A figure that has four sides equal to one another and two diagonals that are also equal to one another is called an equilateral quadrilateral <note>literally "sama-caturasra"</note>. That specific figure is designated by the term "square" <note>original: "varga"</note>. And its area: the spatial measurement within that figure is also designated as the "square." The sum total of the area is called the square. Within a desired area, those equilateral quadrilaterals formed by four sides measuring one cubit <note>a "hasta" or forearm's length, roughly 18 inches</note> each are what we call the "area-units." It should be understood that even in figures such as triangles and circles, the term "area" refers to the number of square units identified by imagining cubit-sized squares within them. "The product of two equal quantities": the product or mutual multiplication of two equal, identical numbers is designated as a "square." This means that the act of "squaring" is the multiplication of a number by itself.

In the second half of the verse, he describes the cube:

->**The product of three equal quantities is a cube, as is a twelve-edged solid.**<-

The product or mutual multiplication of three identical numbers is designated as a "cube" <note>original: "ghana"</note>. This means that the act of "cubing" is the multiplication of a number by itself, and then multiplying that result by the original number again. Likewise, a twelve-edged figure is also designated as a "cube." This is what is meant: just as a pillar with a square base of one cubit in length and width has four horizontal edges at the bottom, so too it has four at the top, and four running vertically. Thus, a figure possessed of twelve edges is designated as a "cube." In this context:

<summary>This page defines the geometric and arithmetical properties of squares and cubes, explaining that a square is both a four-sided shape and the product of a number multiplied by itself.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, arithmetic, square, cube, area, multiplication, Aryabhatiya, spatial measurement</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical exposition of the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the definitions and algorithms for squaring and cubing numbers, as well as the extraction of square roots. It bridges classical definitions with methods found in later works like the Lilavati.</meta>

# In the Aryabhatiya

The product of two identical quantities and the product of three identical quantities—by these two definitions, the operations of squaring <term>vargakarma: the process of squaring a number</term> and cubing <term>ghanakarma: the process of cubing a number</term> are demonstrated. From this established rule, the alternative procedures mentioned by other authorities are written here <note>original: "tatha lilavatyam" — as found in the Lilavati, a famous 12th-century mathematical treatise by Bhaskara II</note>:

The product of two equals is called the square <term>kriti: another term for square</term>. Place the square of the last digit <note>the digit in the highest place value</note> first; then place twice the last digit multiplied by the others above their respective places. Having moved the remaining digits over and discarded the last, repeat the process.
This is the operation of squaring.

The product of three equals is declared the cube. Place the cube of the last digit; then three times the square of the last digit multiplied by the first; then three times the square of the first multiplied by the last; and finally the cube of the first.
All these, when summed according to their shifted positions, result in the cube. Having treated a pair of parts in this way, proceed to the next. Thus, the square and cube are repeatedly established, or this method may be performed starting from the first digit.
This is the operation of cubing. <margin>The "last" refers to the final positions of the roots of the cube established at that time. The "first" refers to the single place preceding it. "Pair of parts" means the part placed as the first and the part placed as the last. "Proceed to the next" means to apply the logic to the next set of digits.</margin> Regarding the squares and cubes of fractions:

-> The square of the numerator divided by the square of the denominator is the result of a fractional square. <-
-> Divide the cube of the numerator by the cube of the denominator for the result of a fractional cube. <-

By these two rules, the results of squares and cubes are to be calculated. Now, he describes the square root <term>vargamula: the root of a square</term>:

-> One should always divide the non-square place by twice the square root. <-
-> After subtracting the square from the square place, the quotient is the root in the next place. // 4 // <-

<summary>This page defines the mathematical operations of squaring and cubing, providing algorithmic steps for calculation as cited in the Lilavati, and introduces the rule for extracting square roots.</summary>
<keywords>squaring, cubing, square root, fractions, Aryabhatiya, Lilavati, Bhaskara II, arithmetic algorithms</keywords>
