<meta>This page continues the mathematical explanations of the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the extraction of square roots and cube roots.</meta>

# Mathematics Section
<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>Mathematics Section</header>

The odd places are designated as **square** <note>original: "varga"</note> places. The even places are designated as **non-square** <note>original: "avarga"</note> places. From the last square place, one should subtract the greatest possible square. The root of that subtracted square should be set down in one place. Then, having set that root down separately, divide the non-square place preceding that square place by twice that root. Subtract the square of the quotient obtained from the square place preceding that non-square place. Then, place that quotient—which is part of the root—in the line <note>original: "pankti"</note> as the next digit of the previously established root. Again, by that entire root-line, doubled and kept separately, divide the non-square place preceding the last subtracted square place. Subtract the square of the quotient obtained there from the square place preceding that non-square place, and place that quotient also in the root-line. One should continue this process until the end of the places. The resulting root-line is the square root itself. Division must always be performed. If no quotient is obtained at a certain step, then a zero should be placed in the root-line and the next non-square place should be divided. When a particular place is being divided, the places following it are considered its constituent parts. The quotient belongs to the next place. This means each obtained quotient should be placed in the line as the next digit.

He now describes the cube root:

->Divide the second non-cube place by thrice the square of the root of the cube.<-
->The square of the quotient, multiplied by thrice the previous root, is to be subtracted from the first non-cube place, and the cube of the quotient from the cube place. || 5 ||<-

The first place is designated as **cube** <note>original: "ghana"</note>. The second and third are designated as **non-cube** <note>original: "aghana"</note>. The fourth is a cube place. The fifth and sixth are non-cube places. Other places are also to be designated in this stated order. The division into square/non-square and cube/non-cube is not explicitly taught by the Teacher <note>referring to Aryabhata</note> here because it is established by mathematical logic. From the last cube place, subtract the greatest possible cube...

<sig>6</sig>

<summary>This page provides a step-by-step algorithmic description for extracting square roots and begins the procedure for cube roots using place-value notation. It establishes the nomenclature for numerical positions as square, non-square, cube, and non-cube.</summary>
<keywords>square root extraction, cube root, Aryabhata, Ganitapada, place value, Indian mathematics, algorithms</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of mathematical operations from the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the extraction of cube roots, following the discussion of square roots on previous pages.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

One should subtract. Again, having placed its <term>root</term><note>mūla: the base or root of a number</note> in one place, and having squared that root and multiplied it by three, one should divide the result into the second <term>non-cubic place</term><note>adhana-sthāna: in Indian mathematics, digits are categorized into cubic (ghana) and non-cubic (adhana) positions to facilitate root extraction</note> which follows the cleared <term>cubic place</term><note>ghana-sthāna</note>. This means one should divide the second non-cubic place. 

Then, having squared the quotient obtained and multiplied it by three, and further multiplying it by the previously established <term>root-result</term><note>mūla-phala</note>, one should subtract this from the first non-cubic place which precedes the divided place. Then, subtract the <term>cube</term><note>ghana</note> of that quotient from the cubic place that follows the cleared digits. 

Again, that result, called the <term>cube root</term><note>ghana-mūlākhya</note>, should be placed in a row at the beginning of the previously established <term>cube-root-result</term>. Again, by the separate row of roots, squared and multiplied by three, divide the non-cubic place following the cleared cubic place. Having squared the quotient and multiplied it by three, and having multiplied it by the previously established <term>row of roots</term><note>mūla-paṅkti</note>, subtract it from the first non-cubic place preceding the divided place. 

Subtract the <term>cube</term> of the quotient from the cubic place following the cleared place. That result, called the <term>cube root</term>, should be placed in the previously established <term>cube row</term><note>ghana-paṅkti</note>. One should continue this process until the end of the digit places. The resulting <term>cube row</term> is the cube root. 

Regarding fractions: "The <term>cube root</term> of the numerator's cube quantity is divided by the <term>root of the denominator</term><note>cheda-mūla</note>." This is how it should be understood. Similarly, for the <term>square root</term> of a fraction, one should divide by three times the <term>square of the root</term><note>mūla-varga</note> of the cube. Thus, the first <term>clearing of the cube</term><note>ghana-śodhana: the process of extracting the root</note> is described. 

In the case of the <term>square root</term>, the first <term>clearing of the square</term><note>varga-śodhana</note> occurs by dividing by twice the <term>square root</term>. The <term>operation of cubing</term><note>ghana-karma</note> is utilized in worldly mathematics, but not in the sections on <term>Calculation of Time and the Celestial Sphere</term><note>Kālakriyā and Gola: the astronomical sections of Aryabhata's work</note>. 

He describes the area of a <term>triangular figure</term><note>tribhuja-kṣetra</note> in the first half of the next verse.

It also notes that while these calculations are vital for general mathematics, they are less frequently used in the astronomical sections of the text.</summary>

# Section on Mathematics

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mathematics</header>

->The area of a triangle is the product of the perpendicular and half the base.<-

The "perpendicular" <term>samadalakoti: literally 'the side that divides into equal parts'</term> of a triangular figure refers to the altitude. The side at the bottom of the triangle is called the base. The straight line drawn from the top vertex to the base is called the perpendicular. This perpendicular serves as a shared side for the two right-angled triangles formed on either side of it within the main triangle. Therefore, it is called the "equal-dividing side." The "side" associated with that perpendicular is the segment of the base adjacent to it. Thus, half of the two sides <note>referring to the base segments</note> is half the base. The product of half the base and the perpendicular is the area of the triangular figure.

The second half of the verse describes the volume of a solid triangle <note>a tetrahedron or pyramid with a triangular base</note>:

->Half the product of the altitude and that area is the volume of a six-edged solid. || 6 ||<-

The "altitude" <term>urdhvabhuja: literally 'upward arm'</term> is the height at the center of the figure. "That" refers to the previously calculated area. Half the product of the altitude and the area is the "solid," meaning the volume. This specific figure is "six-edged" <note>original: "shadasri"</note>. A six-edged solid is a figure that is triangular on all sides <note>a tetrahedron</note>. 

The method for finding the perpendicular in a triangle is known through this rule: "The sum of the two sides multiplied by their difference and divided by the base; the result is added to and subtracted from the base and divided by two to find the two segments of the base <term>abadha</term>. The square root of the difference between the squares of a side and its corresponding segment is the perpendicular." This is also established through logical proof. The proof is demonstrated in the *Commentary on the Lilavati* <note>A famous mathematical treatise by Bhaskara II</note>. In this context, the square root of the difference between the squares of the slant height and half the base becomes the vertical arm <note>altitude</note>.

The first half of the next verse describes the area of a circle:

->Half of the uniform circumference multiplied by half the diameter is exactly the area of a circle.<-

Half of the "uniform circumference" <note>the perimeter of a perfect circle</note> multiplied by half the diameter <note>the radius</note> is the area of the circular figure. By using the word "exactly," the author indicates that this specific method is the accurate way to calculate the area of a circle.

<meta>catchword: volume of a sphere-</meta>

<summary>This page defines the geometric formulas for calculating the area of a triangle, the volume of a triangular pyramid (tetrahedron), and the area of a circle. It references the Lilavati for complex proofs regarding altitudes and base segments.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, triangle area, tetrahedron volume, circle area, perpendicular, altitude, circumference, diameter, Aryabhatiya, Lilavati</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical commentary on the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the geometry of spheres and trapezoids.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>On the Aryabhatiya</header>

He states the result of that <note>referring to the area of a circle mentioned in the previous verse</note> in the second half of the verse:

->That <insert>area of a circle</insert> multiplied by its own square root is the volume of a sphere, exactly. || 7 ||<-

That area of a circle, when multiplied by its own square root <note>original: "nijamūlena"</note>, becomes the volume of a sphere. "Exactly" means it is the precise value. Now, he describes the measurement of the vertical line segments from the intersection of the diagonals in a non-rectangular quadrilateral <note>specifically a trapezoid</note> and its area:

->The two sides <insert>base and top</insert> multiplied by the height and divided by their sum are the two "lines of fall."<-
->The area is known by the height multiplied by half the sum of the widths. || 8 ||<-

The "height" <note>original: "āyāma"</note> is the perpendicular. The "two sides" are the base and the face <note>the top side</note>. When the height is multiplied by the base and the face separately and divided by the sum of the base and face, the two results are the "lines of fall" <note><term>pātarekhe</term>: the vertical distances from the intersection of the diagonals to the base and the top</note>. This means the portion of the perpendicular from the intersection of the diagonals to the base, and the portion of the perpendicular from the intersection to the top. In that context, the value obtained from the base is the distance between the base and the diagonal intersection, and the value from the top is the distance between the top and the diagonal intersection. When the height is multiplied by half the sum of the widths—that is, half the sum of the base and the top—it becomes the area of the figure. This is to be understood. This rule applies to a "level-perpendicular" figure <note>an isosceles trapezoid or a trapezoid with equal heights</note>, but not to one with unequal perpendiculars. In the latter case, there would be doubt as to which of the two perpendiculars should be used here. If the problem-setter does not specify a "level-perpendicular" figure, then for a quadrilateral with equal perpendiculars, one should treat the base minus the top as the base of a triangle, determine the sides, and from that height find the segments of the base. The square root of the sum of the squares of that height and the segment is the diagonal. In a level-perpendicular figure, the sum of the top and the other side is less than the sum of the base and the other side. By this, the level-perpendicular trapezoid, its diagonals, and its properties are to be known. Regarding the mentioned and unmentioned figures...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical section (Ganitapada), moving from specific area calculations to a general rule for all shapes and the relationship between a circle's diameter and circumference.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
# Section on Mathematics

The first half of the verse explains the method for finding the area of all figures.

->For all figures, having determined the two sides, their product is the area.<-

One should determine the sides of both the mentioned and unmentioned figures. This means determining the sides consisting of the length and the breadth. <note>The author suggests that any shape can be reduced to a rectangle for calculation.</note> Having established these through logical proof, their product should be calculated. That result is the area of the figure. In the case of a square or a cube, the sides are equal, so no further determination is needed. In a triangle, the perpendicular is the length and half the base is the breadth. In a solid sphere, the square root of the area of the circular cross-section is the height. In a non-equal quadrilateral with parallel sides <term>samalambe: a trapezoid</term>, the perpendicular is the length and half the sum of the base and the top is the breadth. In a non-equal quadrilateral with non-parallel sides, one should treat the diagonal as the base and find the two perpendiculars of the two triangles on its sides. There, the sum of the two perpendiculars is the length and half the diagonal is the breadth. In this way, the length and breadth should be imagined everywhere using one's own intelligence. It should be understood that the presentation of mathematics here is incidental to its use in the sections on Time-reckoning and the Celestial Sphere.

The second half of the verse explains the knowledge of the chord equal to the radius in the circumference of a circle.

> The chord of the sixth part of the circumference is equal to the semi-diameter. || 9 ||

The chord of the sixth part of the circumference—which corresponds to two signs of the zodiac <note>original: "rashidvaya"; 60 degrees</note>—is equal to the semi-diameter or radius. Here, "chord" refers to the full chord of two signs, not the half-chord found in the sine tables. This means the half-chord of one sign <note>30 degrees</note> is equal to half of the radius.

To determine the diameter from the circumference of a desired circle, or the circumference from the diameter using the Rule of Three, the author shows the values for the "result" and the "standard":

> Add four to one hundred, multiply by eight, and add sixty-two thousand.
> This is the approximate circumference of a circle with a diameter of twenty thousand. || 10 ||

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Ganitapada section of the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the calculation of the ratio between a circle's diameter and its circumference (Pi) and the derivation of sine tables.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
# In the Aryabhatiya

Add four to one hundred, multiply by eight, and add sixty-two thousand. This is the approximate circumference of a circle with a diameter of twenty thousand. <note>This yields the value 62,832 / 20,000 = 3.1416.</note> The author uses the word "approximate" <note>original: "asannah"</note> to mean that it is not an exact, remainderless value. "Circumference" <note>term: parinahah — the perimeter or boundary of a circle</note> refers to the periphery. Regarding the circumference of a circle, it is possible for only one of the two—either the circumference or the diameter—to be a whole number; the other will inevitably have a fractional part. Here, the circumference is stated as the number 62,832 <note>original: "dagnagnyahidvisat-sankhyah" using the Katapayadi system of notation</note>.

All the half-chords <note>term: ardhajya — the precursor to the modern sine function</note> mentioned in the *Gitika* section can be logically derived once the half-chord of one sign <note>30 degrees</note> and the radius are known. To achieve this, the author has demonstrated the full chord of one-sixth of the circumference <note>the chord of 60 degrees, which equals the radius</note> and provided the ratio between the circumference and the diameter as the means of calculation. 

While discussing the half-chord of one sign, the demonstration of the full chord of two signs <note>60 degrees</note> is given to show that in some cases, one calculates the full chord first and then halves it to find the half-chord. To derive the diameter from the circumference, the "Rule of Three" <note>term: trairasika — a mathematical proportion: if A yields B, what does C yield?</note> is applied as follows: 

If a circumference of 62,832 (as stated) has a diameter of 20,000, then what is the diameter for a circumference measured in the minutes of a circle <note>21,600 minutes</note>? This calculation yields the diameter of the zodiacal circle. From that, the value of the radius <note>term: trijya — the "sine of three signs" or the radius of the standard circle</note> is obtained. Thus, the diameter is also logically established. That logic is shown in great detail in the *Siddhanta-dipika*, a commentary on the *Mahabhaskariya*. The logic for deriving the chord of one sign is demonstrated here:

->From the center, draw the radius in two directions toward the North and East lines.<-
->From the ends of those, draw two lines to the edge of the circumference, parallel to the East and North lines.<-
->The one extending East is considered the "cosine" and the other the "sine."<-
->By these, the Northeast quadrant of the sphere is divided into three parts.<-
->The distance from the end of the cosine to the end of the East line, and from the end of the sine to the North...<-

<page-num>27</page-num>
# Section on Mathematics

->The two lines are the base and the perpendicular; these are the previous perpendiculars and bases.<-
->They are equal to the radius; then, of the squares of those two,<-
->joined with the square of their own versed sine <note>original: "utkramajya"; the versed sine is 1 - cos(x)</note>, the two square roots<-
->are indeed the two full chords <note>original: "samastajya"</note> of their own two arcs.<-
->And those two full chords belong to the beginning and end portions of a quadrant of the sphere.<-
->The difference between the long and short bases, and likewise for the perpendiculars—<-
->the square root of the sum of their squares is the full chord of the middle portion.<-
->Because of the equality of the three full chords here, the three segments are equal.<-
->Therefore, it is certain that they belong to a single chord measured by the radius.<-

Thus ends the verse. He now demonstrates the method of reasoning in the calculation of sines:

->Divide a quadrant of the circumference of a circle by means of a triangle and a quadrilateral.<-
->The sines of equal arcs <note>original: "samachapa-jyardhani"</note> are as desired in a circle of a given radius. || 11 ||<-

One should divide the quadrant of the circumference of a circle. This means one should mark it with lines devised through logical reasoning. From the triangle produced there, certain semi-chords <note>term: "jyardha" (half-chord or sine); the text uses this to define the trigonometric sine function</note> are established. This means they are established by virtue of the components of the triangle. Others are established from the quadrilateral produced there; meaning they are established by virtue of the components of the quadrilateral. "Sines of equal arcs" means the sines of half-arcs that are equal to one another. When the radius <note>term: "vishkambhardha" (half-diameter)</note> is established, the others are established. "As desired" means that among the twenty-four semi-sines mentioned in the *Gitika* section, those which are desired are established. This means all of them are established. In this way, the sines of the arc-segments <note>original: "pindajyardhani"</note> are established.

<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

Those <term>makhya</term> <note>A mnemonic system used by Aryabhata to enumerate sine table values</note> and the following values are formed by subtracting the preceding ones from the subsequent ones. Here it is explained:

->In a circle, a portion of the circumference is shaped like a bow and is called the full arc.<-
->The chord connecting its two ends is called the full chord of that arc.<-
->Half of that is the half-chord <note>original: "ardhajya"; this is the ancestor of the modern sine function</note>, and its arc is half of that full arc.<-
->The sine and cosine are always half-chords, as are their respective arcs.<-
->The traversed and the remaining portions of a quadrant are the sine-arc and cosine-arc.<-
->Their sines are measured from the ends of the two directional axes to the desired point on the circle.<-
->The distance from the half-chord to the circumference is the <term>versed sine</term> <note>original: "utkramaguna"; the vertical distance between the chord and the arc's peak</note>.<-
->The radius minus either the sine or cosine results in the versed sine of the other.<-
->The square root of the sum of the squares of the half-chord and the versed sine is the full chord of that arc.<-
->The full chord's half is the half-chord of half that arc.<-
->A triangle is formed here by the half-chord, the versed sine, and the full chord.<-
->A quadrilateral is formed by the sine, the cosine, and the two segments of the radius.<-
->In this context, half of the full chord is designated as the "sine to be calculated."<-
->In the quadrilateral, either the cosine or the sine is the "sine to be calculated."<-
->The square root of the difference between the squares of the radius and the sine is the cosine, and vice versa.<-
->Having understood all this, one should contemplate the derivation of the sines.<-

# Section on Mathematics

When the arc measures three signs <note>90 degrees; a 'sign' or rashi is 30 degrees</note>, the sine of the arc is equal to the radius. In that case, the versed sine <note>the distance from the center of the arc to the chord</note> is also equal to the radius, because the cosine <note>the horizontal component</note> is absent.

Therefore, the square root of the sum of the squares of two radii is the full chord. The sine of a three-sign arc creates a triangle there.

By using the full chord, the half-chord, and the versed sine, the half of the full chord is found. That is the twelfth sine table entry, corresponding to an arc of 22.5 degrees <note>literally "half of thirty-two" or 22.5 degrees in the traditional 24-part division of a quadrant</note>.

From that, and by its inverse, the full chord is produced again. From those, a triangle is formed; the half of the full chord is the sine of half of sixty <note>30 degrees</note>.

From that, the cosine should be calculated. When the sine and cosine are placed together, a quadrilateral is formed by those two segments of the directional lines.

In that case, the eighteenth sine <note>67.5 degrees</note> is the cosine; this should be considered everywhere. Whether it is a quadrilateral or a triangle, the sine depends upon it.

From the eighteenth and the sixth sines, by means of the full chord, the ninth and the third sines are derived through the relationship of the sine and cosine.

From those two, the fifteenth and the twenty-first sines are established. Half of the radius is the eighth sine <note>30 degrees</note>, and its cosine is the sixteenth sine <note>60 degrees</note>.

By the method of the full chord applied to the eighth sine, the fourth sine is found. Then, by the power of the cosine, the twentieth sine is found, and from there, the full chord follows.

> From the tenth <note>sine segment</note>, by the power of the base, the fourteenth is derived.

> Then, by the power of the perpendicular, the seventeenth is found, and then the fifth.

Thus, he explains the method for deriving the sine-segments mentioned in the *Gitika* from the first sine-segment:

->**The second segment is the first half-chord of the arc, diminished by the quotient of itself.**<-
->**The remaining segments are diminished by the quotients obtained by dividing the preceding sum of sines by the first sine.**<-

The half-chord of the arc: the prescribed half-chords of the arc are those such as *makhi* <note>a mnemonic code for the first sine value, 225</note> and so on. The "second half-segment" refers to the second sine-segment. After establishing the first sine-segment, whatever desired sine-segment is established next is called the "second." It means the one immediately following the one already calculated. By whatever specific numerical values the first half-chord of the arc is diminished...

# Section on Mathematics

The specific desired sine difference is called the second. These are subtracted by those respective values. When many values are to be calculated, the first sine differences <note>The first sine difference is the sine of the first arc segment, traditionally 225 minutes in Indian astronomy</note> are placed in many positions and then reduced by the parts of that first sine. By the word "that," the collection of preceding sine differences, starting from the first up to the one immediately before the desired sine, is meant. From that collection of past sine differences, one should subtract the results obtained by dividing that sum by the first sine. These remainders become the subsequent sine differences.

This is what is said: First, establish the first sine difference. To find the next, subtract the preceding sine difference (the second to be calculated) from it, and keep the remainder in one place. Then, divide the sum of the preceding sine differences by the first sine; the resulting quotient, added to the previously stored remainder, should be subtracted from the first sine. The result remaining there is the subsequent sine difference.

**Example:**
To find the second sine difference from the first: the preceding sine difference is *Makhi* <note>original: "मखि" (Makhi) — an alphabetic code representing the number 225</note>. Since there is no deficiency before it, the first quotient is zero. Again, for the desired value, the sum of preceding sine differences is just *Makhi*. Dividing that by the first sine gives one. Subtracting that from the first sine difference, the remaining second sine difference is *Bhakhi* <note>original: "भखि" (Bhakhi) — representing 224</note>. 

Again, for the third desired sine difference: the preceding sine difference is *Bhakhi*, which is one less than the first. The sum of the preceding sine differences for this desired value is equal to *Makhi* and *Bhakhi* combined. Dividing that sum by the first sine gives two. Subtracting this two and the previous remainder of one from *Makhi*, the remaining third sine difference is *Phakhi* <note>original: "फखि" (Phakhi) — representing 222</note>. Others are to be calculated in this same way.

The phrase "by those respective" is used because there are many quotients when many sines are to be calculated. The word "subtracted" is used because the first sine difference is set down many times for the various calculations. The word "remainders" is used because there are many subsequent sine differences to be found.

Now, he describes the method for constructing circles and other figures:

->A circle is produced by rotation; a triangle and a quadrilateral are produced by their two diagonals.<-

<note>The author here transitions from trigonometry to basic geometric construction, defining a circle through the motion of a compass and polygons through their internal dimensions.</note>

<summary>This page explains the recursive mathematical formula used to derive a table of sine differences (jya-khandas) and introduces basic geometric constructions for circles and polygons.</summary>
<keywords>trigonometry, sine table, sine differences, Makhi, Bhakhi, Phakhi, geometry, circle construction, Aryabhata, Indian mathematics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Aryabhata's mathematical treatise, specifically focusing on the practical construction of geometric figures and the leveling of the ground.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>On the Aryabhatiya</header>

-> Level ground is achieved with water; verticality and depth with the plumb-line. || 13 || <-

A **circle** <term>vritta</term> is to be achieved by rotation with a **compass-instrument** <term>karkata-yantra; literally "crab-instrument" due to its two legs</term>. This is explained as follows: Having obtained a straight rod, tie it firmly with a loop at the upper neck portion. From the lower tip up to the neck, split it into two needles and make their tips sharp. Thus, a downward-facing **compass-instrument** is formed. Then, by placing a small peg between the two needles, one can make the compass open its mouth. By moving the internal peg up or down, set the mouth of the compass equal to the desired radius of the circle. Fix one needle-tip at the center-point of the intended circle and the other tip at the circumference-point, then rotate the compass. That becomes the desired circle.

Furthermore, a **triangular figure** <term>tribhuja-kshetra</term> and a **quadrilateral figure** <term>caturbhuja-kshetra</term> are achieved by means of their diagonals <term>karna; literally "ear" or "hypotenuse/diagonal"</term>. This means both of these are achieved using their respective diagonals. Even in a **triangle**, one side is imagined as a **diagonal**, because in a **quadrilateral** arising from two triangles, that side functions as a diagonal. In that process, first place a rod equal to the diagonal on level ground. Place one rod equal to one of the other two sides at one end of the diagonal, and place the other rod at the other end of the diagonal, then join the tips of these "side-rods" together. That becomes the desired triangle.

In a **quadrilateral** as well, first place one of the two diagonals. On one side of it, place two sides as in a triangle, and on the other side, place the other two sides like a triangle. Then place the second diagonal in its proper position. Thus, a **quadrilateral** marked by two diagonals is formed. Here, by fixing one diagonal, the other diagonal is also constrained.

"Level ground is achieved with water." The evenness of the ground is to be achieved with water. This means the knowledge of whether the ground is level or uneven is obtained through water. This is explained as follows: Having made the ground appear level to the eye-line, draw a circle there, and outside of it, at a distance of two fingers...

It also introduces the method of using water to ensure a perfectly level surface for astronomical observations.</summary>

# Section on Mathematics
<page-num>33</page-num>
->Mathematics Section<-

Having drawn two circles separated by three inches, or any other distance, one should dig out the space between the two circumferences all around to create a circular trench and fill that trench with water. If the water is level with the ground all around, then the ground is perfectly level. Where the water appears low, the ground is high; where the water appears high, the ground is low. Verticality is determined by the plumb-line alone. A string with a heavy object tied to its end and suspended is called a <term>plumb-line</term> <note>original: "avalambaka"</note>. By its means, the vertical position of a <term>gnomon</term> <note>original: "shanku"; a vertical pillar or rod used in ancient astronomy to cast shadows</note> and other objects is to be known. Indeed, a gnomon is in a straight vertical position when its base and tip are aligned vertically. To demonstrate the desired circle, he describes the method for finding its radius:

> Having added the square of the height of the gnomon to the square of its shadow,
> the square root of that sum is the radius of its own circle. || 14 ||

"Square root" <note>original: "vargamula"</note> simply means the root. One should add the square of the measure of the chosen gnomon to the square of its shadow and take the square root. That root becomes the radius of the circle known as "its own circle" at that desired time. The vertical circle that passes through the tip of the gnomon and the center of the shadow's end is called "its own circle." Just as the circle of the radius <note>the celestial sphere</note> passes through the tip of the "Great Gnomon," this should be understood in the same way. He now describes the calculation of the shadow produced by the height of a lamp relative to the gnomon:

> The distance between the gnomon and the lamp-post, multiplied by the gnomon,
> and divided by the difference between the heights of the lamp-post and the gnomon,
> the result is the shadow of the gnomon measured from its own base. || 15 ||

The "gnomon" is the chosen gnomon. The "arm" <note>original: "bhuja"</note> is the lamp-post. The "interval" is the ground distance between them. Multiply that distance by the height of the gnomon. Divide that product by the difference between the heights of the gnomon and the lamp-post (the height of the lamp minus the height of the gnomon).

<sig>9</sig>
<vocab>gnomon, shadow, radius, mathematics section, square root, plumb-line, lamp-post, gnomon-height</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstrations from the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the geometry of shadows and gnomons. It follows the previous discussion on proportional triangles.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

The result obtained there is the shadow of that gnomon <note>A gnomon is a vertical rod used in ancient astronomy to measure shadows; the Sanskrit term is "Shanku."</note>. It is the measurement of the shadow originating from its own base.

An example:

->The height of the lamp is 32 digits; the gnomon is 12 digits.<-
->The ground distance between them is 10 digits; tell me the length of the shadow here.<-

Height of the lamp: 32. Height of the gnomon: 12. The ground distance between them: 10. The difference between the vertical arms <term>Bhuja: the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term> is the lamp height minus the gnomon height: 20. The resulting shadow measurement is 6. To prove this by the Rule of Three <note>A mathematical method to find a fourth value from three known values based on proportions.</note>, one should extend the hypotenuse line <term>Karnasutra: the diagonal or hypotenuse</term> from the top of the lamp, passing through the head of the gnomon, until it reaches the ground. Here, two triangles are formed. In the first of these, the portion remaining after subtracting the gnomon's height from the lamp's height at its base is the vertical arm. The horizontal side <term>Koti: the horizontal side or base of a right-angled triangle</term> is equal to the ground distance between the gnomon and the lamp. Then, by asking "what is the horizontal side for the gnomon's vertical arm?", the horizontal side representing the distance from the gnomon's base is established. That indeed is the shadow of that gnomon.

Next, he describes the method for finding the height of the lamp-arm and the distance between the lamp's base and the tip of the shadow, using two shadows from two identical gnomons placed at two different positions and the distance between the two shadow-tips:

---

->The distance between the tips of the shadows multiplied by a shadow and divided by the difference [between the shadows] is the horizontal distance.<-
->That horizontal distance multiplied by the gnomon and divided by the [same] shadow is the vertical arm [the lamp's height]. || 16 ||<-

Multiply the distance between the positions of two shadows of a gnomon—placed along a single line from the lamp—by one of those shadows. Divide this by the "lesser" value, which is the difference between the two shadows. The result obtained is the horizontal distance <term>Koti</term>. This refers to the ground distance between the lamp's base and the tip of whichever shadow was used as the multiplier. That horizontal distance, when multiplied by the gnomon's height and divided by the shadow used as the multiplier, becomes the vertical arm <note>The height of the light source or lamp.</note>.

# Section on Mathematics
<page-num>35</page-num>
->The Base and the Perpendicular<-

When divided, it becomes the base. This refers to the height of the light source. An example:

->The two shadows are equal to ten and sixteen units respectively.<-
->The distance between the tips of the shadows is twelve.<-
->Tell me the height of the light source and its horizontal distance.<-

The first shadow is 10. The second shadow is 16. The ground distance between the tips of the shadows is 12. Here, using the first shadow, the calculated horizontal distance <term>perpendicular (koti)</term> to the light is 20. The height of the light <term>base (bhuja)</term> is 24. Alternatively, using the second shadow, the horizontal distance is 32, and the height of the light remains 24. Indeed, the tip of the shadow is the center of the shadow-hypotenuse circle. Therefore, the calculation of the perpendicular starts from the tip of the shadow. For a <term>gnomon (shanku)</term> <note>a vertical pillar used to cast shadows for measurement</note> located at the base of the light, there is no shadow. Moving outward from there, the shadow increases proportionately. In this case, the <term>rule of three</term> <note>a mathematical method to find a fourth value from three known ratios</note> is applied: If a ground distance equal to the difference between the shadows is obtained by the difference in shadow lengths, then what ground distance is obtained by the desired shadow length? This gives the distance between the shadow tip and the base of the light. If the height of the gnomon is obtained from the perpendicular known as the desired shadow, then what height is obtained from the total horizontal distance? This gives the height of the light source.

The author explains the calculation of the hypotenuse from the base and the perpendicular with a half-verse:

> The square of the base and the square of the perpendicular is the square of the hypotenuse.

This means the sum of the squares of the base and the perpendicular is the square of the hypotenuse <note>This is the Pythagorean theorem</note>.

The author explains the calculation of the chord when the <term>arrow (shara)</term> <note>the sagitta, or the height of an arc</note> is known with the latter half-verse:

> In a circle, the product of the two arrows is certainly the square of the half-chord of that arc. || 17 ||

In a circular figure, there are two "arrows" extending to either side from the center of any full chord of a desired arc. Their product is indeed the square of the half-chord <term>sine (jya)</term> of the two previously mentioned arc segments. The <term>versed sine (utkramajya)</term> of the desired arc is the first arrow. The total diameter minus that is the second arrow. The sum of the perpendicular and the hypotenuse is here—

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical commentary on the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the geometry of intersecting circles and the calculation of segments.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

The greater <term>Shara</term><note>The "Shara" (literally "arrow") refers to the sagitta—the height of an arc or the distance from the midpoint of a chord to the arc itself.</note> is increased. The difference between them is the lesser Shara. The square of that is the difference of the two squares. This is the reasoning applied here.

When two circles intersect, he explains how to calculate the two sagittas on either side from the center of the <term>Jiva</term><note>The "Jiva" is the chord of the arc, representing the line connecting the two points where the circles intersect.</note> which extends from one intersection of the circumferences to the other:

->Subtract the <term>Grasa</term> from each of the two diameters; multiply these by the Grasa separately.<-
->Divide these by the sum of the diameters minus the Grasa; the results are the sagittas of the intersection, respectively. || 18 || * )<-

The interval located in the middle of the parts of the two circumferences that have entered into one another is called the <term>Grasa</term><note>Literally "the bite" or "eclipse," referring to the overlapping portion of two intersecting circles.</note>. The two circles (diameters) are diminished by that Grasa. "Separately" means individually. Having multiplied them individually by the measure of the Grasa, one should divide them separately. He states the result while indicating the divisor (which was previously unstated) in an explanatory form: "divided by the sum of the diameters minus the Grasa, the results are the sagittas of the intersection." When the two quantities are divided by the sum of the two circles minus the Grasa, the two sagittas of the intersection are obtained. This means the two sagittas are located on either side of the center of the entire chord passing through the two points of intersection. "Respectively" means: the result obtained from the smaller circle is the sagitta of the larger circle, and the result from the larger circle is the sagitta of the smaller circle.

An example:

->One circle measures 40, the other measures 16.<-
->The eclipsed portion (Grasa) is 4. State their two <term>Sharas</term> separately.<-

One circle is 40. The other is 16. The Grasa is 4. The results obtained are: the sagitta of the small circle is 3, and the sagitta of the large circle is 1.

He now describes the calculation of the <term>Shredhiphala</term><note>The "Shredhiphala" refers to the sum of an arithmetic progression or series.</note>:

->The desired number of terms minus one, halved, plus the preceding terms, multiplied by the common difference, plus the first term, is the middle term.<-

---

<note>*) In the Prakashika commentary, the reading is "obtained by the sum minus the Grasa." However, the Teacher (Aryabhata) likely wrote "divided by the sum minus the Grasa."</note>

<summary>This page provides the geometric formula for calculating the sagittas (heights of the arcs) of two intersecting circles based on their diameters and the length of their overlap. It also introduces the rule for calculating the sum of an arithmetic series.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical commentary on arithmetic progressions, specifically expanding on the formulas for finding middle terms and sums of series.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>Section on Mathematics</header>
<sig>10</sig>

## Or, the desired number of terms multiplied by the sum of the first and last terms divided by two. || 19 ||

This rule <note>Sutra 19 from Aryabhata's Ganitapada</note> is demonstrative of many mathematical principles. Therefore, it should be applied in various ways. In calculating the "middle value" <note>the average value of the series</note> and the "total value" <note>the sum of the series</note>, the phrase "with the preceding" should be set aside and the rule applied as follows: The desired number of terms <term>Ishta-pada: the count of terms in the sequence</term> minus one, halved, multiplied by the common difference <term>Chaya: the amount by which the sequence increases</term>, and added to the initial value <term>Mukha: the first term of the series</term>, results in the **middle value**. That middle value, multiplied by the desired number of terms, results in the **total value** <note>the sum of the progression</note>.

Here is the formula: The desired terms minus one, halved, multiplied by the common difference and added to the first term is the middle value. The middle value multiplied by the desired number of terms is the total value.

However, for calculating the value of any specific desired term—such as the last, the penultimate, or any other—the application is: "the preceding multiplied by the common difference, with the first term." The terms that have passed before the desired term are called "the preceding" <note>original: *purva*</note>. The number of preceding terms multiplied by the common difference and added to the first term results in the value of that specific desired term. Here is the formula: The number of preceding terms multiplied by the common difference plus the first term shall be the value of the desired term.

Furthermore, for calculating the sum of a specific sub-section of terms within the series, the word "middle" is set aside and the rule is applied sequentially up to "multiplied by the desired." The number of terms in the desired sub-section minus one, halved, added to the number of terms preceding that sub-section, multiplied by the common difference, added to the first term, and finally multiplied by the number of terms in that sub-section, results in the total sum of those specific internal terms. Here is the formula: The desired sub-section minus one, halved, plus the preceding, multiplied by the common difference, plus the first term, multiplied by the desired sub-section, is the result produced by those internal terms. In this context, the word "desired" refers to the count of terms within that specific sub-section.

### Example

> The first term is 5, the common difference is 7, and the total number of terms is 17.
> 
> State separately the middle value, the penultimate term, the eighth term, and the sum of the first three terms.

First term: 5. Common difference: 7. Total terms: 17. Here, to find the middle value, the desired number is 17. From this desired...

It concludes by introducing a practical word problem to demonstrate these algebraic rules.</summary>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical commentary on the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on arithmetic progressions (series) and the derivation of the number of terms (n) from the total sum.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

By the rule "less one" etc., the middle term is established as 61. This, multiplied by the desired number of terms, seventeen, results in 1037. This is the total sum <note>The formula used here is Sum = Middle Term × n</note>. To find the value of the penultimate term, the desired term is 16. The term preceding it is 15. This, multiplied by the common difference and added to the first term, equals 110. This is the value of the sixteenth term. Now, to find the sum of three terms starting from the eighth: the desired number of terms is 3. This, minus one and halved, is 1. Added to the seven terms preceding the eighth, it becomes 8. Multiplied by the common difference and added to the first term, it is 61. Multiplied by the three intermediate terms, it becomes 183. This is the sum of the three terms starting from the eighth.

The author now states another method for finding the total sum, following a specific traditional rule: -> **Or, the sum of the first and last terms, multiplied by half the number of terms.** <- The sum of the first term and the last term, multiplied by half the number of terms, results in the total sum. Regarding the phrase "with the first term and the middle," it should be understood as "the middle term including the first." In cases where there is no single middle term <note>i.e., when the number of terms is even</note>, the middle value is half the sum of the two terms flanking the center. Now, he describes how to find the number of terms <term>gaccha</term>:

-> **The number of terms is found from eight times the common difference multiplied by the sum, added to the square of the difference between twice the first term and the common difference; the square root of this, minus twice the first term, divided by the common difference, plus one, and then halved.** <- (Verse 20)

The "given sum" is the subject here. From the total sum multiplied by eight and further multiplied by the common difference (called <term>caya</term>); added to the square of the difference between twice the first term (<term>mukha</term>) and the common difference; the square root of that result is taken. From that root, subtract twice the first term. Divide this by the common difference. To the resulting quotient, add one, and half of that is the number of terms <term>gaccha</term>. 

In the previous example, the given sum was 1037. This, multiplied by eight and by the common difference of seven, equals 58072. Twice the first term is 10. The common difference is 7. Added to the square of their difference (which is 9), it becomes 58081. The square root resulting from this is 241. Subtracting twice the first term (10) leaves 231. This, divided by its own common difference (7) and increased by one, is 34. <note>The final step of halving 34 gives 17, which was the original number of terms.</note>

<page-num>31</page-num>
# Section on Mathematics

The halved value is 10. This is the number of terms <note>Sanskrit: *gaccha*; the count of terms in a series</note>. Now, he explains how to calculate the sum of the sums of numbers starting with one and increasing by one:

> The product of three terms starting from the number of terms, each increased by one, divided by six, is the sum of the series. Or, it is the cube of the number of terms plus one, minus itself, divided by six. || 21 ||

The sum of the series <note>Sanskrit: *citidhana*; literally "the wealth of the pile," referring to the total sum of a series</note> is calculated here for an accumulation that starts with one and increases by one. This refers to the "sum of the sums" <note>This is the formula for triangular numbers: n(n+1)(n+2)/6</note>. "The product of three terms starting from the number of terms, each increased by one": the first value is the number of terms (*gaccha*). The second value is the number of terms plus one. The third value is the second value plus one. The product of these three values divided by six is the sum of the series. This is the sum of the sums of numbers starting from one.

"Or, the cube of the number of terms plus one, minus itself": Alternatively, take the number of terms plus one and cube it. Subtract that same "number of terms plus one" from the cube, and divide by six; this results in the sum of the series.

**Example:** If the sum of the first five numbers is sought, the number of terms is 5. This is the first value. This increased by one is 6; this is the second value. This also increased by one is 7; this is the third value. The product of these three is 210. Divided by six, it is 35. This is the sum of the series.

**Alternatively:** The number of terms plus one is 6. Its cube is 216. This, minus its root (the number of terms plus one, which is 6), is 210. Divided by six, it is 35. This is the sum of the series.

Now he describes the sum of squares and cubes:

> The sixth part of the product of three terms—the number of terms, that plus one, and twice the number plus one—is the sum of the squares. The square of the sum of the original series is the sum of the cubes. || 22 ||

The word "term" <note>Sanskrit: *pada*</note> is used everywhere synonymously with "number of terms" <note>Sanskrit: *gaccha*</note>. The first value is the number of terms plus one. The second is the number of terms plus one plus the number of terms <note>This equals 2n+1</note>. One should multiply these three in sequence. Of such a triple product...

<header>On the Aryabhatiya</header>

One-sixth of the product <note>of three specific terms: n, n+1, and 2n+1</note> is the sum of the series of squares. This means the total sum of the squares of natural numbers.
The square of the sum of a series is the sum of the series of cubes. The square of the sum of the series from one onwards is the sum of the series of cubes; this means the total sum of the cubes from one onwards. For example:

->Tell me the separate sums of the squares and the cubes of the first five numbers.<-

Here, the number of terms plus one is 6. This same value plus the number of terms is 11. The number of terms itself is 5. The product of these three is 330. Divided by six, it is 55. This is the sum of the squares. Now, for the sum of the cubes, the number of terms is 5. By the previously mentioned rule <note>the formula for the sum of an arithmetic progression</note>—"the desired number minus one, halved," etc.—the sum of the series is 15. The square of this is 225. This is the sum of the cubes from one to five. He now states another method for finding the product of two quantities:

->From the square of the sum, one should indeed subtract the sum of the squares.<-
->Know that half of what remains is the product of the multipliers. // 23 //<-

From the square of the sum—the addition of two quantities acting as the multiplier and the multiplicand—one should subtract the sum of the squares of those same two quantities. Know that half of the remainder is the product of the two quantities known as the multiplier and the multiplicand. In mutual multiplication, either can be considered the multiplier or the multiplicand. Therefore, both are referred to by the term "multiplier" <term>Gunakara: literally 'the maker of the multiplication'</term>. For example:

->Tell me the product of two quantities equal to five and seven:<-

The sum of the two quantities is 12. Its square is 144. From this, subtract the sum of the squares of the quantities, 25 and 49 <note>which is 74</note>. The remainder is 70. Half of this, 35, is the product of the two quantities five and seven. Of the two quantities—

<page-num>31</page-num>
# Section on Mathematics

He explains how to determine two unknown quantities when their product and their difference are known:

> ->From the product multiplied by the square of two, combined with the square of the difference, take the square root.<-
> ->That root, increased and decreased by the difference, then halved, results in the two factors. || 24 ||<-

From the product of two quantities, multiplied by the square of two—which is the number four—and increased by the square of the difference between the two quantities, one should take the square root and set it down in two places. From one, subtract the difference of the quantities; to the other, add the difference. These two results, when halved, become the two factors <note>original: "gunakara-dvayam"</note>. For example:

> ->The product is ten and the difference is three; tell me the two quantities, O wise one!<-

Here, the product of the quantities is 10. Multiplied by the square of two, it is 40. The difference is 3; its square is 9. Added together, they make 49. The square root of this is 7. That root increased by the difference and halved is 5; this is the first quantity. That same root, 7, decreased by the difference and halved is 2; this is the second quantity. All such methods and other means are demonstrated in the <term>Commentary on Lilavati</term> <note>The Lilavati is a famous mathematical treatise by Bhaskara II; the author refers the reader there for more complex proofs</note>. Therefore, it should be understood from that source.

Next, he explains how to find the interest on a principal sum when the original interest has been reinvested:

> ->Multiply the principal-plus-interest by the time and the principal; add the square of half the principal.<-
> ->The square root of this, minus half the principal, divided by the time, is the interest on the principal. || 25 ||<-

The sum given as interest on a principal (such as one hundred) for a single unit of time is called the "interest-on-principal" <note>original: "mula-phala"</note>. This, combined with its own interest over a desired period of time, is "with interest" <note>original: "sa-phalam"</note>. Multiply this by the desired time and again by the principal...

<sig>11</sig>

<summary>This page provides algebraic rules for finding two numbers based on their product and difference, followed by a formula for calculating compound interest or interest on interest over time.</summary>
<keywords>algebra, quadratic equations, factors, interest calculation, Lilavati, Ganitapada, Aryabhata</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical exposition of Aryabhata's principles, specifically focusing on interest calculations and the introduction of the Rule of Three.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
# On the Aryabhatiya

Multiply the given amount by the principal of one hundred and so on. Add the square of half the principal <note>original: "mūla-ardha-kṛti"; referring to (P/2)²</note> and find the square root. Subtract half the principal from that square root and divide the result by the desired time. The quotient obtained is the interest <note>term: "phala" — literally 'fruit', used here for interest earned</note> on the principal of one hundred for one month. This also represents the original amount lent. For example:

->If the interest on one hundred for one month is unknown, but that interest was lent out,<-
->and after six months the total of that interest plus its own interest became sixteen, tell me the monthly interest on the principal.<-

Here, the amount lent (the original interest) plus its own interest is 16. This is multiplied by the desired time of six months, equaling 96. This is then multiplied by the standard principal of one hundred, equaling 9600. Add the square of half the principal (half of one hundred is 50, its square is 2500), which equals 12100. The square root of this is 110. Subtract half the principal (50) from this, leaving 60. Divide this by the desired time of six, which equals 10. This number ten is the monthly interest on one hundred. It is also the original amount that was lent. Next, he explains the Rule of Three <note>term: "trairāśika" — the mathematical 'Rule of Three' used to find an unknown value from three knowns</note>:

> Having multiplied the "fruit" quantity by the "desire" quantity,
> and dividing the result by the "measure" quantity,
> the quotient obtained shall be the "fruit of the desire." || 26 ||

There are three quantities: the Measure <note>term: "pramāṇa"</note>, the Fruit <note>term: "phala"</note>, and the Desire <note>term: "icchā"</note>. The operation performed with these is called the Rule of Three. In the Rule of Three, one should multiply the quantity called "fruit" by the quantity called "desire" and divide it by the quantity called "measure." The result obtained from that division is the "fruit of the desire" <note>the unknown value being sought</note>. For example:

->If ten mangoes are obtained for one hundred betel leaves...<-

# Chapter on Mathematics

->If sixty betel leaves are obtained, how many mangoes would be received in the same manner?<-

In this case, one hundred betel leaves is the <term>argument</term><note>original: "pramāṇarāśi"; the known quantity used as a base for comparison</note>. Ten mangoes is the <term>fruit</term><note>original: "phalarāśi"; the result or yield associated with the argument</note>. Sixty betel leaves is the <term>requisition</term><note>original: "icchārāśi"; the quantity for which we want to find the result</note>. The result obtained from the fruit multiplied by that [requisition] and divided by the argument is the number six. That is the "fruit of the requisition" <note>the final answer to the proportion</note>. He explains the specific rule regarding fractions with half a verse:

->The denominators of the multipliers and divisors are multiplied by one another.<-

The denominators of the multipliers and divisors become clear when multiplied by each other. This is what is being said: The product of the multiplier and the multiplicand is intended here by the word "multiplier." It means the <term>dividend</term><note>original: "hārya"; the quantity to be divided</note>. The denominator of the dividend, when multiplied by the divisor, becomes the [new] divisor. The denominator of the divisor, when multiplied by the dividend, becomes the [new] dividend. Thus, if both the multiplier and multiplicand have denominators, the product of those two denominators becomes the denominator of the dividend. He explains the process of <term>reduction to a common denominator</term><note>original: "savarṇīkaraṇa"</note> with the latter half of the verse:

->That which has a denominator, when multiplied by the [other] denominator, results in mutual assimilation. || 27 ||<-

"That which has a denominator"—here the numerator is the object being qualified. One should multiply the numerator, along with its denominator, by the other's denominator. The meaning is that one should multiply each numerator and its corresponding denominator by all the other denominators distinct from itself in sequence. "Mutual assimilation"—this means the reduction to a common denominator. When this is done, all quantities will have equal denominators. An example:

->What are three-eighths multiplied by a quarter and divided by a third?<-
->O Poet, tell me the multiplier, the multiplicand, and the divisor, and reduce them to a common denominator.<-

Here, the multiplicand is 3 <gloss>8</gloss> <note>3/8</note>. The multiplier is 1 <gloss>4</gloss> <note>1/4</note>. Their product is 3 <gloss>32</gloss> <note>3/32</note>. This is the dividend. The divisor is 1 <gloss>3</gloss> <note>1/3</note>. Of the divisor...

<summary>This page explains the Rule of Three through a practical example involving betel leaves and mangoes, followed by mathematical rules for multiplying and dividing fractions and finding common denominators.</summary>
<keywords>Rule of Three, fractions, multiplication, division, common denominator, Aryabhata, Ganitapada, mathematics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical commentary on the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the reduction of fractions to a common denominator and the "Rule of Inversion" for solving algebraic problems.</meta>

# On the Aryabhatiya
<page-num>44</page-num>

The dividend is multiplied by the denominator: 9. This is the dividend. The divisor is multiplied by the denominator: 32. This is the divisor. Thus, in this case, the dividend becomes the number nine and the divisor becomes the number thirty-two. The placement for the reduction to a common denominator <note>Sanskrit: *savarnikarana*; the process of making fractions have the same denominator to perform operations</note> is as follows:
$^{9}_{4} \text{ , } ^{6}_{4} \text{ , } ^{2}_{1}$
In this case, the multiplicand and its denominator should be multiplied by the denominators of the multiplier and the divisor; the multiplier and its denominator should be multiplied by the denominators of the multiplicand and the divisor; and the divisor and its denominator should be multiplied by the denominators of the multiplier and the multiplicand. When this is done, the multiplicand becomes $^{36}_{16}$, the multiplier becomes $^{24}_{16}$, and the divisor becomes $^{32}_{16}$. This method should be understood to apply everywhere. Now, he explains the Rule of Inversion:

> Multipliers become divisors, and those that were divisors become multipliers.
> That which was an increase becomes a decrease, and a decrease becomes an increase in the inverse. || 28 ||

The Rule of Inversion <note>Sanskrit: *vyastavidhi*; a method of working backward from a known result to find an unknown starting value</note> is applied to find an intended quantity from a visible result. In the case of the intended quantity, whatever was a multiplier becomes a divisor. The divisor becomes a multiplier. An addition <note>Sanskrit: *kshepa*</note> becomes a subtraction <note>Sanskrit: *apachaya*</note>. A subtraction becomes an addition. Thus it happens in the inverse method. Even things not explicitly mentioned are established by this: in the case of a square, one takes the root; in the case of a root, one performs a squaring, and so on. An example:

-> Some quantity is multiplied by three, divided by five, increased by six, and the square root is taken. <-
-> Decreased by one and then squared, the result is the number of the Vedas <note>The number 4, as there are four Vedas</note>. O Calculator, tell me that quantity! <-

The visible result is 4. Because it was squared, the square root is taken: 2. Because it was decreased by one, one is added: 3. Because the square root was taken, it is squared: 9. Because it was increased by six, it is decreased by those: 3. Because it was divided by five, it is multiplied by five: 15. Because it was multiplied by three, it is divided by three: 5. This is the intended quantity.

Where there are many groups, and one group is removed at a time while the numbers of the remaining groups are calculated, he now explains the method for finding the sum of all those numbers.

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>Section on Mathematics</header>

## Section on Mathematics

> Subtracting each quantity from the total, the sums of the remaining quantities are added together. 
> When divided by the number of quantities minus one, the result is the true total sum. || 29 ||

"Subtracting each quantity from the total": Having made sums by omitting one quantity at a time, let the sum of those results—referred to as the "series-wealth"—be set down separately in as many places as there are groups. "Added together": Having calculated their total sum. "By the number of quantities minus one": Divide by the total number of groups less one. The quotient obtained there is indeed the "total sum" <note>original: *sarvadhana*</note>. This means the sum of all the individual group values. 

From that total sum, when each of the previously established "sums-omitting-one-quantity" is subtracted, the remainder is the value of that specific individual group. Specifically, the remainder will be the value of the group that was omitted to create that particular sum. For example:

> Three flocks of birds—hawks, herons, and swans—were seen in the forest. The sums of any two, excluding the third, were found to be twelve, fourteen, and sixteen. O wise one, tell me the number of birds in each of the three flocks.

In this case, the number known by excluding the first quantity is 12 <note>original: *Arka*, a word-numeral for 12</note>. The number known by excluding the second is 14 <note>original: *Śakra*, a word-numeral for 14</note>. The number known by excluding the third is 16 <note>original: *Aṣṭi*, a word-numeral for 16</note>. These three values, placed separately, are added together to get 42. This is divided by the number of groups minus one (which is 2), resulting in 21. This is the total number of birds in the three flocks. 

This total, minus the first mentioned "Arka" (12), leaves 9; this is the number of hawks <note>original: *śyena*</note>. Then, the total minus "Śakra" (14) leaves 7; this is the number of herons <note>original: *kaṅka*</note>. Then, the total minus "Aṣṭi" (16) leaves 5; this is the number of swans <note>original: *haṃsa*</note>. 

To demonstrate the value of unknown items, the author says:

---

> One should divide the difference in the known amounts <note>original: *rūpaka*, often referring to coins or constant units</note> by the difference in the unknown items <note>original: *gulikā*, literally "little balls" or beads, used here as algebraic variables</note> belonging to two men. 
> The result is the value of one unknown item, provided their total wealth is equal. || 30 ||

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Aryabhata's mathematical rules, specifically focusing on linear equations with one unknown and the calculation of conjunction times for planets.</meta>

# In the Aryabhatiya
<page-num>46</page-num>

Commodities such as cows and the like are referred to by the term *gulika* <note>original: "gulika"; literally "bead" or "ball," used here as a placeholder for an unknown quantity or variable</note>. The term *rupaka* <note>original: "rupaka"; a standard unit of currency or a known numerical value</note> refers to wealth in the form of gold or coins like the *pana*. In such cases, one should divide the difference <insert>subtraction</insert> between the two known amounts of wealth (*rupaka*) by the difference between the two quantities of commodities (*gulika*). The result obtained is the value <note>price</note> of a single unit of that commodity. This applies if the total values are equal. This means the rule is used when the sum of the value of the commodities and the cash held by two different persons is equal. For example:

->One man has a hundred coins and six cows; the other has sixty coins and eight cows.<-
->If their total wealth is equal, what is the value of a single cow?<-

The first man's cash amount is 100. His commodity count (cows) is 6. The second man's cash amount is 60. His commodity count is 8. Here, the difference between the cash amounts is 40. This is divided by the difference in the number of commodities, which is 2. The result of this division is 20. This number, twenty, is the price of a single cow. In this case, the total wealth of each person becomes two hundred and twenty coins. 

Next, the author explains how to calculate the time of conjunction <note>the meeting point</note> of two planets based on the distance between them:

->Divide the distance between two planets moving in opposite directions by the sum of their speeds.<-
->Divide the distance between two planets moving in the same direction by the difference of their speeds.<-
->The results are the times of conjunction, either past or future. || 31 ||<-

When two planets are moving in opposite directions <note>one direct and one retrograde</note>, the distance between them—their clear interval converted into minutes of arc—should be divided by the sum of their speeds <note>their daily motion in minutes</note>. When two planets are moving in the same direction <note>both direct or both retrograde</note>, the distance between them should be divided by the difference between their speeds. The word "two" is used because the difference can be of two kinds: the slower speed subtracted from the faster speed, or the faster speed subtracted from the slower speed. Thus, there are two ways to find the interval. In this division, the two results obtained are—

<term>Gulika</term> (Unknown quantity/commodity), <term>Rupaka</term> (Known number/coin), <term>Gomulyam</term> (Price of a cow), <term>Aryabhatiya</term> (The treatise by Aryabhata), <term>Viloma-vivara</term> (Distance in opposite motion), <term>Gatyantara</term> (Difference in speeds), <term>Liptikrita</term> (Converted into minutes of arc).

<summary>This page explains a mathematical rule for solving linear equations involving an unknown (the 'cow' problem) and provides an astronomical rule for determining when two planets will meet based on their relative speeds and directions.</summary>
<keywords>algebra, linear equations, astronomy, planetary conjunction, Aryabhata, Indian mathematics, retrograde motion</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical exposition of the Ganitapada, specifically transitioning from planetary motion calculations to the rules of pulverization (algebraic indeterminate equations).</meta>

# Section on Mathematics
<page-num>47</page-num>

The times of conjunction. For two planets, the times of their conjunction are expressed in days. These times are either past or future. If the faster-moving planet is behind <note>original: "gacchati," literally 'going' or 'moving'</note>, that time is past. If the slower-moving planet is behind, that time is in the future. In the case of retrograde motion, however, if the planet moving "upward" <note>referring to the higher epicycle or specific retrograde velocity</note> is retrograde, the time is future; otherwise, it is past. Now, he speaks two verses <note>term: Arya; a specific meter of Sanskrit verse</note> to demonstrate the mathematics of the "Pulverizer" <note>term: Kuttakara; a method for solving indeterminate equations of the first degree, similar to the Euclidean algorithm or continued fractions</note>.

->Divide the divisor corresponding to the greater remainder by the divisor corresponding to the smaller remainder.<-
->The remainders are mutually divided; a multiplier is chosen such that, when multiplied and added to the difference of the remainders... (32)<-
->Multiplied from bottom to top and added to the last, then divided by the divisor of the smaller remainder, the remainder...<-
->Multiplied by the divisor of the greater remainder and added to the greater remainder, the result is the value for both divisors. (33)<-

Thus: the Pulverizer is of two types: "without remainder" <note>term: Niragra; a non-residual or simple indeterminate equation</note> and "with remainder" <note>term: Sagra; a residual indeterminate equation</note>. When a dividend is multiplied by some multiplier and divided by a divisor, a remainder is produced; the process performed to find that multiplier using that remainder, the dividend, and the divisor is called the "Pulverizer without remainder." In that case, if the calculated multiplier differs from the original multiplier, the original state is restored by adding its own divisor. Where a single quantity is multiplied by two dividends and divided by two divisors, resulting in two remainders, and the two multipliers are found by the "without remainder" method using those dividends and divisors—if those two multipliers are different, then the remaining process to find the original multiplier from those two and their divisors is called the "Pulverizer with remainder." Of the two multipliers brought forth by the two remainders, the larger is called the "greater remainder" <note>term: Adhikagra</note> and the smaller is known as the "lesser remainder." These two verses are intended to demonstrate the Pulverizer with remainder. The one without remainder is also established from this. "Divide the divisor corresponding to the greater remainder..."

<summary>This page introduces the Kuttakara (Pulverizer) method for solving indeterminate equations, distinguishing between simple (Niragra) and residual (Sagra) types. It provides the foundational verses for the algorithm used to find unknown multipliers in planetary conjunctions.</summary>
<keywords>Kuttakara, Pulverizer, indeterminate equations, Aryabhata, Ganitapada, Sanskrit mathematics, residual method, planetary conjunction</keywords>
