<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Aryabhata's mathematical verses, specifically focusing on the "Kuttaka" or pulverizer method for solving indeterminate equations.</meta>

# On the Aryabhatiya
### Page 48

By the divisor belonging to the smaller remainder. One should divide the divisor that serves as the means for the larger remainder by the divisor that serves as the means for the smaller remainder. One should perform the division. **The remainders are mutually divided.** Subsequently, the process of mutual division of the remainders should be carried out.

The word "remainder" here indicates both the remainder of the division and the divisor of the smaller remainder situated near it. The meaning is that the mutual division of the remainder of the division and the divisor of the smaller remainder should be performed. When, however, the first division is not possible because the divisor of the larger remainder is smaller than the divisor of the smaller remainder, then the mutual division of the larger-remainder divisor and the smaller-remainder divisor should be performed. Indeed, in the <term>Pulverizer</term> <note>original: "Kuttaka"; a method for solving indeterminate equations of the first degree</note> method, the mutual division of the dividend and the divisor is prescribed. There, the first division of the divisor by the dividend is intended. Here also, the divisor of the larger remainder is imagined as the <term>dividend</term> <note>original: "bhajya"</note>. The divisor of the smaller remainder is imagined as the <term>divisor</term> <note>original: "bhajaka"</note>. There, the first division is prescribed to reduce the size of the dividend. When the dividend is already smaller at the very beginning, then that first division should not be performed. In the process of mutual division, each resulting quotient should be placed one below the other in sequence so that a <term>vine of quotients</term> <note>original: "phalavalli"; a column or chain of values used in the calculation</note> is formed. By the statement "mutually divided," the taking of the quotients is also implied. The quotient resulting from dividing one by the other is "mutually divided," and that should be placed [in the column]—this is the remainder of the instruction. This mutual division and the placing of its quotients should be performed until the remainders are small enough that an <gloss>optional number</gloss> <note>original: "mati"</note> can be thought of. Because mutual division is directed, the process ends when the number of quotient terms is even. Therefore, the optional number is conceived at an even-numbered position. **The optional number, multiplied and added to the difference of the remainders.** That number is the "optional number" which, when it multiplies the last remainder of the dividend, and the "interpolator" is either added to or subtracted from that product, the result is exactly divisible by the last remainder of the divisor. The "interpolator" <note>original: "ksepa-rashi"</note> shall be the difference between the two remainders. Having mentally conceived that optional number, and having multiplied the small remaining dividend by it, [one adds] the difference of the remainders...

<summary>This page explains the mathematical steps for the "Kuttaka" (pulverizer) method, detailing how to perform mutual division between divisors to create a "vine" of quotients and how to determine an "optional number" to solve the equation.</summary>
<keywords>Kuttaka, Pulverizer, Aryabhata, mathematics, dividend, divisor, quotients, Phalavalli, Mati, interpolator</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the "Kuttaka" (pulverizer) method for solving linear indeterminate equations, focusing on the final steps of the calculation and the "Valli" (chain) reduction process.</meta>

# Section on Mathematics
<page-num>43</page-num>

After adding the value designated as the <term>interpolant<note>original: "kshepa"; a constant added or subtracted in the equation</note></term>, one should divide it by the larger divisor-remainder so that no remainder is left, and take the resulting quotient. Then, place that "thought-number" <note>original: "mati"; an optionally chosen number used to facilitate the division</note> below the column of quotients, and place the obtained result below that. Indeed, the process of mutual division <note>the Euclidean algorithm</note> is performed to make the selection of this "thought-number" easier. To complete that process, the "folding of the chain" <note>original: "valli-upasamhara"</note> is prescribed by the rule: "multiply the term above by the one below and add the last." Therefore, it is established that the quotient must be taken from a division that leaves no remainder.

Now, regarding the "thought-number": according to the rule "multiply the term above by the one below and add the last," the word "below" refers to the penultimate term. One should multiply the term above it by this penultimate term and add the last term to the product. One should repeat this process until only two quantities remain. Of those two, only the upper quantity should be taken.

"When divided by the divisor of the smaller remainder, the remainder multiplied by the divisor of the larger remainder, plus the larger remainder, is the result for both divisors." <note>This is a rule for finding a number that satisfies two different remainders when divided by two different divisors.</note> One should divide the upper of the two quantities by the divisor of the smaller remainder. Multiply the remainder of that division by the divisor of the larger remainder, and add the larger remainder to it. That becomes the quantity satisfying both remainders. This means it is the multiplier <note>original: "gunakara"</note> that yields the two remainders for the two previously mentioned dividends.

The same method applies even when there is no remainder <note>original: "niragra"; referring to equations where the constant is zero</note>. However, in that case, when choosing the "thought-number," the remainder produced is called the "subtrahend" <note>original: "shodhya"</note> if it is a visible quantity to be subtracted. If it is to be added, it is called the "additive" <note>original: "kshepya"</note>. Once the two quantities are produced, divide the upper quantity by the divisor. The remainder there will be the multiplier, such as the <term>number of elapsed days<note>original: "ahargana"</note></term>. Divide the lower quantity by the dividend. The remainder there will be the quotient, known as the <term>number of revolutions<note>original: "bhagana"</note></term>. The rule "multiplied by the divisor of the larger remainder" and so on does not apply in this specific case.

Alternatively, the plan is as follows: "Divide the larger divisor by the smaller remainder's divisor." By the term "larger divisor," both the dividend and divisor of the larger number are meant. For even the dividend can become a divisor during the process of mutual division. One should divide those by the "smaller remainder's divisor," meaning some smaller quantity, until no remainder is left.

<summary>This page explains the "Valli" (chain) method for collapsing a column of quotients to solve indeterminate equations, specifically defining how to derive the multiplier (days elapsed) and the quotient (planetary revolutions).</summary>
<keywords>Kuttaka, Pulverizer, Ahargana, Bhagana, Valli-upasamhara, Indian Mathematics, Indeterminate Equations</keywords>

# On the Aryabhatiya
<page-num>50</page-num>

This means that if a reduction <note>original: "apavartana"; refers to simplifying a fraction by a common factor</note> is possible, one should reduce it. Then, the process of mutual division of the remainders and so on should be performed. That is, the mutual division should be applied to the two reduced quantities. Here is an example:

->A certain quantity is multiplied by eight and divided by twenty-one,<-
->the remainder is equal to four. Furthermore, when that same quantity<-
->is multiplied by seventeen and divided by forty-five,<-
->the remainder is equal to three. O learned one, what is that quantity?<-

In the first case, the dividend <term>bhajya: the number to be multiplied</term> is 8. The divisor <term>hara: the number to divide by</term> is 21. The remainder <term>shesha</term> is 4. When mutual division is performed between the dividend and the divisor, and the resulting quotients are placed in a chain <note>original: "valli"; literally 'creeper' or 'vine', a column of numbers used in the calculation</note>, the known chain of quotients is <gloss>3<br/>1</gloss>. The dividend-remainder is 1. The divisor-remainder is 2.
The quantity to be subtracted is the remainder, which is 4. In this case, <gloss>1</gloss> the assumed intelligence <term>mati: an optional chosen multiplier to facilitate the calculation</term> is 6. When the subtraction-quantity is taken from the dividend-remainder multiplied by the "intelligence" value, the remainder is 2. Dividing that by the divisor-remainder, the quotient <gloss>1</gloss> is 1. The chain joined with the "intelligence" and its quotient is <gloss>3<br/>1<br/>6<br/>1</gloss>. By the rule "multiply the term above by the one below and add the last," etc., the two quantities obtained are <gloss>73<br/>20</gloss>. Of these two, the upper one should be divided by the divisor 21. The remainder there is 15. This is the multiplier <term>gunakara</term>. In the "with-remainder" method <note>original: "sagra-vidhi"</note>, this is the "remainder." The lower one should be divided by the dividend 8. The remainder there is 4. This is the quotient-quantity. This means the quotient is obtained from the divisor when the dividend is multiplied by the multiplier calculated here. This is the "remainder-less" pulverizer <term>niragra-kuttakara</term>.

Now, in the second case, the dividend is 17. The divisor is 45. The remainder is 3. By these also, the multiplier-quantity brought forth as before is 11. In the "with-remainder" method, this is the "remainder." This is called the "lesser remainder" <term>unagra</term>. The one previously calculated is called the "greater remainder" <term>adhikagra</term>, which is 15. The two remainders are <gloss>15<br/>11</gloss>. The difference between the remainders is 4. This is the "addend" quantity <term>kshepya</term>. The divisor of the greater remainder...

<summary>This page provides a step-by-step mathematical demonstration of the Kuttaka (pulverizer) method using a specific word problem involving two linear indeterminate equations. It explains the use of the "valli" (chain) algorithm and the "mati" (interpolated multiplier) to find unknown variables.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the section on Mathematics (Ganitapada) and begins the section on Time Reckoning (Kalakriyapada). It continues the discussion on the "pulverizer" (kuttaka) method for solving linear indeterminate equations.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>
# Section on Mathematics

21. This is the dividend. The divisor with the smaller remainder is 45; this is the divisor. Here, the first division of the divisor with the larger remainder by the one with the smaller remainder is not possible <note>This refers to a specific step in the Euclidean algorithm where the first divisor is larger than the dividend</note>. Therefore, having performed mutual division of the two divisors and having produced the "creeper" <note>valli: a column of quotients used in the Indian method of solving equations</note>, and having designated the difference between the remainders as the "interpolator" <note>jñeyarāśi: the known quantity or additive in the equation</note>, one should bring out the multiplier using the method for equations without remainders. The multiplier thus brought out is 34. This is multiplied by the divisor of the larger remainder, 21, resulting in 714. This is increased by the larger remainder, 287, resulting in 1001. This is the multiplier known as the "remainder of two divisors." This is indeed the intended multiplier. When the remainder of two divisors brought out in this way is different from the intended multiplier, then the intended multiplier is to be calculated by adding its own divisor multiplied by a desired whole number. The "own divisor" would be the product of the divisors of the larger and smaller remainders. Alternatively, the divisor may be the product of those two divisors divided by the remainder left from their mutual division <note>This describes finding the Least Common Multiple (LCM) of the two divisors</note>. This "pulverizer with remainders" <term>sāgra-kuṭṭākāra: a method for solving indeterminate equations of the first degree where remainders are present</term> is performed in many ways by experts in mathematics. The "pulverizer without remainders" also has many varieties such as the "pulverizer of days" and "pulverizer of time." All of that has been demonstrated in detail in the commentary on the *Mahābhāskarīya* called the *Siddhāntadīpikā*. Therefore, it is not treated extensively by us here.

->Thus ends the second section, the Section on Mathematics, in the *Bhaṭṭadīpikā* composed by Parameśvara<-

---

### Now the Section on the Reckoning of Time is presented. There, he describes the divisions of time:

> A year consists of twelve months; a month consists of thirty days.
> A day consists of sixty *nadis*, and a *nadi* consists of sixty *vinadikas*. || 1 ||

One year is twelve months. That in which there are thirty days is "thirty-daily"; a month consists of thirty days. The meaning is that one month is thirty days. One day is sixty *nadis* <note>nadi: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note>...

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of time measurement from the previous section of the Aryabhatiya, specifically defining the subdivisions of a day and the various systems of timekeeping.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

It occurs thus: One <term>nadi</term><note>A unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> consists of sixty <term>vinadikas</term><note>A unit of time equal to 24 seconds</note>. In the years designated as Solar, Civil, Lunar, and so forth, one-twelfth of the duration of that specific year is the duration of its corresponding month. In this manner, the time units of days, <term>nadis</term>, and so on, are to be understood according to their own specific standards. The different varieties of time are said to be of nine types:

-> The Brahma, the Ancestral, as well as the Divine, the Prajapatya, and the Jovian; <-
-> The Solar, the Civil, the Lunar, and the Sidereal are indeed the nine measurements. <-

So it is said.

In the following half-verse, the author demonstrates the duration of a <term>vinadika</term>, which is a fractional part of the Sidereal day—the time equivalent to one full rotation of the star cluster.

-> Sixty long syllables, or indeed six breaths, constitute a sidereal <term>vinadika</term>. <-

The time it takes for a man to utter sixty long syllables <note>original: "gurvaksara"</note> at a moderate pace is the duration of a sidereal <term>vinadika</term>. This is the <term>vinadika</term> pertaining to the stars <note>original: "rksa"</note>. The time in which the celestial sphere, which supports the stars, completes one rotation is a sidereal day. A sixtieth part of that is a sidereal <term>nadi</term>. A sixtieth part of that is this sidereal <term>vinadika</term>. Or, "indeed six breaths": the time in which a man takes six respirations <note>original: "ucchvasa"</note> is also the duration of a sidereal <term>vinadika</term>. The meaning is that both these durations are equal. Thus the division of time is shown. In the second half of the verse, he states that the division of the celestial field should be understood in the same way.

-> Thus is the division of time; the division of the field <note>the zodiac/space</note> is likewise derived from the revolution. || 2 || <-

<meta>This page continues the section on the 'Reckoning of Time' (Kalakriyapada), transitioning from the measurement of time to the measurement of space within the celestial sphere.</meta>

# Section on the Reckoning of Time

The division of time by years has been explained. In the same way, the division of space should be understood through the revolutions <note>original: "bhagana"; refers to a complete orbit or revolution of a planet through the zodiac</note>. This is what is meant: one-twelfth of a revolution is one Sign <note>original: "rashi"; a zodiac sign</note>. One-thirtieth of a Sign is one Degree <note>original: "bhaga"</note>. One-sixtieth of a Degree is one Minute <note>original: "lipta"</note>. One-sixtieth of a Minute is one Second <note>original: "vilipta"</note>. One-sixtieth of a Second is one Third <note>original: "tatpara"</note>. Thus, the revolutions and their subdivisions are spatial, while years and their subdivisions are temporal. In the following half-verse, the author explains the method for knowing the number of conjunctions of any two planets moving in the zodiac during a *Yuga* <note>a vast astronomical cycle of time</note>.

->The difference between the revolutions of any two planets in a Yuga is the number of their conjunctions.<-

The difference between the total revolutions of any two planets in a *Yuga*—that is, the remainder when the smaller number of revolutions is subtracted from the larger—equals the number of their conjunctions during that *Yuga*. When two bodies start at the same time and travel the same circle at different speeds (one slow and one fast), a conjunction occurs when the faster one has completed exactly one full revolution more than the slower one. Therefore, the number of conjunctions for two bodies moving in a circle is equal to the difference in their total revolutions. In the second half of the verse, he gives the number of *Vyatipatas* <note>a specific inauspicious astronomical alignment involving the Sun and Moon</note> in a *Yuga*.

> The sum of the revolutions of the Sun and the Moon are the number of Vyatipatas. || 3 ||

This means the total number of revolutions of the Sun and the Moon in a *Yuga* added together. When the sum of the revolutions of these two is calculated, that is the number of *Vyatipatas* in a *Yuga*. This implies they are equal to twice the sum of the revolutions of the Sun and Moon <note>The commentator clarifies that a Vyatipata occurs twice for every combined revolution</note>. This is what is being said: when the sum of the positions of the Sun and Moon equals half a circle (180 degrees), one *Vyatipata* occurs. When their sum equals a full circle (360 degrees), a second *Vyatipata* occurs. Here, the *Vyatipata* is described in a general sense...

<summary>This page defines the spatial divisions of the zodiac (signs, degrees, minutes, etc.) as counterparts to time divisions and explains how to calculate the number of planetary conjunctions and solar-lunar alignments (Vyatipatas) in a great cycle.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Aryabhatiya, specifically discussing the astronomical phenomena of Vyatipata and Vaidhrita, followed by the calculation of planetary revolutions in epicycles.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>On the Aryabhatiya</header>

...has been stated. However, the precise version was stated by Maya <note>The mythical author of the Surya Siddhanta</note>:

<margin>Surya Siddhanta, Chapter on Nodes, verses 1–2.</margin>

->When the Sun and the Moon are in the same solstice,<-
->and the sum of their positions equals a full circle (360°), and their declinations are equal, it is called Vaidhrita.<-
->When the Moon and Sun are in opposite solstices,<-
->and their minutes of declination are equal, it is Vyatipata; the sum of their positions is then half a circle (180°).<-

Thus it is said. Here too, by the words "full circle" and "half a circle," it is implied that the Sun and Moon are in different hemispheres or the same hemisphere respectively. This should be understood. Now, he explains the revolutions in the epicycle <term>Ucca-nica-vrtta; the "high-low circle" used to model planetary anomalies</term> with the half-verse on revolutions:

->The revolutions of the high-low epicycle are the difference between the revolutions of the planet's own apex and the planet's own revolutions.<-

The meaning is: the difference between the revolutions of the apex <term>Ucca; the point of highest velocity or distance</term> and the planet's own revolutions is the number of revolutions in the epicycle. For the Moon, the difference between the revolutions of its apex <insert>the lunar apogee</insert> and its own revolutions is the revolution of its slow-motion <term>Manda; the anomaly equivalent to the equation of center</term> epicycle. For the other six planets, because their slow-motion apexes are stationary, their own revolutions are simply the revolutions of their slow-motion epicycles. For the five planets starting with Mars, the difference between the revolutions of their fast-motion apex <term>Shighra; the anomaly equivalent to the solar/orbital component</term> and their own revolutions is the revolution of the fast-motion epicycle. All planets wander around their own apex. In that context, when the planet is near the apex, it acts as if at its "high" point, and in the seventh sign from the apex, it acts as if at its "low" point. That wandering is what is called "revolutions in the high-low epicycle" here. In that regard, the revolutions in the high-low epicycle during a Yuga <note>An immense cosmic time cycle</note> are stated here as moving in the direction of the signs from the slow-apex and in the opposite direction from the fast-apex. This separate statement of what is already established by the logic of subtraction is for the purpose of demonstrating the epicyclic motion of the planets. He explains the "Years of Jupiter" with the following half-verse:

->The revolutions of Jupiter multiplied by twelve are the years of Jupiter, starting with Ashvayuja. || 4 ||<-

It also introduces the Jovian cycle where each revolution of Jupiter equals twelve years named after lunar mansions.</summary>

# Section on the Reckoning of Time

The revolutions of Jupiter <note>Sanskrit: *Guru*</note> multiplied by twelve are the years of the Yuga <note>A vast cosmic cycle of time</note> beginning with the month of Ashvayuja; this is the meaning.

On this subject, Varahamihira states: <note>original: "Atra Varahamihirah"; referencing the Brihat Samhita, Chapter 8, Verses 1-2</note>

->The year is to be named after the asterism <note>Nakshatra</note> in which Jupiter rises or sets,<-
->following the sequence of the months.<-
->The years beginning with Karttika follow the constellations in pairs,<-
->except for the fifth, eleventh, and twelfth years, which are associated with three constellations each.<-

This means the sequence of years follows the sequence of months starting with Karttika. Now, the author explains the division of solar, lunar, civil, and sidereal measurements:

->The revolutions of the Sun are solar years. The conjunctions of the Sun and Moon are lunar months.<-
->The conjunctions of the Sun and Earth are civil days, and the rotations of the star-sphere are sidereal days. || 5 ||<-

The time taken for one complete revolution of the Sun is a solar year. The time between two successive conjunctions of the Sun and the Moon is a lunar month. This refers to the time it takes for the two bodies, having started together, to meet again. In a Yuga, the solar years are equal to the number of solar revolutions. In a Yuga, the lunar months are equal to the number of conjunctions between the Sun and Moon. By the phrase "conjunctions of the Sun and Earth," the Sun's apparent circuit around the Earth is meant. The number of such circuits in a Yuga equals the number of civil days <note>Sanskrit: *Bhudivasa* or *Savana* days</note>. The number of rotations of the celestial sphere <note>the star-cluster</note> in a Yuga are the sidereal days. Here, the sidereal days are defined by the rotation of the zodiac, not by the movement of the Moon. 

Next, the author discusses intercalary months and omitted days...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and astronomical definitions of the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the calculation of intercalary months and the hierarchy of time scales for humans, ancestors, and gods.</meta>

# In the Aryabhatiya
<page-num>56</page-num>

->The intercalary months in a Yuga <note>a vast cosmic cycle of time</note> are those lunar months which are in excess of the solar months.<-
->The omissions of lunar days <note>known as 'tithi-pralaya' or 'avama' days</note> are understood to be the lunar days minus the earth days. || 6 ||<-

The lunar months of a Yuga minus the solar months of a Yuga are the intercalary months <note>term: Adhimasaka; extra months added to the lunar calendar to keep it synchronized with the solar seasons</note> in a Yuga. The lunar days of a Yuga minus the earth days <note>civil days</note> of a Yuga are the omissions of lunar days in a Yuga. This means the "omitted days" <note>term: Avama-divasa; days in the lunar calendar that are skipped to maintain alignment with the solar cycle</note>. Now, he describes the measurement of a year for humans, ancestors, and gods:

->A solar year is a human year; thirty of those make a year of the ancestors.<-
->Twelve years of the ancestors are declared to be a divine year <note>a year of the gods</note>. || 7 ||<-

A solar year is a human year. (A human year multiplied by thirty is a year of the ancestors). A year of the ancestors multiplied by twelve is a divine year. Here, the ancestral year is described according to solar measurement; however, in other branches of sacred tradition <note>shakhas</note>, it is described by lunar measurement. As Maya <note>a legendary astronomer</note> says:

->A lunar month of thirty lunar days is remembered as a single day and night of the ancestors.<-

Thus it is said. Now he describes the duration of a Yuga for the planets and the duration of a Day of Brahma:

->Twelve multiplied by a thousand divine years is a common Yuga of the planets.<-
->One thousand and eight of these planetary Yugas is a Day of Brahma. || 8 ||<-

Twelve times a thousand divine years—that is, twelve thousand—is a common Yuga of the planets. This means it is the Yuga for all the planets. Since all planets are at the beginning of the zodiac <note>zero degrees longitude</note> at the start of a Yuga, there is no distinction between their individual cycles for the purpose of calculating mean positions; this is indicated by the word "common." Regarding the divisions of time such as the "ascending" and "descending" periods...

<note>original: "Surya-siddhante Manadhyaye Shlo. 14" — This refers to the 14th verse of the Chapter on Measurements in the Surya Siddhanta, a foundational Sanskrit astronomical text.</note>

<summary>This page defines the calculation of intercalary months and omitted lunar days, while establishing a hierarchy of time where human years scale up into ancestral, divine, and cosmic cycles (Yugas and Days of Brahma).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the division of time within the Kalakriyapada (Section on the Reckoning of Time) of Aryabhata's work.</meta>

# Section on the Reckoning of Time

He explains the division:

->The first half of a Yuga <note>A vast astronomical cycle of time</note> is the Ascending era, and the second half is the Descending era.<-
->In the middle of the Yuga is the Even era; at the beginning and end are the Uneven eras, reckoned from the Moon's apogee. || 7 ||<gloss>1</gloss>*)<-

The meaning of this was not demonstrated by the [original] commentator. Therefore, what was stated in the *Bhatta-prakashika* <note>A specific commentary on Aryabhata's work</note> is written here: The time in which the lifespan, fame, and strength of living beings increase is called the **Ascending era** <note>Utsarpini</note>. That in which they decrease is called the **Descending era** <note>Avasarpini</note>. The first half of a Yuga is the Ascending time; the latter half is the Descending time. The middle third of the Yuga is the **Even era** <note>Sushama</note>. The first and last thirds are the **Uneven eras** <note>Dushshama</note>. All of this should be understood as starting from the Moon's apogee <note>Inducca</note>. This is the explanation in the *Prakashika*, though the learned must decide its true meaning. 

Regarding this, we do not know what was meant by the phrase "should be understood as starting from the Moon's apogee." Nor do we see how the stated meaning is useful for planetary mathematics. 

Alternatively, the meaning is as follows: For the moving bodies starting from the Moon's apogee, the motion is "ascending" in the first half of the Yuga, "descending" in the second half, and "even" in the middle. The position of the planets at the beginning and end of a Yuga occurs either above or below the position they occupy during the middle period. Therefore, there would be a variation in the mean motion. For that reason, a correction to the mean motion must be calculated from time to time. 

Or perhaps: it refers to the positions of the various "high points"—the slow apogees <note>Mandocca</note> and fast apogees <note>Shighrocca</note>—starting with the Moon's apogee. Their positions may be ascending or even. The meaning is that at the beginning and end of a Yuga, the position of these apogees is either above or below where they stay during the middle of the period. Because of this, there would be a change in the [orbital] circle. From a change in the circle, there would be a change in the computed true position of the planet.

---

*) In the two manuscripts of the *Bhatta-dipika*, there is a corrupt reading: "Even at the beginning and end, Uneven..." <term>etc.</term>

->Aryabhatiya<-

Therefore, the correction for the planetary orbits <term>vritta-samskara; the adjustment of planetary cycles to match observation</term> should be performed after investigating the specific time. Thus it is said. He now indicates the time of the composition of this scientific treatise <term>shastra; a systematic body of knowledge</term> and his own age at that time:

> When sixty times sixty years and three quarter-ages <term>yuga-padas; the four divisions of a cosmic cycle</term> had passed, then twenty-three years had passed since my birth. || 10 ||

In this current twenty-eighth Great Cycle <term>Chaturyuga</term>, when three out of the four parts and sixty times sixty years <note>3,600 years</note> had elapsed, then twenty-three years had passed from the time of my birth. This means that when 3,600 solar years of the fourth quarter <note>the Kali Yuga</note> of the current cycle had passed, this treatise was composed by me at the age of twenty-three. Regarding this, the author of the *Prakashika* <note>a well-known commentary on Aryabhata</note> says: "This is the intention: at this specific time, the mean positions, apogees, and nodes of the planets brought forth by the Rule of Three using the revolutions stated in the *Gitika* section will be accurate. After this point, a correction established by tradition must be applied to those values calculated in that manner." And so says his disciple, Lallacharya, <note>In the treatise titled *Dhivridhida*</note>:

> Subtract 449 from the Shaka era <note>the Shaka era began in 78 CE</note>; multiply the Moon's position by 25, its apogee by 114, and its node by 66. For Jupiter, multiply by 47 and divide by 153; for the apogee of Venus, divide by 505. For Mars, divide by 48, and for Saturn, multiply by 20...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of astronomical corrections and the cyclical nature of time, following the previous section on planetary calculations.</meta>

# Section on the Reckoning of Time

> Multiply by four thousand three hundred and three, and apply the resulting quotient as an increase to the minutes of Mercury, Mars, and Saturn.

<note>original: "vyomāgnivedanihate..." This verse describes a specific mathematical correction for planetary positions.</note>

Regarding this: the divisor for all is equal to thirty-five <note>literally 'arrows and fire'</note>. The increase in the minutes of Mars, Saturn, and the fast-moving Mercury is that which is subtracted from the remaining minutes. This correction does not differ greatly from those calculated from the Shaka era. Here, it is appropriate to subtract four hundred and twenty-one from the Shaka year, though that was not explicitly stated. The author of the *Prakashika* <note>a famous commentary on the Aryabhatiya</note> stated that the instruction to subtract forty-nine years was given for the sake of ease. The correction for the precession of the equinoxes <term>Ayana-samskara: the adjustment required to account for the slow shifting of the Earth's rotational axis</term> is also shown:

> From the years elapsed in the Kalpa, subtract three thousand six hundred. Divide the remainder by five thousand eight hundred and eight.
> With the remainder, the "quadrant" is formed by one thousand four hundred and fifty-two years. The "arm-years" <note>the years elapsed within a specific quadrant of the cycle</note> are multiplied by two.
> Divide by one hundred and thirty-one; the resulting degrees and minutes are treated like the "result of the arm" <term>Bhujaphala: a trigonometric correction</term>.
> Apply this as a negative or positive correction to the fixed points of the equinoxes to make them agree with observation.

In the regions of the quadrants, within the one thousand four hundred and fifty-two years, the "arm-year" is the elapsed portion in an odd quadrant. In an even quadrant, the "arm-year" is the portion yet to come. "Like the result of the arm" means it is negative starting from Aries and positive starting from Libra. This means the correction is negative or positive at the two fixed points of the equinoxes—at three signs <note>90 degrees</note> and nine signs <note>270 degrees</note> respectively. It is said that the end of the solstice occurs when the Sun reaches such a state.

The author now demonstrates the simultaneous beginning of the ages and the infinity of time:

> The Age, Year, Month, and Day all began simultaneously from the first day of the bright half of the month of Chaitra. <gloss>B. 11. 6</gloss>
> This time is without beginning or end; in the field of the sky, it is measured by the planets and the asterisms. || 11 ||

At the beginning of the Age, when all planets were at the starting point of the circle, the Solar and Lunar cycles began exactly at the same time. Time is without beginning or end; it is inferred or measured by the planets and stars situated in the "field"—that is, the celestial sphere. The meaning is this: although time itself has no start or finish...

<summary>This page details mathematical corrections for planetary positions and the precession of the equinoxes, while asserting that time is an infinite cycle measured by the synchronized movements of celestial bodies starting from the month of Chaitra.</summary>
<keywords>Ayana-samskara, precession of the equinoxes, Chaitra, Prakashika, planetary correction, Kalpa, Yuga, cyclical time</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the measurement of time and the motion of celestial bodies according to the Aryabhatiya.</meta>

# On the Aryabhatiya

Even so, time is measured by the celestial bodies situated in the zodiac <note>original: "jyotiścakra"</note> which serve as limiting conditions, taking the forms of an Aeon <note>original: "kalpa," a vast period of time</note>, a Manu-interval <note>original: "manvantara"</note>, an Age <note>original: "yuga"</note>, a year, a month, a day, and so on. Now, the author describes the uniform motion of the planets:

> In sixty solar years, the planets complete the circumference of the stars,
> and in a Divine Age, they complete the circumference of the sky,
> wandering at a uniform speed within their own orbits. || 12 ||

In sixty solar years, all the planets complete the circumference of the stars <note>original: "bha-pariṇāha," the perimeter of the sidereal zodiac</note>. This means that in that amount of time, they travel a distance in leagues <note>original: "yojana," a traditional unit of distance</note> equal to that circumference. "In a Divine Age, the circumference of the sky": in a Divine Age <note>original: "divya-yuga"</note>, which is a period of four ages common to the planets, they complete the circumference of the sky <note>original: "namaḥ-paridhi," the perimeter of the entire celestial sphere</note>. This means they travel a distance in leagues equal to that. "Wandering at a uniform speed within their own orbits": all the planets, wandering an equal number of leagues every day in their own orbits <note>original: "kakṣyā"</note>, complete both the circumference of the stars and the circumference of the sky.

The author now explains that the appearance of slow or fast motion among those who actually move at a uniform speed arises from the difference in their orbits:

> The Moon, being lowest, completes its small orbit in a short time;
> Saturn, being highest of all, completes its great orbit in a long time. || 13 ||

Moving below all the other planets, the Moon completes its own orbit—which consists of few leagues—in a short time. Its orbit is "small" in comparison to the orbits of other planets, and the time is "short" in comparison to the time taken to complete those other orbits. Moving above all the other planets, Saturn <note>original: "śanaiścarī," literally 'the slow-mover'</note> completes its own orbit—which consists of many leagues—in a long time.

The author then explains that the size of the spatial divisions such as signs and degrees follows the size of the respective orbits:

> In a small orbit, the signs are to be known as small, and in a large orbit, large.
> Likewise, the degrees and minutes are equal in their divisions within their own orbits. || 14 ||

<note>The vocabulary list below defines the technical terms used in the Sanskrit text above.</note>

**Zodiacal circle** <note>jyotiścakra</note>, **Aeon** <note>kalpa</note>, **Manu-interval** <note>manvantara</note>, **Age** <note>yuga</note>, **Planet** <note>graha</note>, **Uniform motion** <note>samagati</note>, **Circumference of the stars** <note>bha-pariṇāha</note>, **Circumference of the sky** <note>namaḥ-paridhi</note>, **Orbit** <note>kakṣyā</note>, **League** <note>yojana</note>, **Four-age cycle** <note>caturyuga</note>, **Slow and fast motion** <note>manda-śīghra-gatitva</note>, **Moon** <note>candra</note>, **Saturn** <note>śanaiścarī</note>, **Zodiac sign** <note>rāśi</note>, **Degree** <note>aṃśa</note>, **Minute** <note>kalā</note>.

<summary>This page explains that while all planets travel at the same physical speed (leagues per day), their varying orbital heights cause them to complete their circuits in different amounts of time. It defines the relationship between orbital size and the perceived size of astronomical divisions like degrees and signs.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhata, planetary motion, orbits, yojana, celestial mechanics, Saturn, Moon, Indian astronomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the division of the celestial sphere and the relative positions of the planets within the cosmos.</meta>

# Section on the Reckoning of Time

In a small circular field, the signs of the zodiac and other divisions are small; in a large circular field, the signs and other divisions are large. They are equal in terms of their proportional division within their own orbits <note>The author explains that while the physical size of a zodiac sign changes depending on the size of the orbit, its value as 1/12th of a circle remains constant</note>. A sign is equal to one-twelfth of its own orbit. A degree is a field equal to one-thirtieth of a sign. The same applies to minutes and further divisions. Thus, the signs and other divisions are established as equal parts of their respective orbits. Now, the author describes the order of the planetary orbits situated below the sphere of the fixed stars:

->Below the stars are Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, the Sun, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon.<-
->Below them all is the Earth, situated in the center of space like a hitching post. || 15 ||<-

Below the stars situated in the stellar sphere, Saturn and the others move in their own orbits in the given order. Situated below these planets is the Earth, which stays in the center of space. The Earth is the "hitching post" <term>medhī: a central pillar used in threshing floors</term> for these planets. A *medhī* is a thick stake fixed in the middle of a threshing floor for tying the oxen that tread the grain. Just as oxen, buffalo, and other animals make that stake the center and roam around it, so too the stars and planets make the Earth—situated in the middle of space—the center and roam around it. This is the meaning. Here, the division of "above" and "below" is made in relation to the equatorial region. However, the rotation of the planets around the Earth (acting as the hitching post) is considered in relation to the center of Mount Meru <note>Mount Meru represents the Earth's polar axis in traditional Indian cosmology</note>. By this same order of orbits, the author demonstrates the lordship of the hours and the lordship of the days:

->These seven, starting with Saturn, are the lords of the hours in order of their increasing speed.<- <insert>B~11, 12</insert>
->The fourth in the order of speed from the previous one becomes the lord of the day starting from sunrise. || 16 ||<-

The aforementioned planets, starting with Saturn, are successively "fast" or possess faster velocities <note>Planets closer to Earth appear to move faster through the zodiac</note>. This is established by the order of their orbits. Being thus faster in succession, these planets starting with Saturn are, in that same order, the lords of the hours <term>kālahorā: the planetary hour, a 24th part of a day</term>...

<summary>This page describes the geocentric model of the universe, placing Earth at the center like a hitching post for the planets, and explains how the order of planetary orbits determines the lords of the hours and days.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of planetary motion and timekeeping from the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the sequence of the Lords of the Hours and the geometric models for planetary correction.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>
->On the Aryabhatiya<-

The <term>Lords of the Day</term><note>Sanskrit: Varadhipa; the planet ruling a specific day of the week</note> are determined as follows. The first <term>Hour</term><note>Sanskrit: Hora; a 24th part of a day, from which the English word 'hour' is derived</note> belongs to the Lord of the Day. Then, following the previously mentioned sequence <insert>the order of planets from slowest to fastest</insert>, the first hour of the night belongs to the fifth planet from the Lord of the Day. This continues according to the stated rule. Starting from sunrise, the Lords of the Day follow the fourth planet in the "fast" sequence. For example, following the day of Saturn, the next day's Lord is the Sun, which is the fourth from Saturn in that sequence. Then, for the following day, the Moon is the Lord, being the fourth from the Sun. In this manner, the Lords of the Day succeed one another at sunrise in every fourth position.

Now begins the explanation of <term>True Planetary Computation</term><note>Sanskrit: Sphutikarana; the process of converting a theoretical mean position into an observed true position</note> to account for the discrepancy in visual observation of the <term>Mean Planet</term><note>Sanskrit: Madhyagraha; the planet's position assuming a perfectly circular and uniform orbit</note>. The cause of this visual discrepancy is shown here:

> All planets move in their own orbits within the <term>Deferent</term><note>Sanskrit: Kaksha; the primary circular path</note> and the <term>Epicycle</term><note>Sanskrit: Pratimandala; an auxiliary circle used to model eccentric motion</note>. They move in a direct direction from their <term>Slow Apex</term><note>Sanskrit: Mandocca; similar to apogee, the point where the planet moves slowest</note> and in a retrograde direction from their <term>Fast Apex</term><note>Sanskrit: Shighrocca; the point related to the planet's conjunction with the Sun</note>. || 17 ||

In reality, there is only one true planet. However, because its motion appears irregular, a separate "Mean Planet" with uniform motion is imagined to calculate its position. The Mean Planet always moves at its mean speed along the circle called the Deferent, which is centered on the Earth. The True Planet, however, moves at that same mean speed along a circle called the Epicycle, whose center is offset from the Earth's center. 

Alternatively, one can say: the Mean Planet on the Deferent moves at a mean pace relative to the observer, while the True Planet on the Epicycle moves at its true pace relative to the observer. This is the application. 

Regarding "direct from the Slow Apex": from wherever the Slow Apex is positioned, the planet moves forward day by day according to its <term>Anomaly</term><note>Sanskrit: Kendra-bhukti; the movement relative to the apex</note>. Regarding "retrograde from the Fast Apex": from wherever the Fast Apex is positioned, the planet moves in reverse daily by an amount equal to the difference in its fast motion. 

Next, the dimensions and positions of these Epicycles are described.

# Section on the Reckoning of Time

> The eccentric circle of each of these planets is equal to its own orbital circle. The center of the eccentric circle, however, is shifted away from the center of the solid Earth. || 18 ||

<margin>*) See Dasagitika, verse 4.</margin>
The orbital circle <term>kakṣā-maṇḍala; the mean orbit of a planet</term> for all planets is obtained by dividing the total space of the sky by the number of revolutions. This was previously demonstrated in the verse beginning with "The speed of the planets in a portion of the sky-age..." <gloss>*) referring to the calculation of planetary speeds</gloss>. Each planet's eccentric circle <term>pratimaṇḍala; a circle whose center is offset from the Earth to explain varying speeds</term> is also equal in size to its own orbital circle. The center of the orbital circle lies exactly at the center of the solid Earth—meaning the innermost central point of the terrestrial globe. However, the center of the eccentric circle is shifted away from that center of the solid Earth. <note>The "solid Earth" refers to the physical globe at the center of the universe in this geocentric model.</note> This shift is equal to the radius of the epicycle <term>ucca-nīca-vṛtta; literally 'high-low circle'</term>. He will explain this further: the distance between the center of the eccentric and the center of the Earth is derived from the difference between the mean and true positions of the planet.

> The distance between the center of the eccentric circle and the center of the Earth is the radius of the planet's own epicycle. The planets move at their mean speed along the circumference of that [eccentric] circle. || 19 ||

In the *Gitika* section, the "slow-circle" <term>manda-vṛtta; the epicycle used to calculate the equation of center</term> mentioned is the epicycle used in the "slow-process" <note>manda-karma; the correction for the planet's apsis</note>. The "fast-circle" <term>śīghra-vṛtta</term> mentioned there is the epicycle used in the "fast-process" <note>śīghra-karma; the correction for conjunction/parallax</note>. The radius of this epicycle is also the distance between the center of the eccentric and the center of the Earth. The circles mentioned in the *Gitika* are established through the use of sines, hypotenuses, and observation. Therefore, one should draw the orbital circle with the center of the Earth as the center and the standard radius <term>trijyā; the radius of a circle where the sine of 90 degrees is 3438 minutes</term>. Then, from that center, at a distance equal to the radius of the epicycle, one should set a new center and draw a circle with the same standard radius. That becomes the eccentric circle. The planet is seen in the sky moving along that eccentric circle. The imaginary "mean planet" moves along the orbital circle. Where the mean planet stands on the orbital circle...

<page-num>64</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

Having made the center, one should draw the <term>epicycle</term><note>original: "svocca-nica-vrtta" - literally the 'circle of high and low'</note> with its own radius. Upon its circumference, the planets move in their mean motion. The mean planets move and wander in that circle at the mean rate of speed. The <term>apogees</term><note>original: "ucca" - the highest point of an orbit</note> also move in that circle at their own speed. This is the meaning. In that circle, whatever the magnitude of the <term>sine of the arc</term><note>original: "bhujajya"</note> between the apogee and the mean planet, that same magnitude becomes the sine of the arc between the mean and the true planet in the circle of the radius. There is equality here by geometric measurement, but not by the number of minutes of arc and so on. Having projected the movement of the mean planets and apogees on the epicycle, one should project the interval between the mean and true planets as equal to the sine of the arc arising from the interval between the mean planet and the apogee. This is the meaning. Because the circles mentioned in the *Gitika* section <insert>specifically *Dashagitika* 8</insert> have been reduced by a common factor, the <term>radius</term><note>original: "trijya"</note> should be taken as reduced by that same factor for the purpose of drawing the diagrams. Regarding this, he describes the method of rotation:

->That planet which moves faster than its apogee moves in the opposite direction in its own epicycle;<-
->That planet which moves slower than its apogee moves in the natural direction in its circle. || 20 ||<-

A planet which is faster than its own apogee moves in a <term>retrograde</term><note>original: "pratiloma" - against the natural order</note> direction in the orbit known as its own epicycle. In that case, the known motion is to be considered retrograde. The meaning is that the "sine of motion" is the difference between the mean planet and the true planet which has gone below the mean. A planet which is slower than its own apogee goes in the <term>natural direction</term><note>original: "anuloma" - with the hair/grain, i.e., forward</note> in its circle. In that circle, the known sine of motion is to be considered as being in the natural direction. The meaning is that in that case, the true planet is above the mean. Here, a planet situated in the six signs <note>180 degrees</note> starting from the apogee is called "fast-moving." One situated in the six signs going downwards is called "slow-moving." This is how it should be viewed. The retrograde and natural motion of the true planet relative to the mean has thus been stated. The method of rotation of the epicycle and its—

# He now describes the positions of the planets.

->The planets move in their orbits in a forward direction from their slow apex <note>apogee</note> and in a backward direction from their fast apex <note>perigee</note>.<-

->The mean planet is located at the center of its own epicycle, which is attached to the circular orbit. || 21 ||<-

This means that one should draw the epicycle by taking the point where the mean planet <note>the average position of a planet without corrections</note> is located on the orbital circle as the center.
The true motion <term>sphuta-gati: the actual observed speed of a planet</term> differs every day due to its own height <note>distance from Earth</note>. Therefore, the procedure for calculating the true position is performed to establish that true motion.
In that regard, for the Sun and the Moon, which have only one apex <note>manda-ucca or apogee</note>, there is only one procedure for calculating the true position. For Mars and the others, which have two types of apexes <note>manda and shighra</note>, there are two procedures for calculating the true position.
Even when those two procedures are performed for them, a difference in observation <note>parallax or calculation error</note> can sometimes occur. This occurs because of the difference between the orbital circles and the eccentric circles of the slow and fast motions. To remove that discrepancy, the following is done:
For Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, one first calculates the "slow correction" <term>manda-phala: the equation of center</term> from the mean position and applies it to the mean; then one calculates the "fast correction" <term>shighra-phala: the equation of conjunction</term> from that, applies half of it to that same position, then applies the full slow correction to the original mean, and finally applies the full fast correction to that result. That is the true planet.
However, for Mercury and Venus, one first calculates the fast correction from the mean, applies half of it inversely to the slow apex, subtracts that slow apex from the mean to calculate the slow correction, applies that in full to the mean, and then applies the full fast correction to that result. That would be the true planet.
He demonstrates all of this through three verses. There, he first states the division of negative and positive corrections <note>subtraction and addition</note> with a half-verse, and explains the procedure for the true position with the remaining two and a half verses.

->The corrections are negative, positive, positive, and negative relative to the slow apex; they are the opposite relative to the fast apex.<-

"From the slow apex" means from the mean planet minus the slow apex. The sines produced from that, according to the sequence of the quadrants, are negative, positive, positive, and negative <note>subtracted in the 1st and 4th quadrants, added in the 2nd and 3rd</note>. "The opposite relative to the fast apex" means the sines produced from the fast apex minus the mean planet are...

# In the Aryabhatiya

...by inversion, they become positive, negative, and positive respectively. This is what is being said: In the first quadrant, the result of the sine of the slow anomaly <note>manda-bhuja: the arc used to calculate the slow equation</note> is negative. In the second quadrant, the result of the versed sine of the complement is negative. When processed with the full result of the sine of the arc reached in the third quadrant, it is negative. However, in the rapid correction <note>shighra: the correction for the planet's orbital position relative to the Sun</note>, this occurs with an inversion of the positive and negative signs. In the slow process, starting from Aries <note>the first 180 degrees of the anomaly</note>, the result is negative; starting from Libra <note>the latter 180 degrees</note>, it is positive. In the rapid process, because of the rule of subtracting the mean position from the apex, it is positive starting from Aries and negative starting from Libra.

->For Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars, half of the slow correction is negative or positive in the first step. (22)<-
->From the slow apex, half of the rapid correction is negative or positive for the "slow" planets.<-
->From the slow apex, the "true-mean" positions are known; from the rapid apex, the "true" positions are known. (23)<-

For Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars, half of the result of the slow sine—derived from the slow apex—is negative starting from Aries and positive starting from Libra. This applies "in the first" step of the calculation for true position. This means that half of the slow result, derived from the mean position minus the slow apex, should be applied to the mean position as negative or positive according to the rule. 

"Half of the rapid correction is negative or positive for the slow planets": One should apply half of the rapid result—produced from the rapid apex minus the mean position (which has already been adjusted by half the slow result)—to those "slow" planets, namely the mean positions of Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars that were previously adjusted by half the slow result. 

"From the slow apex, the true-mean positions": This means from the adjustment of the full slow result derived from the slow apex. After the mean position is adjusted by both half the slow result and half the rapid result, one subtracts the slow apex from it; the full slow result produced from that is then applied to the original mean position. This result is called the "true-mean" <term>sphuta-madhya: the intermediate corrected longitude</term>. This is how the true-mean positions of Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars are found. 

"From the rapid apex, the true positions are known": The full rapid result is produced from the rapid apex minus the true-mean position. When the true-mean is adjusted by that full rapid result, it becomes the "true planet" <term>sphuta-graha: the final corrected celestial longitude</term>. Thus, for Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars...

# Page 67
## Section on the Reckoning of Time

...should be known as true.

->Subtract or add half of the <term>shighrochcha</term> <note>the "fast apex" or anomaly of conjunction</note> to its own <term>mandochcha</term> <note>the "slow apex" or apogee</note>.<-
->The true-mean positions of Venus and Mercury are then derived from this established apogee. || 24 ||<-

For Venus and Mercury, one should take half of the <term>shighraphala</term> <note>the correction for the planet's second inequality</note>—which is produced from the <term>shighrochcha</term> minus the mean position—and subtract it from its own <term>mandochcha</term> if it is in the signs beginning with Aries, or add it if it is in the signs beginning with Libra. This is the meaning of the "fast-method" verification. The "true-mean" positions of Venus and Mercury are thus established from this modified <term>manda</term> <note>slow</note> process. In this way, the mean positions of Venus and Mercury, when corrected by the full <term>mandaphala</term> <note>the correction for the planet's first inequality/eccentricity</note> derived from that modified apogee, become what are called the "true-mean" positions. To clarify: the <term>mandochcha</term> is corrected by half the <term>shighraphala</term>, then subtracted from the mean position; the resulting full <term>mandaphala</term> is applied to the mean to produce the <term>madhyasphuta</term> <note>true-mean</note> position.

The method for calculating these corrections is as follows:
Multiply the <term>bhujajya</term> <note>the sine of the arc of the anomaly</note> of the <term>mandakendra</term> <note>slow anomaly</note> by the <term>mandasphutavritta</term> <note>the corrected epicycle of the slow anomaly</note>, divide by eighty, and the arc of the resulting value is the <term>mandaphala</term>.

Similarly, multiply the <term>bhujajya</term> of the <term>shighrakendra</term> <note>fast anomaly</note> by the <term>shighrasphutavritta</term> <note>the corrected epicycle of the fast anomaly</note> and divide by eighty. Multiply that result by the <term>vyasardha</term> <note>the radius of the base circle, usually 3438 minutes</note> and divide by the <term>shighrakarna</term> <note>the hypotenuse of the fast anomaly</note>. The arc of that result is the <term>shighraphala</term>.

To find the <term>karna</term> <note>hypotenuse</note>:
One should multiply the <term>bhujajya</term> and the <term>kotijya</term> <note>the cosine of the anomaly</note> produced from the respective anomaly by its own epicycle and divide by eighty. The results are the <term>bhujaphala</term> and <term>kotiphala</term>. Add the <term>kotiphala</term> to the <term>vyasardha</term> in the signs beginning with Capricorn; subtract the <term>kotiphala</term> from the <term>vyasardha</term> in the signs beginning with Cancer. Square that result, add the square of the <term>bhujaphala</term>, and take the square root. That is the <term>karna</term>.

In this manner, the <term>shighrakarna</term> is made true with a single calculation. However, the <term>mandakarna</term> <note>the hypotenuse for the slow anomaly</note> requires a more specific refinement. The method for that is: multiply the first calculated hypotenuse by the <term>bhujaphala</term> and <term>kotiphala</term> and divide by the <term>vyasardha</term>. The results are the <term>bhujaphala</term> and <term>kotiphala</term> as established by the hypotenuse. Again, from these two, calculate the hypotenuse using the <term>vyasardha</term> as before. That hypotenuse also... <note>text continues to next page</note>

Page 68
# On the Aryabhatiya

By multiplying the results of the sine and cosine <note>original: "bhujā-koṭi-phala"; the horizontal and vertical components of the planetary position</note> and dividing by the radius <note>original: "vyāsārdha"</note>, one should calculate the sine-result and cosine-result. From these two, the hypotenuse <note>original: "karṇa"</note> should be derived. This process should be repeated until the value of the hypotenuse remains unchanged through iteration. This final, unchanging value is the "true slow-hypotenuse" <note>original: "manda-karṇa-sphuṭa"; the distance from the center to the planet in its slow/apsis cycle</note>.

Regarding the operation of the circles: multiply by the difference between the odd and even quadrant circles, divide by the radius, and the result should be added to or subtracted from the odd quadrant circle. If the value is less than the other in the odd circle, add it; if it is more, subtract it. That becomes the "true circle." All of this has been demonstrated through the principles of the orbit and the epicycle <note>original: "kakṣyā-pratimaṇḍala"</note>.

->The logic of the procedure for true positions cannot be established for scholars without a diagram.<-
->Therefore, the process of diagrammatic construction is briefly shown here for them.<-
->A circle with the radius of the Earth as its center is the orbit; that is the "fast" circle.<-
->Its center is in the direction of the "fast" anomaly; then, at a distance of the maximum "fast" result, another center is placed.<-
->Having drawn this, it is called the "fast-epicycle" <note>original: "śīghra-pratimaṇḍala"</note>.<-
->This same circle becomes the orbit in the "slow" <note>manda</note> process; then from its center,<-
->at a distance of the maximum "slow" result in the direction of the "slow" anomaly, another center is made.<-
->One should draw the epicycle; this is called the "slow" circle for Saturn, Mercury, and Mars <note>original: "śanī-jña-bhūputrāḥ"</note>.<-
->While the planets move on the "slow" epicycle, where they are observed on that orbit,<-
->there their "slow-corrected" positions are declared; likewise in the "fast" process.<-
->They are situated on the epicycle, but are observed again on the "fast" orbit.<-
->In whichever degree of the orbital circle they appear, there the "true planets" <note>original: "sphuṭa-grahāḥ"</note> are located.<-

<page-num>61</page-num>
# Section on the Measurement of Time

Thus it is established that there is a difference in appearance <note>parallax or observational discrepancy</note> in the true conjunction. Because the planets are observed in one place, yet they are actually located in another.

For this reason, half of the slow correction <term>mandardha: the correction for the planet's elliptical eccentricity</term> and half of the fast correction <term>sighrardha: the correction for the Earth's orbital motion</term> are applied here. Elsewhere, the sequence is remembered as: fast, slow, slow, and then fast.

First, for Mercury and Venus, the center of the Earth is the center of the slow orbit <term>manda-kakshya-vritta</term>. From that center, in the direction of the slow apex <note>apogee</note>, the mean position is located at a distance equal to the maximum slow correction.

The center of the fast orbit <term>sighra-pratimandala</term> is located where the Sun is positioned within that slow epicycle; its dimensions have also been stated.

Mercury and Venus always move within that circle, which is equal to their own fast orbits. The true conjunction is as before, and the difference in appearance is to be understood here as previously described.

For this purpose, half of the fast correction is applied inversely to the slow apex. This was established as the slow correction first and the fast correction afterward by the ancient sages.

Thus. To calculate the distance between the Earth and the star-planets <note>the five planets: Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn</note>, he says:

> The distance between the Earth and a star-planet is the product of their own hypotenuses divided by the radius. The velocity of a planet in its orbit is that which occurs at the slow perigee and apogee. // 25 //

The product of the fast hypotenuse <term>sighra-karna</term> and the slow hypotenuse <term>manda-karna</term>, which are derived from the final and penultimate true steps, divided by the radius, is the distance between the Earth and the star-planet. This means the interval between the Earth and the star-planets is expressed in minutes of arc. In the calculation of the celestial latitude <term>vikshepa</term> of the star-planets, this distance between the Earth and the star-planet serves as the divisor. There, from its own node—

<sig>17</sig>

<summary>This page explains the geometric corrections for the orbits of Mercury and Venus and provides the mathematical formula for calculating the distance between the Earth and the planets using the product of two hypotenuses.</summary>
<keywords>planetary distance, Mercury, Venus, epicycle, hypotenuse, celestial latitude, parallax, Aryabhata</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Aryabhatiya, specifically concluding the section on Time Reckoning and beginning the section on the Celestial Sphere.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>On the Aryabhatiya</header>

Multiply the sine of the latitude by its own maximum latitude and divide by its own distance between the Earth and the planet <note>original: "bhutara-graha-vivarena"; this refers to the geocentric distance</note>. The result obtained there is the planet's own celestial latitude <term>vikshepa: the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</term>. Regarding its application in the orbit: here the author of the *Prakashika* <note>A specific commentary on the Aryabhatiya</note> says: "In an orbit constructed with a radius equal to the distance between the Earth and the planet, that which is the planet's position is at the slow apogee and perigee <note>original: "manda-nicocca"</note>. In an orbit of such magnitude, the planet moves with its slow-corrected velocity." However, this does not seem correct to us. Alternatively, the interpretation is: in the orbital circle, it is beyond the mean position of the corrected planet. The same applies to the fast <note>shighra</note> correction. Or: for a planet moving in its orbit, the radius of the slow epicycle is equal to the maximum motion outside or inside the eccentric circle. The same applies to the fast correction.

->Thus ends the third chapter, the Section on Time Reckoning, in Parameshvara's *Bhatadipika*<-

---

### Now begins the Section on the Celestial Sphere. Therein, he describes the arrangement of the ecliptic.

> One half of the ecliptic, from the beginning of Aries to the end of Virgo, deviates equally to the north.
> The remaining half, from the beginning of Libra to the end of Pisces, deviates to the south. || 1 ||

The half of the ecliptic characterized by the signs from the beginning of Aries to the end of Virgo is deviated to the north. The remaining half, characterized by the signs from the beginning of Libra to the end of Pisces, is deviated to the south. It deviates "equally." This is what is meant: it deviates equally in the direct order from the beginning of Aries and in the inverse order from the end of Virgo. The deviation of Virgo is equal to that of Aries; the deviation of Leo is equal to that of Taurus, and so on. For the deviation of the circle occurs in a regular sequence. Similarly, the deviation of Pisces is equal to that of Libra; the deviation of Aquarius is equal to that of Scorpio, and so on. From the beginning of Aries and from the end of Virgo...

<summary>This page concludes Parameshvara's commentary on the "Kala-kriya-pada" (Time Reckoning) and begins the "Gola-pada" (Celestial Sphere), explaining the northern and southern declination of the ecliptic relative to the celestial equator.</summary>
<keywords>Bhatadipika, Section on Time, Section on the Sphere, Ecliptic, Aries, Virgo, Libra, Pisces, Celestial Latitude</keywords>

# Section on the Sphere
<page-num>71</page-num>
->**The Section on the Sphere**<-

<margin>*) Verse 3.</margin>
The maximum declination <note>The greatest distance of the sun from the celestial equator</note> occurs at a distance of three signs <note>90 degrees</note>. The maximum declination is twenty-four degrees. This was stated in the *Gitika* verse as "the declination of the ecliptic is the parts of the planets" <note>original: "bhāpakramo grahāṃśā iti"</note>. Here, the words "beginning with Aries" and "ending with Virgo" refer to the signs and degrees that have passed the eastern and western horizons <note>literally: the eastern and western "swastikas" or cardinal points</note>. Therefore, when the additive degrees of the precession of the equinoxes <note><term>Ayana-samskara</term>: the correction for the shifting of the Earth's axis</note> are fifteen, the middle of Pisces is at the eastern horizon and the middle of Virgo is at the western horizon. At that time, from the middle of Pisces to the middle of Virgo, that half of the sphere is deviated to the north, and the remaining half is deviated to the south. When the degrees of precession are fifteen and subtractive, the middle of Aries is at the eastern horizon and the middle of Libra is at the western horizon. Then, from the middle of Aries to the middle of Libra, that half is deviated to the north and the other half to the south. This is how it should be understood. For this reason, the precession correction is applied to the calculation of declination starting from Aries. Now, the author describes those who move along the ecliptic:

->**The nodes of the star-planets and the Moon, and the Sun, wander constantly on the ecliptic.**<-
->**And at a distance of half a circle from the Sun, the shadow of the Earth wanders upon it. || 2 ||**<-

The nodes <note>the points where an orbit crosses the ecliptic</note> of the star-planets <note>Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn</note>, the nodes of the Moon, and the Sun always move along the ecliptic <note><term>Apamandala</term>: the "circle of declination" or the Sun's path</note>. At a distance of half a circle <note>180 degrees</note> from the Sun, the Earth's shadow always moves on the ecliptic. The Moon, Mars, and the others move in their own respective orbits of celestial latitude <note><term>Vikshepa-mandala</term>: the inclined orbit of a planet</note>. He now describes the position of the orbit of latitude:

->**From the node on the ecliptic, the Moon goes to the north and to the south;**<-
->**Jupiter, Mars, and Saturn do the same, as do Mercury and Venus via their conjunctions. || 3 ||**<-

When the true Moon is equal to its node located on the ecliptic, the Moon is then moving exactly on the ecliptic.

# On the Aryabhatiya
<page-num>72</page-num>

From there, it moves gradually northward. At a distance of three signs <note>90 degrees</note> from the node <term>pata; the point where the orbit crosses the ecliptic</term>, the maximum elevation of latitude occurs. When the Moon is at a distance of six signs <note>180 degrees</note> from the node, it moves exactly along the ecliptic <term>apamandala; the sun's path</term>. Indeed, the position of the second node is said to be there.

From that second node, it moves gradually southward. There too, at a distance of three signs from the node, the southward movement is equal to the maximum latitude. In this way, the position of the celestial circle that supports the Moon's latitude is described. The maximum latitude is said to be "half" <note>original: "sardha"</note> <note>*) In the Dashagitika, verse 8.</note>. This means four and a half degrees.

Jupiter, Mars, and Saturn <note>original: "Guru-Kuja-Kona"</note> are also like this. Just as the Moon, when its slow-corrected position <term>mandasphuta; the planet's position corrected for its elliptical orbit</term> is equal to its node, moves on the ecliptic, so too do Jupiter, Mars, and Saturn move on the ecliptic when their slow-corrected positions are equal to their respective nodes. From there, they move gradually northward. At a distance of three signs from the node in their slow-corrected position, the maximum elevation of latitude occurs. At a distance of six signs from the node in their slow-corrected position, they move on the ecliptic. From there, they move gradually southward. There too, at a distance of three signs, the southward movement is equal to the maximum latitude. Such is the arrangement of the latitude-supporting circles for the slow-moving planets: Jupiter, Mars, and Saturn.

Mercury and Venus [move] by means of their fast-anomaly <term>shighrochcha; the correction related to the Earth's motion or the planet's orbital speed</term>. Mercury and Venus move above and below the ecliptic according to their fast-anomaly. The word "also" <note>original: "api"</note> implies this is also influenced by the slow-corrected position. This is what is meant: for Mercury and Venus, one should apply the slow-correction <term>mandaphala</term> inversely to their fast-anomaly, subtract their node from that, and then calculate the latitude. Therefore, when the fast-anomaly, refined by the slow-correction, is equal to their node, they move on the ecliptic. From there, they gradually rise northward. When the fast-anomaly is three signs from the node, the maximum elevation of latitude occurs; at six signs, they move on the ecliptic. From there, they move gradually southward. There too, at three signs, the southward movement is equal to the maximum latitude. In this way, the latitude circles of all [planets] are situated upon the ecliptic...

---

*) In the Dashagitika, verse 8.
