--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary motion, specifically focusing on the calculation of celestial latitude and the visibility of planets relative to the Sun.</meta>

# Section on the Sphere

The celestial latitude is tied to the two nodes <note>the points where a planet's path crosses the ecliptic</note>. At a distance of three signs <note>90 degrees</note> from these nodes, the maximum latitude occurs to the north or south of the ecliptic. The maximum latitudes for Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars are half of 2, 2, and 3 degrees respectively <note>original: "kha-ka-ga-ardha" using the Aryabhatiya's number-notation system</note>; for Venus and Mercury, they are 2 degrees. Some teachers calculate the latitude by applying the "fast equation" <term>Śīghrocca-phala: the correction for the planet's orbital position relative to the Earth</term> to the nodes of Jupiter, Mars, and Saturn, just as they do for the planets themselves. They then subtract this corrected node from the true planet to find the latitude. For Mercury and Venus, they apply the "slow equation" <term>Mandaphala: the correction for the planet's elliptical eccentricity</term> to the node and subtract that from the "fast anomaly." As the teacher Lalla says:

> The nodes of Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn are corrected by their respective "fast equations"; likewise, the nodes of Mercury and Venus are made true when corrected by their "slow equations."

In this view, the latitude is derived from the true planet minus the node. Now, the author explains the knowledge of the rising and setting <note>heliacal rising and setting</note> of the Moon and other planets, which is caused by their distance from or proximity to the Sun.

<margin>Manuscript B~612</margin>
> The Moon is visible when situated at an interval of twelve degrees from the Sun, provided it has no latitude. Venus is visible at nine degrees. The others are visible at intervals increasing by two degrees each, in order of their decreasing size. || 4 ||

The Moon, when without latitude, is visible at twelve degrees from the Sun. Venus is visible at nine degrees of time <note>original: "navabhiḥ kālāṃśaiḥ"</note>. This refers to units of time. Jupiter is visible at two degrees more than Venus—that is, at eleven degrees. Mercury is visible at two more than that—at thirteen degrees. Saturn is visible at two more than that—at fifteen degrees. Mars is visible at two more than that—at seventeen degrees. "In order of their decreasing size" <note>original: "yathā-ślakṣṇāḥ"</note> means according to their increasing fineness or smallness. Jupiter is smaller <note>in apparent diameter</note> than Venus; Mercury is smaller than that; then Saturn; then Mars. Thus Venus, Jupiter, Mercury, Saturn, and Mars—

---

*) As stated in the *Ten Gītikā Verses*, verse 6.

<sig>18</sig>

<summary>This page details the methods for calculating planetary latitude (vikṣepa) using nodes and corrections, and defines the specific angular distances from the Sun required for the heliacal rising of the Moon and the five visible planets.</summary>
<keywords>celestial latitude, nodes, planetary visibility, heliacal rising, Lalla, Aryabhatiya, degrees of time, astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on planetary visibility and the physical nature of celestial bodies from the previous section of the Aryabhatiya.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

...the degrees of visibility <note>original: "si-u-a-pa-na-ma-am-sha-ka"; a mnemonic from the Dashagitika for planetary visibility limits</note> and the calculation for subtraction have been stated. When a planet is "conquered" <note>meaning visible or distinct</note>, it becomes visible when the distance between the Sun and the planet (after applying the visibility corrections) matches the numbers taught by the physicians <note>referring to expert astronomers</note>. Now, the author explains the cause of light for the Earth and other bodies which do not possess light of their own.

<margin>Ba 11.39</margin>
->**The halves of the spheres of the Earth, the planets, and the stars are dark due to their own shadows.**<-
->**The other halves, facing the Sun, are bright according to their respective sizes. || 5 ||**<-

The halves of the spheres—that is, the halves of the naturally round bodies—of the Earth, of the planets like the Moon, and of the "Bhas" <note>the stars of the zodiac starting with Ashvini and other stars</note>, are "colorless" or dark due to their own shadows. This means they are non-luminous by nature. Alternatively, it means they are dark because of the shadow—a form of darkness—produced by their own bodies obstructing the rays of the Sun. The other halves, which face the Sun, shine "according to their essence" <note>original: "yathasaram"</note>. This means small bodies appear with a small amount of light, and large bodies appear with a great amount of light.

Furthermore, half of the Moon is always luminous. On the day of the New Moon <note>Amavasya</note>, the upper half of the Moon is the luminous part; therefore, that half is invisible to us. From the first day of the lunar fortnight onwards, the white portion gradually hangs downward. On the Full Moon day, the entire part facing us becomes white; therefore, the half visible to us is completely bright. Even though Mercury and Venus are below the Sun, their disks always appear bright because of their proximity to the Sun and the great size of the solar orb. Now, he describes the arrangement of the orbits and the Earth.

->**In the center of the circular cage of the stars, surrounded by the orbits and situated in the middle of space,**<-
->**is the globe of the Earth, made of earth, water, fire, and air, and perfectly round. || 6 ||**<-

---

<note>*) Dashagitika, verse 5.</note>

<summary>This page explains that the Earth, planets, and stars are non-luminous spheres that only shine by reflecting sunlight, and describes the Earth as a multi-elemental sphere suspended in the center of the celestial orbits.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhata, planetary light, lunar phases, Earth's composition, celestial spheres, astronomy, shadow, reflection</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Golapada (Section on the Sphere) of the Aryabhatiya, specifically discussing the position and nature of the Earth within the celestial sphere.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>Section on the Sphere</header>

The "cage of stars" refers to the orbit of the asterisms <note>original: "bhapañjaro nakṣatrakakṣā"</note>. In the center of this circular orbital path of the stars sits the Earth. It is surrounded by these orbits. This means it is situated in the middle of the orbits of the Moon, Sun, and other planets. It is at the center of the sky, which is defined by the shell of the universe <note>original: "brahmāṇḍa-kaṭāha"</note>. This terrestrial globe, composed of earth, water, fire, and air, is perfectly spherical on all sides. The reason for restating the Earth's position—already famous as being "in the middle of the stars"—is to demonstrate the movement of living beings; specifically, to show that living beings move everywhere upon such an Earth. He demonstrates that movement of beings as follows:

> Just as the bulb of a Kadamba flower is covered on all sides by blossoms,
> so too is the terrestrial globe covered by all living beings, both aquatic and terrestrial. || 7 ||

Just as the flower-bulb of the tree called the Kadamba is covered on all sides—on the upper part, the lower part, and the sides—with blossoms, so too is the spherical Earth covered everywhere by all aquatic beings and all terrestrial beings. The meaning is that stationary and moving things, as well as rivers and lakes, exist everywhere on the Earth. Now, he describes the growth and decay of the Earth occurring over a *Kalpa* <term>Kalpa: a vast cosmic cycle or "Day of Brahma"</term>.

> During a Day of Brahma, the Earth increases by one league <term>Yojana: a traditional Indian measure of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term> on its outer surface.
> During a Night of Brahma, which is equal in length to the Day, the Earth—which had accumulated soil—suffers an equal loss. || 8 ||

During a Day of Brahma, there is an increase of one league on the surface of the Earth. This means the increase happens all around. During a night equal to the day—that is, the Night of Brahma—there is a loss of the same amount from the Earth that had been built up with soil. This means a loss of one league occurs. Therefore, at the beginning of a *Kalpa*, the diameter <note>original: "viṣkambha"</note> of the Earth is one thousand and fifty leagues. In the intervening periods, the size should be calculated by proportion. This is what has been stated. Regarding the eastward motion of the Earth and of the stars...

<meta>The text breaks off discussing the relative motion of the Earth and the stars, continuing the argument for the Earth's rotation or the appearance thereof.</meta>

<summary>This page explains the Earth's central position in the universe and uses the famous Kadamba flower analogy to describe how life exists all over its spherical surface. It also details the cosmic growth and decay of the Earth's diameter over the course of a Day and Night of Brahma.</summary>
<keywords>Earth's sphericity, Kadamba flower analogy, Day of Brahma, Yojana, Earth's diameter, cosmic cycles, Aryabhatiya commentary</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical discourse of the Aryabhatiya, specifically addressing the relative motion of stars and the physical description of Mount Meru.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

Some people believe in the absence of motion <note>referring to the Earth's rotation</note>; he explains that this is due to false perception.

<margin>B ~ 11. 17</margin>
->**Just as a man in a boat moving forward sees stationary objects moving backward,**<-
->**so do the stationary stars appear to move straight toward the west in Lanka.**<- <note>Lanka here refers to a hypothetical point on the equator used as a prime meridian in Indian astronomy.</note> **|| 9 ||**

Just as a man situated in a boat, while traveling in the boat, moving in a forward direction toward his intended western destination, sees stationary objects—such as trees and mountains on both banks of the river—as if they were moving in the opposite direction toward the east; in the same way, the stars, which are actually stationary, appear to those in Lanka to be moving straight toward the west. It is only at the equator, such as in Lanka, that the cage of stars <term>Bhapinjara: the celestial sphere or "cage" of constellations</term> appears to move directly westward. Thus, while acknowledging the perception of the stars moving westward as being due to false knowledge, the eastward motion of the Earth is asserted. However, the underlying meaning is that in ultimate reality, the Earth remains steady. He now states the cause of the rotation of the celestial sphere:

->**Driven by the Provahe wind for the sake of rising and setting,**<-
->**the cage of stars, along with the planets, rotates constantly toward the west of Lanka.**<- **|| 10 ||**

The cage of stars—the celestial sphere consisting of the zodiac—which is the cause of the rising and setting of the Sun and other bodies, is constantly driven by the wind known as *Pravaha* <note>The "pro-vaha" or "forward-carrying" wind believed to drive the celestial bodies</note>. It rotates toward the west of Lanka along with the planets. He now describes the dimensions and nature of Meru:

->**Meru is one yojana in size, a source of light, surrounded by the Himavat mountains;**<-
->**situated in the middle of the Nandana forest, it is made of jewels and is circular on all sides.**<- **|| 11 ||**

Meru is one *yojana* <note>a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</note> in height and equally wide. It is circular on all sides, and because it is composed of jewels, it is a source of light...

<term>Eṇākara</term><note>Literally "the deer-marked," a poetic name for the Moon</note>. It is surrounded by the <term>Himavata</term><note>The Himalayas</note> mountain range and sits in the middle of the <term>Nandanavana</term><note>The celestial grove or "Garden of Delight"</note>. It is said that <term>Meru</term><note>The sacred five-peaked mountain considered to be the center of all the physical, metaphysical and spiritual universes</note> emerges above and through the middle of the Earth. Thus also says Maya <margin>original: "Sūryasiddhānta, Chapter on the Earth, verses 32-34"</margin>:

> ->In the middle of the cosmic egg, the Earth-sphere stands in space,<-
> ->Bearing the supreme power of Brahma which has the nature of support.<-
> ->Within its interior layers are the beautiful underworlds of <term>Pātāla</term>,<-
> ->The refuge of the <term>Nāgas</term> and <term>Asuras</term>, filled with divine medicinal juices.<-
> ->A mountain made of <term>Jāmbūnada</term> gold, a heap of many jewels,<-
> ->Is Meru, passing through the middle of the Earth-sphere and emerging at both ends.<-

Thus it is said. He now describes the locations of Meru and <term>Vaḍavāmukha</term><note>The "Mare's Mouth," a mythical fire-breathing vent at the South Pole</note>:

> ->The gods' Meru is in the middle of the land; the <term>Naraka</term> <insert>Hell</insert> and Vaḍavāmukha are in the middle of the water.<-
> ->The immortals and the mortals certainly consider each other to be situated underneath. || 12 ||<-

The half of the Earth belonging to the Meru side is designated as "land" due to the abundance of land. The half belonging to Vaḍavāmukha is designated as "water" due to the abundance of water. There, in the middle of the land, are Meru and Heaven. In the middle of the water are Naraka and Vaḍavāmukha. The "immortals" are the inhabitants of Heaven. The "mortals" <note>here referring to those in the lower regions</note> are the inhabitants of Naraka. The inhabitants of Heaven think, "The inhabitants of Naraka are situated underneath us." And the inhabitants of Naraka likewise think, "The inhabitants of Heaven are situated underneath us."

->Situated above that are the gods, including Indra, and the Great Sages.<-

# On the Aryabhatiya
<page-num>78</page-num>

->Likewise, the demons <note>Asuras</note> dwell below, both groups <note>gods and demons</note> hating each other but supported by one another.<-

This is the end of the verse <note>original: "iti"</note>; "of that Meru" <note>Mount Meru, the North Pole</note> is the implied subject.

->Then, all around the circumference, this great ocean gradually<-
->stands like a girdle around the Earth, dividing the gods and the demons.<-

And so it is said. At the junction of the land and water portions, he <note>Aryabhata</note> describes four cities situated at intervals of one-quarter of the Earth's circumference around the globe:

->That which is sunrise in Lanka is sunset for the Sun in Siddhapura;<-
->it is midday in Yavakoti and midnight in the Romaka region. || 13 ||<-

Lanka is located in the southern direction <note>The author refers to the equatorial point south of India</note>. What is sunrise there—meaning, at the time the sun rises—that same moment is sunset in Siddhapura. By this, it is stated that the city named Siddhapura is located in the northern direction <note>The "North" here refers to the opposite side of the globe, 180 degrees away</note>. That same sunrise in Lanka would be midday in Yavakoti; meaning, it is the time of noon there. By this, it is stated that the city named Yavakoti is in the eastern direction. In the Romaka region <note>likely referring to the Roman West</note>, that same sunrise is midnight. By this, it is stated that the city is located in the western direction. Thus, Maya <note>the traditional author of the Surya-Siddhanta</note> also says:

->Equidistant from the center of Meru, in the middle of the ocean,<-
->in the islands of the cardinal directions starting from the East, are the cities built by the gods.<-

<meta>This page continues the astronomical descriptions of the Earth's geography and the positioning of major cities relative to the prime meridian, following the established cosmological model of the Sphere.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>Section on the Celestial Sphere</header>

->At a quarter of the Earth's circumference to the east, in the region of Bhadrasva, lies the famous city of Yavakoti, adorned with golden walls and gates.<-
->To the south, in the region of Bharata <note>India</note>, is the great city of Lanka.<-
->To the west, in the region known as Ketumala, the city called Romaka <note>likely referring to the Roman West or Byzantium</note> is celebrated.<-
->To the north, in the region of Kuru, is situated the city named Siddhapuri, where the great perfected souls <term>Siddhas: enlightened beings possessing supernatural powers</term> dwell, free from all suffering.<-

Thus it is said. Because the Sun wanders all around, the division of directions like East and West is established here relative to Lanka and the position of Meru <note>the North Pole</note>, accounting for the difference in time across various countries. The author now describes the distance between Meru and Lanka, between the Badavamukha <note>the South Pole; literally "the mare's mouth"</note> and Lanka, and the distance between Lanka and Ujjayini <note>modern Ujjain, the historical prime meridian of Indian astronomy</note>.

->Lanka is located at one-quarter of the Earth's circumference from the center of the land and water.<-
->Ujjayini is situated exactly north of Lanka, at one-fourth of that quarter-distance. (14)<-

Lanka is situated at a distance of one-quarter of the Earth's circumference from the center of the land, which is the location of Meru. Similarly, Lanka is one-quarter of the circumference away from the center of the water, which is the location of the Badavamukha. Like Lanka, the regions of Siddhapuri, Yavakoti, and Romaka are also located at one-quarter of the Earth's circumference from the centers of land and water. At one-fourth of the distance of Lanka's northern direction—that is, one-fourth of a quarter of the circumference, or one-sixteenth of the total Earth's circumference—lies the city named Ujjayini. Some say that Ujjayini is at one-fifteenth of the Earth's circumference exactly north of Lanka. They have shown the distance as follows:

->Avanti <note>another name for Ujjayini</note> is north of Lanka, at one-fifteenth of the Earth's circumference.<-

<summary>This page defines the four cardinal cities of the ancient Indian geographic system—Yavakoti, Lanka, Romaka, and Siddhapuri—and specifies the latitudinal position of Ujjain relative to the equator.</summary>
<keywords>Yavakoti, Lanka, Romaka, Siddhapuri, Ujjayini, Earth's circumference, Meru, Badavamukha, prime meridian, Indian geography</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical exposition of the Aryabhatiya, specifically discussing the visibility of the celestial sphere from different vantage points on Earth.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
# In the Aryabhatiya

Thus says Brahmagupta <note>A famous 7th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer who often critiqued Aryabhata</note>. Now, he describes the visible and invisible portions of the celestial sphere for those situated on the surface of the Earth:

->**The visible portion of the celestial sphere from a level place is half the sphere minus the Earth's semi-diameter.**<-
->**The half hidden by the Earth is increased by that same semi-diameter. (15)**<-

From a level place—that is, a location on the Earth's surface free from obstructions like mountains—the upper half of the celestial sphere <note>original: "jyotiścakra," literally the 'wheel of lights' or starry sphere</note> is visible, but it is diminished by a portion equal to the Earth's semi-diameter <note>The observer's height on the surface of the globe slightly shifts the horizon compared to the center of the Earth</note>. The other half, increased by the Earth's semi-diameter, is hidden by the Earth and remains invisible.

This is what is meant: Of the two quarters of the celestial sphere, in the eastern part, a portion equal to the Earth's semi-diameter is invisible to us due to the obstruction of the Earth's surface. Similarly, in the western part, a portion equal to the Earth's semi-diameter is also invisible to us. Therefore, the upper half, minus those two portions, is visible to those situated on a level spot on the Earth's surface. The other half, joined with those two portions, is invisible to those on the Earth's surface because it is hidden by the Earth.

Next, he describes the portions of the celestial sphere visible to the Gods and the Demons:

->**The Gods, situated at Meru in the north, see half of the celestial sphere moving to the right.**<-
->**The Spirits at the Badavamukha in the south see the other half moving to the left. (16)**<-

The Gods, situated at the northern Meru <note>The mythological North Pole</note>, see the half of the celestial sphere moving to the right <note>clockwise</note> from the perspective of a person at Lanka <note>The equator</note> facing the celestial sphere. This refers to the northern half. The Spirits <note>original: "preta," often referring to the deceased or inhabitants of the lower realms</note> dwelling in the underworld, situated at the southern Badavamukha <note>The mythological South Pole</note>, see the southern half moving to the left <note>counter-clockwise</note>.

The meaning is: the Gods see the northern half beginning with Aries; the inhabitants of the underworld see the southern half beginning with Libra. Some explain it thus: the Gods at Meru see the northern half of the celestial sphere as "right-moving" (clockwise).

<summary>This page explains how the Earth's radius affects the visible horizon for an observer and defines the different hemispheric perspectives of the celestial sphere from the North and South Poles.</summary>
<keywords>celestial sphere, Earth's radius, horizon, Meru, Badavamukha, visibility, northern hemisphere, southern hemisphere</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the celestial sphere (Golapada), specifically focusing on the relative perspectives of different beings regarding the movement of the sun and the length of their days.</meta>

# Section on the Sphere

The demons see the southern half in a counter-clockwise direction <note>original: "apasavyam"</note>. And so says Brahmagupta <note>A famous 7th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note>:

->The gods always see the northern half of the ecliptic, beginning with Aries, moving clockwise.<-
->The demons see the southern half, beginning with Libra, moving counter-clockwise.<-

This is to be applied here: for the gods and demons residing at Mount Meru <note>the North Pole</note> and Badavamukha <note>the South Pole</note>, the rotation of the celestial sphere appears clockwise and counter-clockwise respectively. Indeed, the word "apasavya" refers to the right-hand side or counter-clockwise direction. Now, he describes the measure of a day for the gods and others:

->The gods see the sun risen for half a solar year; likewise the inhabitants of the underworld.<-
->The ancestors residing on the moon see it for half a lunar month; humans here see it for half a civil day. || 17 ||<-

For half a solar year—from the beginning of the month of Aries to the end of the month of Virgo—the gods see the sun constantly risen, because the signs from Aries to Virgo move above the horizon due to the elevation of Meru and the rotation of the celestial sphere. Therefore, the six months beginning with Aries constitute the day for the gods. From the beginning of the month of Libra to the end of the month of Pisces, the gods never see the sun, because the six signs beginning with Libra move below the horizon relative to the elevation of Meru. Thus, the six months beginning with Libra constitute the night for the gods.

Likewise the inhabitants of the underworld <note>original: "preta," often referring to spirits or those in the lower realms</note> see the sun for half a solar year. However, they see the sun from the beginning of the month of Libra to the end of the month of Pisces. Therefore, that period is their day. They never see the sun from the beginning of Aries to the end of Virgo <note>The text likely means Pisces here to complete the cycle</note>; thus, that is their night. Because the "up" and "down" directions are reversed between Meru and Badavamukha, their day and night are also reversed. The common practice of saying the six months beginning with Capricorn <note>the winter solstice</note> is the "day of the gods" is only because Vedic rituals are prescribed for that time.

<sig>20</sig>

<summary>This page explains the varying perceptions of time and solar movement for gods, demons, ancestors, and humans based on their geographic or cosmological locations. It defines the "Day of the Gods" as the six-month period when the sun is in the northern signs.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, Meru, South Pole, solar year, zodiac signs, Aries, Libra, Brahmagupta, Day of the Gods, lunar month</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the astronomical discussion from the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the relative lengths of days and nights for different beings and the construction of the celestial sphere.</meta>

# On the Aryabhatiya
<page-num>82</page-num>

Because it is not prescribed for the six-month period beginning with Cancer, it is treated as their night. On this matter, Varahamihira <note>A famous 6th-century Indian astronomer and polymath</note> says:

->“When the Sun is in Aries, Taurus, and Gemini, it is day; when it enters Cancer and the following signs, it is night.<-
->To those who say this is the case for the Gods residing on Mount Meru <note>The sacred five-peaked mountain of Hindu cosmology, considered the center of the physical and spiritual universe</note>, I offer my salutations.”<-

Thus, the Fathers <note>original: "Pitri" — the ancestral spirits inhabiting the upper side of the Moon</note> residing on the upper part of the lunar sphere see the Sun for half of a lunar month. They do not see it for the other half of the lunar month. Therefore, for the Fathers, half a lunar month is a day, and the other half is a night. Indeed, during the New Moon <note>original: "Amavasya"</note>, the Sun is positioned above the lunar sphere; therefore, at that time, it is midday for the Fathers. During the Full Moon <note>original: "Paurnamasi"</note>, the Sun is below the lunar sphere; therefore, at that time, it is midnight for the Fathers. Their sunrise and sunset occur on the two eighth lunar days <note>The first and last quarter moons</note>. For half of a civil day <note>original: "Kudina" — a terrestrial day from sunrise to sunrise</note>, humans here see the Sun, and for the other half, they do not.

He explains the conceptualization of the celestial sphere <note>original: "Golakalpana"</note> in two verses:

->A circle placed East-West and Up-Down, and another North-South;<-
->Then the Horizon placed horizontally, where the rising and setting of the stars occur. || 18 ||<-

One should construct a circle or ring made of bamboo strips or similar material and place it East-West and Up-Down; this is called the Prime Vertical <note>original: "Samamandala"</note>. One should place another circle of the same size North-South and Up-Down; this is called the Meridian <note>original: "Dakshinottara"</note>. Again, one should place another circle of the same size horizontally, passing through the four cardinal points where they meet in a cross; this is called the Horizon <note>original: "Kshitija"</note>. Upon this Horizon, the rising and setting of the stars and the planets, such as the Sun, take place.

<summary>This page explains the different perceptions of time for Gods, Ancestors, and Humans based on solar and lunar positions, followed by a technical description of how to construct a physical model of the celestial sphere using bamboo rings.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhatiya, Varahamihira, Ancestors, Lunar Month, New Moon, Full Moon, Celestial Sphere, Horizon, Prime Vertical, Meridian, Astronomy</keywords>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>Section on the Sphere</header>

# Section on the Sphere

> That circle which is connected to the East and West points, and meets the horizon at the two poles of the axis, is the **Equinoctial Circle** <note>original: "Unmandala"</note>. It is upon this circle that the increase and decrease of days and nights are determined. || 18 ||

Following the previous proportions, another circle should be placed such that it bisects the East and West intersections <note>the cardinal points</note> transversely. It should be attached to the North-South meridian at a distance of the "Sine of Latitude" <note>original: "Akshajya"</note> above the North point of the horizon and at the same distance below the South point of the horizon. This is called the **Equinoctial Circle** <note>Unmandala</note>. The increase and decrease of the days and nights are to be understood through this. This entire structure is known as the **Celestial Sphere** <note>original: "Khagola"; literally "Sky-sphere"</note>.

Inside this, there is also the **Sphere of the Asterisms** <note>original: "Nakshatragola"; the sphere of the fixed stars</note>. Its arrangement is as follows: One should fasten three "Equatorial Circles" <note>original: "Vishuvanmandala"</note>—one running East-West, one running North-South, and one positioned horizontally, intersecting at the four cardinal points. Among these, the East-West circle is called the **Circle of Measures** <note>original: "Ghatikamandala"; the celestial equator marked in time units</note>.

Then, another circle should be fastened transversely at the East and West intersections. It should be attached to the North-South rods at a distance equal to the "Maximum Declination" <note>original: "Paramapakrama"; approximately 24 degrees in ancient Indian astronomy</note>—to the North of the nadir <note>bottom point</note> and to the South of the zenith <note>top point</note>. This is the **Ecliptic** <note>original: "Apamandala"; the path of the sun</note>, and it is marked with the signs of the Zodiac <note>Rashis</note>.

Furthermore, to the South and North of the Equator, one should fasten circles extending East-West at distances corresponding to specific declinations. These are the **Diurnal Circles** <note>original: "Svahoratramandala"; the daily paths of specific stars or planets</note>.

Then, having placed a firm, slender iron rod piercing through the North and South poles of the sphere, one should fix two steady pivot-pins <note>original: "Sharadandaka"</note> at its ends. One should then construct the "Sky-sphere" <note>Khagola</note> with a diameter equal to the distance between these two pins. Finally, by making holes at the two points where the Equinoctial and Meridian circles intersect on the Sky-sphere, one should insert the ends of the iron rod. This is how the celestial sphere is established for one's own location.

<summary>This page describes the construction of an armillary sphere, detailing the placement of the Equinoctial Circle, the Ecliptic, and the Diurnal Circles to model the movement of celestial bodies.</summary>
<keywords>armillary sphere, celestial equator, ecliptic, declination, Indian astronomy, Aryabhata, Khagola, Unmandala</keywords>

# On the Aryabhatiya
<note>original: "Aryabhatiye"</note>

The division of directions such as "below" and "above" must be made according to the position of the observer. Thus, he says:

> The line going East-West, the line going Below-Above, and the line situated North-South;
> The intersection of these lines is where the observer is located in any given place. || 20 ||

The line that goes East-West, the one that goes Below-Above, and the one that goes North-South—their point of union is at the observer's station. Now, he describes the <term>vertical circle</term> <note>original: "drngmandala"; the circle passing through the observer's zenith and the planet</note> and the <term>ecliptic's vertical circle</term> <note>original: "drkshepa-mandala"; the vertical circle passing through the highest point of the ecliptic</note>:

> Passing above and below the observer and facing the planet is the vertical circle.
> The circle of the ecliptic's zenith is also there, ninety degrees <note>original: "trirashyuna"; literally 'less three signs'</note> behind the oriental sine. || 21 || <insert>Ba 11.26</insert>

The vertical circle goes above and below, centered on the observer, and faces the desired planet. The previously mentioned circles were centered on the Earth's center; however, this one is centered on the observer situated on the Earth's surface. The circle of the ecliptic's zenith is ninety degrees behind the rising point of the ecliptic. This is the meaning. These two circles are used in the calculation of <term>parallax</term> <note>original: "lambana"; the difference in the apparent position of a celestial object as seen from the Earth's surface versus its center</note>. Some rotate the sphere <note>the celestial globe</note> by means of a machine. He demonstrates the method for that:

> Make a sphere out of wood, perfectly circular, equally heavy all around, and light.
> One should rotate it in time with the day using mercury, oil, water, and one's own intelligence. || 22 ||

"Made of wood" means constructed from wood such as bamboo. "Perfectly circular" means spherical in every way. "Equally heavy all around" means it is made such that the weight is equal in all its parts. "Light" means not overly heavy. Having made such a sphere, one should rotate it in time with the passage of time using mercury and other materials, guided by one's own intelligence. This is the meaning: one should place the ends of the iron needle <note>the axis</note> pierced through the sphere upon two North-South pillars fixed on the ground. One should lubricate the North and South holes of the sphere with oil so that the sphere rotates without friction. To the west of the sphere...
<meta>catchword: To the...</meta>

<summary>This page defines the observer's coordinate system and introduces the vertical circles used for parallax, concluding with instructions for building a self-rotating wooden celestial globe.</summary>
<keywords>observer's station, vertical circle, drkshepa, parallax, mechanical globe, mercury clock, Aryabhata, astronomy</keywords>

# The Section on the Sphere

One should arrange a tube, equal in length to the circumference of the sphere, with a hole at the bottom and filled with water. Then, having fixed a pin at the opposite intersection point <note>the "svastika" or cardinal point</note> of the sphere, one should tie one end of a string to it. This string should be led eastward along the surface of the equatorial circle, then pulled upward and led westward along that same path. To the end of that string, one should attach a hollow gourd <note>Alabu</note> filled with mercury and place it inside the water-filled tube. Then, one should open the hole at the bottom of the tube. As a result, the water flows out. As the water in the tube goes down, the gourd—which moves downward with the water due to its weight from the mercury—pulls the sphere westward. In this manner, the outflow of water should be arranged by one's own ingenuity so that in thirty ghatikas <note>a ghatika is 24 minutes; 30 ghatikas equal 12 hours</note>, half the water is discharged and the sphere rotates by half a turn.

This sphere is merely a means of measuring time, similar to a water-clock <term>Ghatika-yantra; a device for measuring time through the flow of water</term>, but it is not a means of replicating the actual motion of the celestial sphere <term>Jyotish-chakra; the "circle of lights" or the starry heavens</term>. For in the actual celestial sphere, Jupiter and the Moon, even if they rise together, are seen to change their relative positions every hour. In this mechanical sphere, they would not be seen to do so. Therefore, this sphere is equivalent to a water-clock, not to the actual celestial sphere. Indeed, the purpose of the sphere is the understanding of the proofs for the sine of declination, the earth-sine, the sine of the altitude, the sine of the amplitude, and the gnomon.

Now, wishing to demonstrate specific geometric figures using the semi-chords <note>sines</note> located in the celestial sphere, the author describes the method of imagining these figures and defines the Latitude-sine and the Co-latitude:

> **In the visible half-bowl of the celestial sphere, one should map the hemisphere using semi-chords.**
> **The sine of the latitude is the base, and the sine of the co-latitude is the perpendicular.** || 13 ||

In the visible half-bowl of the celestial sphere—that is, in the observable portion of the hemisphere—one should map the half of the celestial sphere in various ways using the semi-chords <term>Jyardha; literally "half-chord," the Sanskrit term for the sine function</term> produced in this section, which exist as the "base" (latitude, etc.) and the "perpendicular" (co-latitude, etc.). The visible portion is thus joined with various geometric figures depending on the latitude-sine, the gnomon, the earth-sine, and so on.

<column-break/>

<note>Vocabulary: Sphere-section, Sphere, Tube, Gourd, Mercury, Water-clock, Celestial Sphere, Semi-chord (Sine), Visible Sphere, Latitude-sine, Co-latitude (the perpendicular to the latitude).</note>

# On the Aryabhatiya
### Page 86

One should imagine half of the celestial sphere. It is said that the proof of this is to be understood through the construction of the figures <note>geometric diagrams</note> starting with the latitude, which will be explained later. The **Equinoctial Sine** <term>Vishuvajjiva: the sine of the latitude</term> is the "latitude-arm." At midday on the day of the equinox, the sine of the interval between the Sun and the zenith is the Equinoctial Sine. This means it is the equinoctial shadow. That is the "latitude-arm," meaning the **Sine of Latitude** <term>Akshajya</term>. The **Co-latitude** <term>Avalambaka: literally 'the pendant'</term> is its "complementary side" <note>the cosine</note>. The Co-latitude is the square root of the square of the radius minus the square of the Sine of Latitude. The gnomon at midday on the equinox would be the Co-latitude. Now, he describes the **Radius of the Day-Circle** <term>Svahoratrardha: literally 'half the day-and-night'</term>:

->Subtract the square of the desired declination from the square of the radius; the square root of that,<-
->north or south of the equator, is the semi-diameter of the day-circle. || 24 ||<-

Having subtracted the square of the sine of the desired declination from the square of the radius, the root of the remainder is the semi-diameter of the individual day-circles to the north or south of the equatorial circle (known as the circle of time-measures). This means it is the **radius of the circle** <term>Vishkambhardha</term>. The radius of the day-circle is the "complementary side" <note>cosine</note> to the "arm" of declination. The radius of the sphere is the hypotenuse. The figures such as the "latitude-arm" mentioned in the *Sphere* section have been demonstrated in detail in the **Commentary on the Mahabhaskariya** <note>A major astronomical work by Bhaskara I, c. 629 CE</note>. Therefore, we shall not explain them further here. Now, he states the measure of the rising of the zodiac signs at the **Equator** <term>Niraksha-desha: literally 'the place without latitude' or Lanka</term>:

->The semi-diameter of the day-circle multiplied by the desired sine is the 'end of the arc';<-
->the result divided by the radius of the day-circle is the Sine of the Right Ascension starting from Aries. || 25 ||<-

The **Semi-diameter of the day-circle** <term>Svahoratra-vyasardha</term> is the "end of the arc" reaching the limit of declination; this means the semi-diameter of the day-circle calculated from the **Maximum Declination** <term>Paramapakrama: 24 degrees in this system</term>. In the case of all zodiac signs, only the day-circle radius established by the maximum declination is used for multiplication. This is indicated by the word "only"—one should multiply the day-circle radius established by the maximum declination (which is equal to 3141 <note>original: "shashi-kruta-shashi-rama" - a numerical code where Moon=1, Square=4, Moon=1, Rama=3, read right-to-left as 3141</note>) by the desired sine of the arc, and divide that by the specific day-circle radius calculated for that latitude.

<summary>This page provides geometric definitions for the celestial sphere, specifically defining the Sine of Latitude and the method for calculating the radius of a planet's daily path (day-circle) based on its declination. It references Bhaskara I's earlier commentary for detailed proofs and begins explaining how to calculate the rising times of zodiac signs at the equator.</summary>

<keywords>Aryabhatiya, trigonometry, Sine of Latitude, Co-latitude, Day-circle radius, Declination, Mahabhaskariya, Right Ascension, Indian Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical exposition of the Sphere (Gola), specifically focusing on the calculation of rising times at the equator and the variation in the length of days and nights.</meta>

<page-num>87</page-num>
# Section on the Sphere
<meta>Devanagari script, printed Sanskrit text</meta>

The result obtained therefrom is the "rising sine" <note>original: "prāgjyā"</note> at the equator <note>original: "Lankā", the zero-point of the equator in Indian astronomy</note>. At the equator, the rising sine produced by the time of rising for the degrees of the sine of the arc is the east-west circle sine. This means it is the sine of the celestial equator. That, when converted into an arc, becomes the rising and setting. Thus is the calculation of the measure of the rising of the degrees of the sine of the arc.

As for the measure of each zodiac sign: one should calculate two separate measures for the signs using the sine of the desired sign and the sine of the preceding sign, and then take the difference between them. That becomes the measure of the rising at the equator for that desired sign. The progression of the sine of the arc begins from Aries and Libra in regular order; therefore, the rising of the signs there occurs in regular order. From the end of Virgo and the end of Pisces, the progression of the sine of the arc is in reverse order; therefore, the rising of the signs there occurs in reverse order.

The rule of three <term>trairāśika: a mathematical proportion involving three known values to find a fourth</term> here is as follows: If, by the radius, a "cosine" <note>the side of a right triangle</note> equal to the semi-diameter of the diurnal circle at the maximum declination is obtained, then what is obtained by the desired sine? This gives the desired cosine within the desired semi-diameter of the diurnal circle. If such a cosine exists in the desired semi-diameter of the diurnal circle, then what would it be in the radius? This gives the rising of the zodiac sign on the celestial equator. In the first rule of three, the radius is the divisor. In the second, it is the multiplier. Since the multiplier and divisor are equal, the operation is performed without both of them.

<margin>Calculation of the increase and decrease of day and night.</margin>
He now describes the calculation for the decrease and increase of day and night:

> The sine of the desired declination multiplied by the sine of the latitude, divided by the cosine of the latitude, is the sine of the increase or decrease of the day and night, produced at the horizon within the diurnal circle. || 26 ||

Multiplying the sine of the desired declination by the sine of the latitude <note>original: "akṣajyā"</note> and dividing by the cosine of the latitude <note>original: "lambaka"</note>, the result obtained is the sine of the increase or decrease of day and night produced within the diurnal circle, originating from the horizon. This means it is "horizon-born." The rule of three here is: If the sine of the latitude is the "sine" <note>opposite side</note> for the cosine of the latitude, then what is the "sine" for the declination? The resulting sine is produced within the diurnal circle. Therefore, that—

<summary>This page explains the mathematical methods for calculating the rising times of zodiac signs at the equator and introduces the formula for determining the variation in the length of days and nights based on local latitude and solar declination.</summary>
<keywords>spherical astronomy, rule of three, zodiac rising times, declination, latitude, diurnal circle, Aryabhata, Indian mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical explanation of planetary motion and the calculation of rising times for zodiac signs, following the previous discussion on the celestial sphere.</meta>

# In the Aryabhatiya
<page-num>88</page-num>

Multiply by the radius and divide by the <term>radius of the diurnal circle</term> <note>original: "svahoratra"; the radius of the specific path a star or planet takes across the sky in 24 hours</note>. The result obtained there is the <term>sine of the ascensional difference</term> <note>original: "charadalajya"; the difference between the time a celestial body takes to rise at the equator versus a specific latitude</note>.

In this case, the <term>rule of three</term> <note>a mathematical proportion: if A gives B, what does C give?</note> is applied as follows: If this is the sine in the <term>diurnal circle</term>, then what would it be in the <term>circle of the radius</term> <note>the great circle or equator</note>? Thus, the <term>sine in the equatorial circle</term> is obtained. From this <term>ascensional difference</term>, the "ascensional difference breaths" <note>units of time where one breath equals 4 seconds</note> are derived. Now, he describes the <term>rising of the zodiac signs at one's own location</term>:

> ->A quarter of the zodiac rises in a quarter of a day minus the ascensional difference.<-
> ->This applies to the first and the last quarters; the other two quarters rise with the addition of the same, in direct and reverse order. // 27 //<-

The first quarter of the zodiac—consisting of <term>Aries, Taurus, and Gemini</term>—rises in a quarter of a day <note>15 ghatikas or 6 hours</note> minus the <term>ascensional difference</term>. That is, it rises in fifteen ghatikas reduced by the ascensional difference. The last quarter—consisting of <term>Pisces, Aquarius, and Capricorn</term>—likewise rises in fifteen ghatikas minus the ascensional difference. Therefore, for the six signs from <term>Capricorn to Gemini</term>, the <term>rising times at one's own location</term> are the <term>rising times at the equator</term> <note>original: "lankodaya"; literally "rising at Lanka," the reference point on the equator</note> minus the <term>ascensional difference breaths</term> produced by those specific signs.

"The other two with the addition of the same": The quarters known as <term>Cancer, Leo, and Virgo</term> and <term>Libra, Scorpio, and Sagittarius</term> rise in a quarter of a day plus the ascensional difference. Therefore, for the six signs from <term>Cancer to Sagittarius</term>, the <term>rising times at one's own location</term> are the <term>equatorial rising times</term> plus their respective <term>ascensional differences</term>.

"In direct and reverse order": In the first quarter, for the first sign, Aries, the <term>ascensional difference</term> of the first sign is subtracted from its <term>equatorial rising time</term>. For the second sign, Taurus, the <term>ascensional difference</term> of the second sign is subtracted from its <term>equatorial rising time</term>. For the third sign, Gemini, the <term>ascensional difference</term> of the third sign is subtracted from its <term>equatorial rising time</term>. However, in the second quarter, the values are added in reverse order: for Cancer, the ascensional difference of the third sign is added; for Leo, the ascensional difference of the second sign is added; for Virgo, the ascensional difference of the first sign is added. In the third quarter, they are added in direct order. In the fourth quarter, they are subtracted in reverse order. This is what is being stated. Because the northern part of the celestial sphere is inclined, the signs starting from Pisces rise more quickly. Therefore, in those cases, the <term>ascensional difference</term>...

It details which signs rise faster or slower than they do at the equator based on their position in the four quarters of the zodiac.</summary>

# Section on the Celestial Sphere

<page-num>81</page-num>

It must be corrected. For this reason, starting from the sign of Cancer, the sun rises slowly. Therefore, the "ascensional difference" <note>the difference between the time of sunrise and 6:00 AM</note> must be applied to those calculations.

Now, he describes the method for calculating the gnomon <note>the vertical pillar of a sundial</note> at a desired time:

> ->Multiply the sine of the sun's altitude above the horizon on its own diurnal circle by the sine of the colatitude;<-
> ->When divided by the radius, the result is the gnomon for the elapsed or remaining part of the day. || 28 ||<-

"From the horizon" means produced from the horizon circle. One should take the sine of the sun's altitude on its diurnal circle—calculated using the elapsed ghatikas <note>units of time equal to 24 minutes</note> in the forenoon, or the remaining ghatikas in the afternoon—and multiply it by the sine of the colatitude <note>the angle between the zenith and the celestial equator</note>. When that sum is divided by the radius <term>Vishkambhardha: half the diameter</term>, the result is the gnomon. This is the "Great Gnomon" at the desired time. It is the gnomon for the elapsed or remaining time of the day. The relationship is: the sine of the sun's altitude on its diurnal circle for the time passed or the time yet to come in the day.

The rule of three <note>a mathematical proportion</note> is applied here as follows: If, for a radius-length sine of the diurnal circle, a gnomon equal to the sine of the colatitude is obtained, then what gnomon is obtained for the desired sine of the diurnal circle? The result is the desired gnomon. Indeed, at noon on the day of the equinox, the sine of the diurnal circle is equal to the radius, and the gnomon is equal to the sine of the colatitude.

As for calculating the sine of the sun's altitude on its diurnal circle: In the northern hemisphere <term>Uttara-gola</term>, subtract the "ascensional difference" <note>the time difference in breaths</note> from the elapsed or remaining time, take the sine, multiply by the radius of the diurnal circle, and divide by the total radius. To this result, add the "earth-sine" <term>Bhujya</term>. That becomes the sine of the sun's altitude above the horizon on its diurnal circle. In the southern hemisphere <term>Dakshina-gola</term>, the difference is that one adds the ascensional difference and subtracts the earth-sine.

By subtracting the square of the gnomon from the square of the radius, the square root of the remainder is the shadow of that gnomon. From the relationship of the gnomon and the shadow as the vertical and horizontal sides of a triangle, the desired shadow is calculated via the rule of three. To convert the shadow into time: multiply the radius by a twelve-digit gnomon and divide by the hypotenuse of the desired shadow; the result is the Great Gnomon. From that—

By the reverse process of the <term>gnomon</term> <note>Sanskrit: śaṅku; a vertical pillar used to measure time and latitude by its shadow</note> method, the elapsed and remaining hours are determined. Now, he describes the calculation of the <term>gnomon-tip</term> <note>Sanskrit: śaṅkvagra; the distance of the shadow's tip from the rising-setting line</note>.

->The desired gnomon multiplied by the sine of the latitude and divided by the sine of the co-latitude,<-
->is the gnomon-tip of the Sun, south of the rising and setting line. || 29 ||<-

One should multiply the desired <term>great gnomon</term> by the sine of the local latitude and divide it by the sine of the local co-latitude <note>Sanskrit: lambaka; the angle complementary to the latitude</note>. The result obtained is the Sun's gnomon-tip, located to the south of the rising-setting line. The gnomon-tip is always toward the south because of the northward inclination of the celestial sphere. The mention of the Sun here also implies the Moon. Here too, the <term>rule of three</term> <note>A mathematical proportion: if A gives B, what does C give?</note> is applied: If the sine of the co-latitude is the base and the sine of the latitude is the upright, then for a given gnomon as the base, what is the upright? Because the properties of the triangles are the same in both cases, the rule of three is applicable. Alternatively: If the sine of the co-latitude corresponds to the sine of the latitude, what does the desired gnomon correspond to? Now, he describes the calculation of the <term>solar amplitude</term> <note>Sanskrit: arkāgrā; the angular distance of the rising or setting Sun from the East or West points</note>.

->Multiply the sine of the maximum declination by the sine of the [Sun's] longitude, then divide<- <insert>B III.22</insert>
->by the sine of the co-latitude; the result is the solar amplitude on the eastern and western horizons. || 30 ||<-

Having multiplied the sine of the maximum declination <note>The Sun's greatest distance from the celestial equator, roughly 24 degrees in ancient Indian astronomy</note> by the sine of the Sun's longitude <note>Sanskrit: bhujajyā; the sine of the Sun's position along the ecliptic</note>, one should then divide by the sine of the co-latitude. "By the sine of the co-latitude" means exactly that. The result obtained is the solar amplitude. "On the eastern and western horizons" refers to where the Sun rises on the eastern horizon and where it sets on the western horizon. The meaning is that the solar amplitude is the sine of the interval on the horizon circle between those two points and the exact East-West points. Here also, the rule of three applies: If the radius yields the maximum declination, what declination does the desired longitude yield? This gives the desired declination. Then: If the radius is the hypotenuse for a triangle with the co-latitude as the base, what is the hypotenuse for a triangle with the desired declination as the base? This yields the solar amplitude. In the first rule of three, the radius...

<page-num>11</page-num>

The divisor. In the second case, the radius is the multiplier. Therefore, the calculation is performed without both of those values. Now, he describes the calculation of the gnomon's shadow <note>The "shanku" or gnomon is a vertical pillar used to measure time and solar position via its shadow</note> at the moment the sun enters the prime vertical <note>The east-west vertical circle</note>.

<margin>Bhaskara I's Commentary 11, 22</margin>

> If the amplitude of the sun at the horizon is diminished by the sine of the latitude, and then multiplied by the sine of the colatitude, and divided by the sine of the latitude, the result is the gnomon when the sun is on the east-west line. || 31 ||

The "equinoctial sight" refers to the sightline from the equinoctial circle. This occurs in the northern hemisphere. The "amplitude of the sun at the horizon" <note>original: "arkagra"</note> is what is meant. "If diminished by the sine of the latitude" means if it is calculated using the declination <note>The angular distance of the sun north or south of the celestial equator</note> minus the sine of the latitude. This resulting solar amplitude, multiplied by the sine of the colatitude <note>original: "lambaka"; the complement of the latitude</note> and divided by the sine of the latitude, should be calculated. The result obtained there is the gnomon when the sun is situated on the east-west line. This means it is the gnomon of the prime vertical. Indeed, when the sun is on the prime vertical, the distance of the shadow's tip from the east-west line is equal to the solar amplitude. In this case, the rule of three <term>trairashika: a mathematical proportion where if three values are known, the fourth is determined</term> is as follows: If a gnomon equal to the sine of the colatitude is obtained from a shadow-tip equal to the sine of the latitude, then what gnomon is obtained from a shadow-tip equal to the solar amplitude? Thus, the prime vertical gnomon is obtained.

Now, he describes the midday gnomon and its shadow.

<margin>32-4 Bhaskara I's Commentary 11, 23</margin>

> The sine of the degrees of altitude above the horizon is the maximum gnomon. The sine of the degrees of zenith distance at midday is the shadow of that very gnomon. || 32 ||

At the time of midday, the number of degrees the sun is elevated from either the southern horizon or the northern horizon—the sine of those degrees is the "maximum gnomon." This means the midday gnomon.

The number of degrees the sun is depressed (inclined) from the zenith <note>The point directly overhead</note>—the sine of those degrees is the shadow of that gnomon. This means the midday shadow. In the southern hemisphere <note>original: "dakshina-gole"</note>, the sum of the arc of declination and the arc of latitude is the sun's zenith distance. In the northern hemisphere, the difference between them is the sun's zenith distance. Three signs <note>90 degrees</note> minus the zenith distance is the altitude.

# On the Calculation of the Vertical Sine

> Multiply the meridian sine by the sine of the amplitude; divide this product by the radius. 
> The square root of the difference between the square of that result and the square of the meridian sine is the specific vertical sine. || 33 ||

The **meridian sine** <note>Sanskrit: madhyajya; the sine of the zenith distance of the meridian ecliptic point</note> is the sine of the sum of the southern declination of the meridian ecliptic point and the local latitude. Alternatively, the meridian sine is the sine of the difference between the northern declination of the meridian ecliptic point and the local latitude. The **sine of the amplitude** <note>Sanskrit: udayajya; the distance on the horizon between the east point and the point where the ecliptic intersects the horizon</note> is defined as the sine of the interval between the point where the specific rising sign <term>Lagna|The point of the ecliptic rising on the eastern horizon</term> meets the horizon and the true East point. This sine of the amplitude is calculated by taking the sine of the longitude of the tropical rising sign, multiplying it by the sine of the maximum declination, and dividing by the cosine of the latitude.

**Product** <note>Sanskrit: samvarga</note> refers to mutual multiplication. When the product of the meridian sine and the sine of the amplitude is divided by the radius, one must subtract the square of that resulting value from the square of the meridian sine. The square root of the remainder is the **vertical sine** <note>Sanskrit: drkksepajya; the sine of the zenith distance of the highest point of the ecliptic</note>. This means it becomes the vertical sine for whichever body—the Sun or the Moon—the meridian ecliptic point was calculated for. The sine of the arc between the zenith and the "vertical sine pole" <note>the point on the ecliptic nearest to the zenith</note> is called the vertical sine. 

In a solar eclipse, the meridian sine and vertical sine must be calculated separately for both the Sun and the Moon. The underlying logic <note>Sanskrit: yukti</note> for this should be understood through geometric diagrams <note>Sanskrit: chedyaka</note>; this has been demonstrated elsewhere. The meridian ecliptic point <note>Sanskrit: madhyalagna</note> is calculated in the forenoon by taking the "set time" <note>time passed since noon</note> in breaths, subtracting the times of rising at the equator in reverse order starting from the Sun's position, and subtracting those signs from the Sun. In the afternoon, one subtracts the equatorial rising times in forward order from the "set time" and adds those signs to the Sun.

Now, he describes the calculation of the sine of the ecliptic motion and the earth's parallax in yojanas:

> The square root of the difference between the squares of the sine of the zenith distance and the vertical sine is the sine of the ecliptic motion, which is due to the Earth's influence. 
> On the horizon, the sine of the zenith distance is equal to the Earth's radius; at the zenith, it is zero. || 34 ||

The cause of the observational difference <note>parallax</note> is either the shadow, the sine of the zenith distance <note>drgjya</note>, the sine of the ecliptic motion <note>drggatijya</note>, or the vertical sine. That...

<keywords>Aryabhatiya, vertical sine, meridian sine, sine of amplitude, solar eclipse, parallax, trigonometry, Indian astronomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of spherical astronomy, specifically focusing on the calculation of parallax and its components in the context of eclipses and planetary visibility.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
# Section on the Celestial Sphere
<sig>23</sig>

If the planet is on the horizon, the distance from the zenith to the horizon is equal to the radius <note>original: *vyāsārdha*, the radius of the celestial sphere</note>. This means that the parallax <note>original: *dṛgbheda*, literally 'difference in sight' caused by the observer's position on Earth's surface</note> produced by the Earth's size is at its maximum. In this case, the parallax in terms of distance is equal to the Earth's radius. For positions in between the zenith and horizon, the value should be calculated by proportion. Therefore, by multiplying the sine of the ecliptic zenith distance <note>original: *dṛggatijyā*</note> by the Earth's radius and dividing by the total radius, the resulting distance of parallax is obtained. In an eclipse, this becomes the longitude parallax <note>original: *lambana*</note>. By multiplying the sine of the ecliptic altitude <note>original: *dṛkkṣepajyā*</note> by the Earth's radius and dividing by the total radius, the result is the latitude parallax <note>original: *nati*</note> in terms of distance. The parallax along the vertical circle, which acts as a hypotenuse, is obtained from the sine of the zenith distance; however, this is not used in the practical calculation of eclipses. It should be understood for the sake of theoretical reasoning. One should multiply the parallax in longitude and latitude by the total radius and divide by the planet's own orbital distance <note>original: *yojanavyāsa*</note>. The results obtained are the minutes of arc for longitude parallax and latitude parallax respectively. The difference between the latitude parallax of the Sun and the Moon is the net latitude parallax in a solar eclipse. This should be subtracted from the time of the new moon <note>original: *parvānta*</note> if it occurs before noon, and added if in the afternoon. The time of the new moon thus corrected is called the "corrected end of the lunar month." To determine the rising and setting of the Moon and other planets, the author describes the "visibility correction" <note>original: *dṛkkarma*</note> using their respective latitudes:

<gloss>Reference to verses 35-36, or Bhāskara I's commentary 11.26, 11.37</gloss>
> The latitude multiplied by the sine of the terrestrial latitude <note>original: *akṣajyā*</note> and divided by the cosine of the terrestrial latitude <note>original: *lumbaka*</note> shall be subtracted if the planet is to the north.
> At rising it is negative, at setting it is positive; if the Moon is to the south, it is positive at rising and negative at setting. || 35 ||

The latitude multiplied by the sine of the terrestrial latitude and divided by the cosine of the terrestrial latitude gives the visibility correction in minutes of arc. "If to the north" means when the Moon is north of the ecliptic. "At rising, negative" means that for a northern latitude, this correction should be subtracted from the Moon's position at the time of rising. At the time of setting, that result should be added to the Moon's position. "If to the south, positive and negative" means for a southern latitude, the result is added at rising and subtracted at setting. This is applied to the Moon at that specific time. This is known as the terrestrial visibility correction <note>original: *ākṣa-dṛkkarma*</note>. Now, he describes the ecliptic visibility correction <note>original: *āyana-dṛkkarma*</note>.

<summary>This page explains the mathematical derivation of parallax in longitude and latitude for solar eclipses and introduces the 'visibility correction' (dṛkkarma) required to calculate the rising and setting of celestial bodies.</summary>
<keywords>parallax, longitude parallax, latitude parallax, solar eclipse, visibility correction, terrestrial latitude, lunar computation, Indian astronomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and astronomical commentary on the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the calculations for planetary visibility and the physical nature of eclipses.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
->**In the Aryabhatiya**<-

> Multiply the versed sine by the latitude and the obliquity, and divide by the square of the radius. In the northern path, the result is added or subtracted; in the southern path, it is subtracted or added. || 36 ||

The versed sine <note>original: "utkramanim"; the versed sine is 1 minus the cosine</note> is multiplied by the latitude and the obliquity. This refers to the versed sine of the longitude of the Moon including the precession of the equinoxes <note>original: "sayana-candra"</note>. Multiply that by the latitude and the maximum obliquity <note>the tilt of the Earth's axis, roughly 24 degrees in ancient Indian astronomy</note>, then divide by the square of the radius <note>original: "vistarardha" or "vyasardha"</note>. The result obtained is the "visibility correction" <term>Drik-phala: a correction applied to find the exact time a celestial body rises or sets</term> expressed in minutes of arc.

"In the northern path, the result is added or subtracted": When the Moon is in the northern progress <note>the passage from the winter to summer solstice</note>, if the latitude is northern, the result is subtracted from the Moon's position. If the latitude is southern, the result is added to the Moon's position. This should be applied accordingly. "In the southern path, it is subtracted or added": When the Moon is in the southern progress, the previous rules are reversed; it is added for northern latitude and subtracted for southern latitude.

It should be understood that the Teacher <note>referring to Aryabhata</note> has shown here two approximate forms of the visibility correction, not the precise form. The implication is that the precise form can be logically derived from this approximate one. For whichever Moon-phase the rising or setting point is required, these two visibility operations must be performed, and not elsewhere.

Now, he describes the true nature of the Moon, the Sun, the Earth, the Earth's shadow, and the eclipses of the Sun and Moon:

> The Moon is water, the Sun is fire, the Earth is soil, and its shadow is darkness. The Moon covers the Sun, and the great shadow of the Earth covers the Moon. || 37 || <gloss>Reference to Bhaskara I's commentary 11.9</gloss>

The Moon is composed of water. The Sun is composed of fire. The Earth is composed of soil. That which is called the "Earth's shadow" is indeed darkness. At the time of a solar eclipse, the Moon covers the Sun, not the demon Rahu <note>a reference to the mythological cause of eclipses, which Aryabhata replaces with a physical explanation</note>. At the time of a lunar eclipse, the great shadow of the Earth covers the Moon, not Rahu.

He now describes the timing of the eclipse:

> At the end of the true lunar month, when the Moon, being near its node, enters...

# Section on the Celestial Sphere

<page-num>35</page-num>

-> At the end of the lunar fortnight, the Earth's shadow [causes an eclipse]; the middle of the eclipse occurs when [the moon] is more or less [at the node]. // 38 // <-

At the end of the true lunar month <note>The moment of the new moon (amāvāsyā)</note>, adjusted by parallax <term>lambana: the apparent displacement of a celestial object viewed from different locations</term>, when the Moon is near its node and has little latitude, it enters the Sun; at that time, the middle of the eclipse occurs, being either more or less in duration. This means the middle of the lunar eclipse happens then, whether the duration is long or short. At the end of the fortnight—that is, at the end of the full moon—when the Moon enters the Earth's shadow <term>bhū-chāyā</term>, the middle of the lunar eclipse occurs. However, some explain that at the end of the true lunar month, which is simply the end of the new moon, the eclipse may sometimes occur above or below [the ecliptic]. (So it is explained). // Now he describes the length of the Earth's shadow:

-> Divide the distance between the Earth and the Sun, multiplied by the Earth's diameter, by the difference between the diameters of the Sun and the Earth. <-
-> The result is the length of the Earth's shadow starting from the diameter of the Earth's sphere. // 39 // <-

The distance between the Earth and the Sun, equal to the Sun's true distance in <term>yojanas: a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term>, should be multiplied by the diameter of the Earth in yojanas. This should then be divided by the difference between the Sun's diameter and the Earth's diameter, expressed in yojanas. The result obtained there is the length of the Earth's shadow in yojanas. "From the diameter of the Earth's sphere" means this shadow length is calculated starting from the center of the Earth's sphere. // Now he describes the calculation of the diameter of the shadow in yojanas at the region of the Moon's orbit:

-> The distance between the tip of the shadow and the Moon, multiplied by the Earth's diameter, <-
-> and divided by the length of the Earth's shadow, provides the diameter of the darkness [the shadow] at that point. // 40 // <-

The "distance between the tip of the shadow and the Moon" means the total length of the shadow minus the Moon's true distance (hypotenuse) in yojanas. Multiply that by the Earth's diameter and divide by the total length of the Earth's shadow. The result obtained is the diameter of the darkness—the Earth's shadow—along the Moon's path.

<summary>This page provides the mathematical formulas for determining the timing of eclipses and calculating the physical dimensions of the Earth's conical shadow in space. It establishes the geometric relationship between the Sun, Earth, and Moon required to predict the size of the shadow during a lunar eclipse.</summary>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of eclipses according to the Aryabhatiya, focusing on the mathematical derivation of the diameters of the celestial bodies and the duration of the eclipse phases.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

The <term>diameter</term><note>original: "vishkambha"</note> is the width measured in <term>yojanas</term><note>A traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8 to 15 kilometers.</note>. One should divide that diameter by the <term>radius-sine</term><note>original: "trijya-karna"; the hypotenuse representing the radius of the celestial sphere.</note>. The result obtained there is the diameter of the <term>shadow</term><note>The Earth's shadow during a lunar eclipse.</note> expressed in minutes of arc. Regarding the Sun and the Moon: multiply their respective diameters in yojanas by the radius-sine and divide by their own true distance in yojanas; the result is their respective diameters expressed in minutes of arc.

He now describes the calculation for the <term>half-duration</term><note>original: "sthityardha"; the time from the first contact to the middle of the eclipse, or from the middle to the end.</note>:

> ->From the square of the half-sum of the diameters, the square of the Moon's latitude should be subtracted.<-
> ->The square root of this should be known as the half-duration, calculated from the daily motion of the Moon and Sun. (Verse 41)<-

"From the square of the half-sum": In a solar eclipse, one should subtract the square of the Moon's <term>latitude</term><note>The angular distance of the Moon north or south of the ecliptic.</note> from the square of the half-sum of the diameters of the Sun and Moon. This means "one should perform the subtraction." In a lunar eclipse, one should subtract the square of the Moon's latitude from the square of the half-sum of the diameters of the Moon and the Earth's shadow. The square root of the remainder is the "half-duration." This refers to the method of achieving the half-duration. He explains how that is done with the phrase "from the daily motion of the Moon and Sun." This means that the square root, multiplied by sixty and divided by the difference in the daily motions of the Sun and Moon, results in the half-duration expressed in <term>nadikas</term><note>A unit of time equal to 24 minutes.</note>. In a lunar eclipse, these values are true (final). However, in a solar eclipse, they become true only when combined with the time of <term>parallax</term><note>original: "lambana"; the apparent displacement of the celestial body due to the observer's position on Earth.</note> produced during the half-duration period. Specifically, the half-duration of the "touching" (start) phase is made true by adding the difference between the parallax at the middle of the eclipse and the parallax at the time of first contact. Likewise, the half-duration of the "release" (end) phase is made true by adding the difference between the parallax at the time of release and the parallax at the middle of the eclipse.

He now describes the calculation for the <term>half-duration of totality</term><note>original: "vimardhardha"; the time during which the eclipse is total.</note>:

> ->From the square of the half-diameter of the shadow diminished by the Moon's half-diameter,<-
> ->subtract the square of the latitude; the square root of that is the half-duration of totality. (Verse 42)<-

<summary>This page details the trigonometric formulas for calculating the angular diameters of the Sun, Moon, and Earth's shadow, as well as the mathematical steps to determine the duration of partial and total eclipse phases.</summary>
