<sig>24</sig>

# Section on the Celestial Sphere

Subtract the square of the latitude <note>the angular distance of the Moon north or south of the ecliptic</note> from the square of the difference between the semi-diameter of the Earth's shadow and the semi-diameter of the Moon's disk. The square root of the remainder is the "half-duration of total darkness" <term>vimardhardha: the period during a total eclipse when the Moon is completely covered</term>. This serves as the calculation for total eclipse duration. By multiplying that result by sixty and dividing by the difference between the daily motions of the Sun and the Moon, the duration of total darkness is expressed in units of time <note>specifically in nadikas, where 1 nadika equals 24 minutes</note>. Now, the author describes the measurement of the portion of the Moon that remains un-eclipsed:

->Subtract the semi-diameter of the Moon from the semi-diameter of the shadow.<-
->Whatever remains after subtracting the latitude from that value is the portion of the Moon not eclipsed. (43)<-

Subtract the semi-diameter of the Moon's disk from the semi-diameter of the shadow's disk. From that result, subtract the latitude. The remaining value represents the portion of the Moon that is not seized by the shadow. That is to say, the minutes of arc equal to that remainder are not obscured. Next, he explains how to determine the magnitude of the eclipse at any given moment:

->From the square root of the sum of the square of the latitude and the square of the half-duration minus the elapsed time,<-
->subtract that from the sum of the semi-diameters; the remainder is the eclipse at that moment. (44)<-

<insert>The square root of the sum of the square of the latitude and the square of the "base" (which is the half-duration minus the desired time) should be subtracted from the sum of the semi-diameters of the two bodies. The remainder is the magnitude of the eclipse at that specific time. Now, he explains the knowledge of the contact (start) and release (end) of the eclipse:</insert>*)

->The sine of the latitude multiplied by the sine of the hour angle from noon, divided by the radius, gives the direction.<-
->This is applied to the half-duration and the precession of the Sun and Moon at the moment of contact. (45)<-

<insert>The sine of the terrestrial latitude is multiplied by the sine of the altitude from noon and divided by the radius. The arc of that result determines the direction.</insert> This is the "deflection due to latitude" <term>akshavalana: the variation in the eclipse's appearance caused by the observer's position on Earth</term>. To the south...

---

*) <note>original: "pustakadvaye'pi vyakhyanam khanditam..."</note> In both source manuscripts, the commentary is fragmented. Therefore, the explanation from the *Prakashika* <note>a well-known commentary on the Aryabhatiya</note> has been written here. The phrase "the time elapsed since the middle of the duration... the desired time after the middle time..." appears as an identical broken sentence in both manuscripts.

<summary>This page provides geometric formulas for calculating the duration of a total lunar eclipse, the magnitude of the eclipse at any specific moment, and the visual deflection caused by the observer's latitude.</summary>
<keywords>lunar eclipse, celestial geometry, Aryabhatiya, shadow diameter, latitude deflection, eclipse magnitude</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of eclipse calculations from the Aryabhatiya, specifically focusing on the determination of "valana" (deflection or variation) and the visual colors of the moon during different stages of an eclipse.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>In the Aryabhatiya</header>

In the forenoon (the first part of the day), the southern deflection occurs. <note>original: "dakṣiṇaṃ valanaṃ"</note> This means that at the moment of the Sun's contact <note>the beginning of the eclipse</note> in the eastern hemisphere, the deflection is toward the south. In the western hemisphere, the deflection is toward the north. At midday, there is no direction <note>zero deflection</note>. For the Moon, the directions are always the opposite of the Sun's. This "latitude deflection" <term>Aksha-valana: the variation due to the observer's terrestrial latitude</term> is also derived from the half-duration of the eclipse. By the term "half-duration," the underlying cause—the celestial latitude—is implied; for the Sun, the "true parallax in latitude" <note>sphuta-nati</note> becomes the deflection. Its direction at the beginning and end of the eclipse is the same as the direction of the parallax. In a lunar eclipse, the Moon's own celestial latitude <term>Vikshepa: the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</term> becomes the deflection. Because of the reversal of latitude, the direction changes between the beginning and the end of the eclipse. Regarding the Sun and Moon, the deflection also arises from the "solstitial point plus three signs." By the word "solstice" <note>Ayana</note>, the declination is meant. The declination calculated from the Sun or Moon increased by three signs <note>90 degrees</note> also constitutes their deflection. "At the contact" means at the time of the eclipse. This is the "solstitial deflection" <term>Ayana-valana: the variation due to the obliquity of the ecliptic</term>. Its direction at the face of the disk follows the direction of the declination. For the Moon, at the beginning, it follows the declination; at the end, it is the reverse of the declination. The Sun's solstitial deflection direction is the opposite of the Moon's. Having added the arcs of the latitude deflection and solstitial deflection if they are in the same direction, or taking their difference if they are in different directions, one should take the sine <note>jiva</note>, multiply it by the sum of the radii <note>of the eclipsing and eclipsed bodies</note>, and divide by the radius of the celestial sphere. To the result, one should apply the latitude. That becomes the "true deflection" <note>sphuta-valana</note>.

Now, he describes the colors of the eclipsed disk:

> ->At the beginning and end of the eclipse, the Moon is smoky; in a partial eclipse, it is black.<-
> ->In a total eclipse, it is tawny; when it enters the middle of the shadow, it is dark copper-red. || 46 ||<-

At the "beginning" <note>pragrahana</note> and at the "end" <note>ante</note> or conclusion of the eclipse, the Moon appears smoky. In a "partial eclipse," when the disk is mostly seized, it is black in color. When "total obscuration" <note>sarva-grasa</note> or full contact occurs, it is tawny. Even in a total eclipse, as it enters the "middle of the darkness" <note>the center of the Earth's shadow</note>, the Moon becomes a dark copper-red color. The Moon—

<meta>catchword: The Moon—</meta>

<summary>This page explains the calculation of 'valana' (deflection) used to orient an eclipse on the celestial sphere and describes the shifting colors of the Moon—from smoky to dark copper—as it passes through the Earth's shadow.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhatiya, eclipse colors, deflection, celestial latitude, declination, lunar eclipse, solar eclipse, tawny, copper-red</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of solar eclipses and the verification of planetary positions from the previous section of the Golapāda (Section on the Celestial Sphere).</meta>

# Section on the Celestial Sphere

It is stated in the *Prakāśikā* <note>A well-known commentary on the Aryabhatiya</note> that this is also the color of the Sun. Now, he describes the invisible portion during a solar eclipse:

> When the circumferences of the Sun and Moon meet, one-eighth of the Sun remains invisible and cannot be predicted, <gloss>A-11.9</gloss>
> because of the Sun’s intense brightness and the transparency of the Moon’s edge. || 37 ||

At the beginning of the contact between the circumferences of the Sun and Moon, even if one-eighth of the solar disk is eclipsed, it is "unpredictable"—meaning it is impossible to perceive with the eye. He provides the reason for this: "because of the Sun." Due to the extreme brilliance of the Sun and the transparency of the water-filled Moon’s edge. <note>Ancient Indian astronomy often characterized the Moon as having a watery or reflective nature</note>. The transparency of the Moon's edge occurs because of its proximity to the solar rays. However, when the eclipsed portion exceeds one-eighth, the grasped part becomes visible along with that eighth portion.

Thus, he explains the accuracy of the planetary motions taught in his own treatise by demonstrating their agreement with observation:

> The Sun is determined from its conjunction with the Earth <note>referring to the horizon/sunrise</note>; the Moon is determined from its conjunction with the Sun;
> and all the star-planets <note>Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn</note> are determined from their conjunctions with the Moon and the stars. || 38 ||

In this system, the Sun is established through its "conjunction with the Earth"—it is considered "true" or "accurate" based on its rising. For instance, the Sun's position on the celestial sphere is determined by its rising and setting on the east-west line. Furthermore, the Sun's instantaneous position is calculated using the shadow of a gnomon <term>Shanku: a vertical pillar used to measure time and solar altitude</term> on the east-west line and the north-south line, which also accounts for the cessation of north-south movement <note>the solstices</note> and the movement of the equinoxes. In this way, through many methods of observation, the Sun mentioned here is considered "true." This is what is stated in the *Prakāśikā*. By these various methods, only the Sun with precession <term>Sayanarka: the longitude of the sun adjusted for the precession of the equinoxes</term> is established, not just a theoretical calculation. Since the shifting of the equinoxes varies over time, the precise knowledge of it comes from the mathematically derived Sun. *) He now states the source of the science:

---

*) Following this, several statements by scholars are found in two of the manuscripts. For example: "Therefore, some say this. Regarding the stars beginning with Krittika <note>the Pleiades</note>..."

<summary>This page explains why the initial phase of a solar eclipse is invisible to the eye and details the observational methods used to verify the true positions of the Sun, Moon, and planets.</summary>
<keywords>solar eclipse, visibility threshold, gnomon shadow, precession of equinoxes, planetary conjunctions, Aryabhatiya, Golapada</keywords>

<page-num>100</page-num>
# The Section on the Sphere in the Aryabhatiya

->By the grace of God, I have rescued from the ocean of true and false knowledge<-
->the best jewel of true knowledge, which lay submerged, using the boat of my own intellect. (49)<-

From the ocean known as the <term>Jyotishashastra</term> <note>The science of astronomy and mathematics</note>, which contains jewels of both true and false knowledge, I have entered into its depths by boarding the boat of my own intellect. There, by the grace of the Self-Born Creator <note>Brahma</note>, I have properly rescued this excellent jewel called "True Knowledge." The meaning is that I have simply brought to light the truths originally taught by the Self-Born One. This confirms the title given to the work. Now, he concludes:

->This work, named Aryabhatiya, was formerly known as the Svayambhuva; it is eternally true.<-
->Whoever finds fault with this work destroys the merit of his good deeds and his very life. (50)<- <note>The original text reads "pratikanchuka," meaning one who acts as a "cover" or opponent to the truth.</note>

The astronomical science which was extracted from the Vedas and revealed to the world in ancient times, and which is eternally true—that very same essence has been revealed by me under the name *Aryabhatiya*. Whoever acts as an opponent to this science—meaning one who attempts to disparage it by inventing faults—will suffer the destruction of his religious merit and his lifespan.

->This <term>Bhatadipika</term> <note>Light on the Aryabhatiya; a famous 15th-century commentary</note> was composed by the one named Parameshvara.<-
->May it forever illuminate the temple of the hearts of those who know the science of astronomy.<-

->Thus ends the Section on the Sphere in the Light on the Aryabhatiya.<-

->Thus the Aryabhatiya is completed.<-

---

...having correctly determined the rising sign, the culminating sign, and the setting sign of those [stars] through the methods taught in the scriptures, and having set up the <term>Ghatika-yantra</term> <note>An outflow water clock used for measuring time</note> at the moment of sunset, by that time [one should observe] the Pleiades and others... by which time the Brahmin...
<note>The text here appears to be a fragmentary instruction on observational practice.</note>

<margin>The reading "pratikanchuko yo 'sya" should be followed. The commentary titled "Dipika" is sometimes noted for grammatical irregularities.</margin>

<summary>This concluding page of the Aryabhatiya features Aryabhata’s final verses claiming his work as a restoration of Brahma's original revelation and a warning against detractors, followed by the commentator Parameshvara’s closing prayer.</summary>
<keywords>Aryabhata, Parameshvara, Bhatadipika, Jyotishashastra, water clock, astronomical observation, Brahma, Vedic science</keywords>
